#3793. 5 Best Walter Koenig TV Shows That Aren't Star Trek, Ranked

Walter Koenig has sadly passed away at 90 years old. He was best known for playing the role of Pavel Chekov on "Star Trek: The Original Series" and was brought in during the show's second season (a move, Koenig once said, that was done to appeal to a younger audience). Chekov was a Russian character seen working alongside the USS Enterprise's main crew, and he was cute, a little cocksure, and claimed that Russians invented Western Civilization. Although rarely highlighted on "Star Trek," he was an invaluable part of the series and appeared in several "Star Trek" feature films.

But Koenig was more than "Star Trek." He began accruing credits in the early 1960s, turning up in episodes of the day's hottest TV shows. Koenig started off by scoring a small role on the revolutionary soap opera "General Hospital," and from there, it was off to the races. He soon appeared on shows like "The Alfred Hitchcock Hour" and "Jericho," leading to his time on "Star Trek." Koenig continued to work from there, acting in various projects and taking on numerous voice roles for practically the rest of his days.

Many may not be familiar with Koenig's non-"Trek" career, so we're here to offer some guidance. The following is a list of notable, fun, funny roles that Koenig tackled on TV, including his most famous part outside of the "Star Trek" franchise.

Starting in 1986, young fans of Ivan Reitman's 1984 blockbuster "Ghostbusters" were treated to a creative and exciting animated spin-off TV show called "The Real Ghostbusters." The animated series re-envisioned the four leads from Reitman's film with new designs but very much the same character traits, and it sported some amazing voice performances. Lorenzo Music initially played Peter, Maurice LaMarche played Egon, Frank Welker played Ray, and Arsenio Hall played Winston after Ernie Hudson was rejected from reprising the role (for a very silly reason). Because of its episodic structure, "The Real Ghostbusters" had the leeway to introduce a wide menagerie of ghosts, ghouls, and demons, and it would even occasionally stray into the Lovecraftian.

Case in point: Vladimir Pavel Maximov, the character played by Walter Koenig in the episode "Russian About." Maximov was a scientist who had been laughed out of the scientific community for his zany theories. (The Titanic sank, he said, because it crashed into Elvis.) In the episode, Maximov comes to possess a Necronomicon-like grimoire called the Nameless Book, which has access to incantations that can summon the Old Ones. The Old Ones, as fans of H.P. Lovecraft can tell you, are a gaggle of evil gods that have been biding their time before reclaiming the Earth.

Maximov uses the Nameless Book to summon a god called Yibb-Tstll, much to the annoyance of the Ghostbusters. It's fun to hear Koenig play a goofy cartoon villain like this. Koenig was a talented voice actor, and folks loved his Russian accent. (Koenig, notably, was of Russian descent, although he was born in Chicago.)

The one-season sci-fi TV series "The Starlost" has a great premise. The series is set on board a 200-mile-long starship called Earthship Ark, outfitted with dozens of enormous biospheres, merrily making its way through the cosmos and hoping to relocate humanity to a new world. A century into the voyage, an accident on the ship seals off all the biospheres from the others, and everyone on board has to fend for themselves. The action of "The Starlost" takes place 405 years after said accident, in the year 2790. Keir Dullea plays Devon, an agrarian farmer who manages to flee his biome and discover the now-forgotten mission of Earthship Ark. Devon and a few of his friends spend the series exploring the ship. William Ostler plays the computer who instructs them.

Walter Koenig appeared in two episodes of "The Starlost" as a humanoid alien named Oro, hailing from the planet Exar. A year prior, Oro's ship crashed into the Ark, and he managed to get on board. He has since been tearing apart the Ark, looking for the components he needs to rebuild his ship and employing the aid of Idona (Alexandra Bastedo), who also fled one of the ship's biospheres.

Koenig plays Oro as a cold, calculating character, an opportunist with plans of his own. The character also returns a few episodes later, promising Devon and the crew a place to settle (naturally, he's lying for his own purposes). Koenig's presence on "The Starlost" was like getting a blessing from the sci-fi head honchos.

"The Starlost" was created by the infamously grumpy and undeniably great writer Harlan Ellison. It may be obscure these days, but it's worth looking into. I love the premise.

Gen-Xers like me remember Stretch Armstrong as a mere toy, a smiling superhero figure with goo-filled rubber limbs that could be stretched across a room. But I'll wager that not many of us were aware that in 2017, Netflix adapted Stretch Armstrong into an animated series called "Stretch Armstrong and the Flex Fighters." The new series was about a trio of teens who were accidentally exposed to a miracle chemical called Flexarium that turned them into stretchy, rubbery superheroes. They become Stretch (Scott Menvile), Wingspan (Steven Yuen), and Omni-Mass (Ogie Banks).

"Stretch Armstrong" also attracted a few "Star Trek" alumni. Kate Mulgrew from "Star Trek: Voyager" played a seeming antagonist in the first season of the series, before becoming a mentor figure in the second. Wil Wheaton from "Star Trek: The Next Generation" played Jonathan Rook, the benefactor of the Flex Fighters, but also secretly a were-monster. Keith David, Henry Rollins, Clancy Brown, and many other notable actors had small roles.

In a few episodes, Walter Koenig played a character named Mr. Savic, a mad scientist who created an intelligent plant creature called Anastasia (Tara Strong). He was a biology teacher who, in the show's second season, learns how to turn into a massive superhero monster called Biomass. Koenig brought a certain degree of energy and humanity to the role. He was a nerd with genuine enthusiasm. Audiences also see that Mr. Savic is a lonely man who only wanted to create a family for himself. It's actually a pretty great role.

In the "Futurama" episode "Where No Fan Has Gone Before," Fry (Billy West) learns that, in the 31st century, it is illegal to say the phrase "Star Trek." In the centuries since the present, Trekkies became religious zealots who started wars. Thereafter, Earth took precautions against future conflagrations by ridding the planet of all episodes of the original "Star Trek" series. The cast, reduced to living heads in jars, fled the planet.

Armed with Leonard Nimoy's head, Fry goes looking for the rest of the cast and finds that William Shatner, Walter Koenig, George Takei, DeForest Kelley, and Nichelle Nichols have been resurrected on a distant planet by a weird energy being named Melllvar (Maurice LaMarche), who has fallen in love with "Star Trek." Melllvar's "action figure" collection is complete when Fry brings Nimoy along. James (James Doohan was replaced by a new character named Welshie.)

The entire cast of "Star Trek" has a great sense of humor about their place in popular culture, and they are all game to make fun of themselves a little bit (Doohan notwithstanding). At one point, Fry points out that everyone can achieve their goals in life, pointing out that after "Star Trek," Walter Koenig became an actor.

That's a bit of an insult, but Koenig expands on the joke by saying that he became an actor, but also a person with friends and keys. The plot of the "Futurama" episode involves the cast of "Star Trek" fighting the cast of "Futurama" in hand-to-hand combat, and Koenig points out that he has no experience actually fighting, "except with you guys," he says to the "Star Trek" cast.

Koenig had a sense of humor, and that's important to remember. Shatner, meanwhile, called Fry voice actor Billy West to make sure the episode was on the up and up.

One of Walter Koenig's best roles was Alfred Bester in 12 episodes of "Babylon 5." Bester was a member of the Psi Corps, a cadre of human psychics that are, in the lore of the series, required to become cops or else be injected with psychic-dampening drugs. Because there are no checks to the organization's power, they hastily became somewhat fascistic. Fans of "Babylon 5" might be able to tell you about the show's storied Telepath War.

One of the show's wickedest Psi Cops was Bester, a war criminal who passed through Babylon 5 occasionally. Bester was first introduced investigating another rogue psychic, and presented as a cool, calming, insidious presence that was clearly using his low energy as a manipulation tool. While many admire Koenig for his energetic "Star Trek" performances, his best acting came from "Babylon 5." Bester was an arrogant figure, able to diffuse any threats made in his direction. There is a scene wherein Babylon 5's security chief Garibaldi (Jerry Doyle) says that he'd like to beat Bester like a piñata, and Bester takes it as a compliment: "So, you think of me as something bright and cheerful," he says, "full of toys and candy for young children? Thank you. That makes me feel much better about our relationship."

Unlike Chekov, Bester drove the stories on "Babylon 5," and his character had a grand arc and was given a great deal of background. If anyone only ever saw Koenig as a supporting player or a side character, know that he was capable of evil, pathos, and rich, intriguing characters. Bester might be the actor's best role, period.

#3792. Coca-Cola Organic Revenue Jumps 7% Despite Flat North America Volumes. Here’s What Investors Need to Know.

There was a lot to like about Coca-Cola (KO -1.64%) and its third-quarter earnings report on Tuesday. The company grew its global revenue by 7%, and unit case volume was up 5%, with strong demand in Latin America, Asia Pacific, and its Europe, Middle East, and Africa segment.

But closer to home, there's a problem. North American sales volume was flat year over year, making the domestic market Coca-Cola's weakest geographic operating segment. And that weighed on Coca-Cola stock, with shares down 1% in early afternoon trading.

Now investors need to consider how long Coca-Cola can rely on international markets to drive its growth, and what it needs to do to spur sales in North America.

Revenue in the third quarter was $13.4 billion, up 7% from a year ago. Organic revenue, which excludes the effects of acquisitions, divestitures, and currency fluctuations, also increased 7%.

Adjusted earnings per share reached $0.91, an 11% improvement from the previous year.

But the most encouraging number may be the increase in beverage volumes. Coca-Cola's 5% global volume growth demonstrates that consumers are buying more drinks, rather than the company relying entirely on higher prices to generate additional revenue.

And some of its most recognizable products are doing particularly well. Volume for Coca-Cola Zero Sugar jumped 16%, while the flagship Coca-Cola brand grew 5%. Diet Coke and Coca-Cola Light increased 6%. Water volume climbed 8%, while sports drink volume declined 3%, and coffee and tea volumes were unchanged.

International markets provided much of the momentum. Latin America and the Asia Pacific each recorded 6% volume growth, while Europe, the Middle East, and Africa recorded 5% volume growth. Latin America's organic revenue surged 16%, helped by higher prices and a favorable product mix.

Those are encouraging results for a company that has spent decades building a distribution network capable of reaching consumers around the world.

Coca-Cola's home market told a different story. North American revenue increased just 3%, while unit case volume was unchanged from a year ago. That's a noticeable slowdown from the second quarter, when North American volume grew 3%.

The weakness wasn't necessarily in Coca-Cola itself. The company reported higher volumes for its trademark Coca-Cola products, but those gains were offset by declines in water, sports drinks, coffee, and tea. So, consumers haven't suddenly stopped drinking Coke. But some of the company's other beverage categories are struggling to generate growth.

"Diet Coke and Coca-Cola Zero, especially Coca-Cola Zero, growing double digit again, and fairlife continues to do really well," CEO Henrique Braun told analysts during the company's earnings call. "Powerade also grew, and we have other areas that we have work to do, which is always good for us also to instill here our motto of being constructive with discontent that there are areas for us to work on."

Despite the North American slowdown, Coca-Cola's financial performance gives investors plenty of reasons to be encouraged. The company's operating margin expanded to 33.4% from 32% a year earlier. Its comparable operating margin also improved to 32.9%, up from 31.9% last year.

And Coca-Cola continues to generate gobs of cash. Free cash flow reached $11.4 billion through the first nine months of 2026. Management now expects at least $13 billion for the full year, up from its previous forecast of approximately $12.4 billion.

The company also raised its outlook for adjusted earnings growth to at least 11%, compared with its previous projection of 9% to 10%.

For long-term investors, those results matter more than one disappointing regional volume figure. Coca-Cola is increasing sales, improving profitability, and generating the cash needed to support its dividend and invest in future growth.

But North America deserves attention. International markets can keep Coca-Cola growing, even when its home market struggles. Getting domestic consumers to buy more of its beverages would provide the company with another source of growth -- and make an already strong business even stronger.

#3791. This Versatile Roborock Vacuum and Mop Is $750 Off Right Now

Pradershika Sharma is a tech deals writer for Lifehacker. She has a Master’s degree in English Literature, a B.Ed., and a TESOL certification. She has been writing professionally since 2018, creating product reviews, affiliate articles, and search ads for global clients while working with Rubix Agency and Cognizant. Previously, she spent a year teaching English at the junior high level.An avid reader since childhood, Pradershika's idea of extreme sports is staying up to read “just one more chapter.” She lives in India.

Homes with shedding pets, mixed flooring, and low furniture can quickly reveal the shortcomings of a basic robot vacuum. The Roborock Qrevo CurvX is built to handle those challenges, and it is currently available on Amazon for $749.99, down from $1,499.99. That is a $750 discount, and price trackers also indicate it is the lowest price recorded for this model. And while $750 is still a considerable amount to spend on a cleaning appliance, the discount brings it closer to midrange model pricing while retaining features normally associated with flagship machines.

The CurvX earns that higher price by doing more than simply vacuuming an open stretch of floor—its 22,000 pascals of suction can handle pet hair, crumbs, and debris caught in carpet, while the DuoDivide main brush guides hair toward the center to reduce tangles. There’s also an extendable side brush that reaches along walls and into corners, and a lifting chassis that helps the robot cross rugs, exercise mats, and other raised surfaces without getting stuck. Its mop pads are similarly adaptable, extending toward room edges for better coverage and lifting by up to 17 millimeters over carpet so the CurvX can move between hard floors and rugs without dragging a wet mop across both.

When it encounters low furniture, its top-mounted LiDAR sensor retracts to reduce the robot’s height to 3.14 inches, allowing it to clean beneath most sofas and beds. And once the CurvX finishes cleaning, its docking station takes care of much of the work that would otherwise fall to you—it empties the dustbin, refills the water tank, washes the mop pads with water heated to 176 degrees Fahrenheit, and dries them with air heated to 113 degrees. It also has a dirt sensor that can prompt it to wash its mop pads again or repeat a cleaning pass when an area needs more attention. The Roborock app gives you further control, allowing you to clean specific rooms, create schedules, set virtual barriers, and mark areas the robot should avoid, while SmartPlan 2.0 adapts the routine to different rooms and floor types.

#3790. Christina Applegate Feels Part of MS Community Despite Misdiagnosis

After Christina Applegate shared doctors confirmed she was misdiagnosed with multiple sclerosis, the Married…With Children actress addressed those who do suffer from the autoimmune disorder.

Christina Applegate might not be struggling with MS, but she stands with the community.

Shortly after the Married…With Children actress revealed she had been misdiagnosed her with multiple sclerosis five years ago, she shared a message for those who live with the autoimmune disorder.

“You guys are my people,” Applegate said during the Oct. 27 episode of her and Jamie Lynn Sigler’s MeSsy podcast. “Even though I don’t have this disease, I have fallen in love with everyone that we know—listeners and our friends—that have MS and I’m still your warrior.”

Indeed, the 54-year-old—mom to Sadie, 15, with husband Martyn LeNoble—emphasized she’ll still advocate for those struggling with MS.

“I’m still going to speak out on your behalf for ever and ever,” she assured. “I still feel like part of this community. No matter what, I love you.”

Earlier this month, Applegate detailed learning recently that she had been misdiagnosed with MS by doctors in 2021, expressing frustration that she was “back at square one” on her health journey.

“I was, like, not happy at all,” the Dead to Me star shared of what went through her head upon receiving the news during an Oct. 20 episode of the MeSsy podcast. “Everybody else was so happy.”

Although she does not have MS, she is still grappling with debilitating health struggles.

“I don't know what's going on with me,” Applegate said. “That's going to be an online investigation that my doctors are going to be getting into just to find out what has been going on since 2021.”

And while she has at least received one answer, her misdiagnosis has made her doubt her own symptoms.

“I'm sitting here having slept all day yesterday feeling like absolute crap because of the heat,” she continued. "My legs aren't working. All the same stuff is happening to me. And yet I have this thing of like, 'Well, you don't have that, so maybe you're faking it' in my head.”

Meanwhile, Applegate admitted that she feels like she “let everybody down” with her misdiagnosis.

“It's this weird feeling because I became this, you and I: the poster children for MS,” she told her cohost Sigler. “Everything I was doing, I had a reason. I had a f--king reason as to why my day sucked every day. And now I don't have a reason. It's very unnerving, and it's very confusing for me.”

For a deeper look into Applegate’s health journey, keep reading…

Christina Applegate shared she was diagnosed with multiple sclerosis in 2021.

"Hi friends. A few months ago I was diagnosed with MS," she shared on her social media channels that August. "It's been a strange journey. But I have been so supported by people that I know who also have this condition. It's been a tough road. But as we all know, the road keeps going. Unless some a--hole blocks it."

"Oh, by the way, I have a disease," she joked during her November 2022 Hollywood Walk of Fame induction ceremony, her first public appearance since her MS diagnosis. "Did you not notice? I'm not even wearing shoes."

Applegate believes her MS journey actually began "six or seven years" before her 2021 diagnosis.

"I noticed, especially the first season [of Dead to Me], we'd be shooting and my leg would buckle," Applegate explained during a March 2024 interview on Good Morning America. "I really just put it off as being tired, or I'm dehydrated, or it's the weather. Then nothing would happen for months, and I didn't pay attention."

By the time she was shooting the Netflix series' third and final season, the actress said she was "being brought to set in a wheelchair."

"I couldn't move that far," she recalled, "so I had to tell everybody because I needed help."

The Dead to Me star captioned this photo of her cane collection amid her battle with MS: "Walking sticks are now part of my new normal."

Joined by her daughter Sadie Grace LeNoble, Applegate had a simple message for multiple sclerosis while attending the 2023 SAG Awards: "F U MS."

And she's not the only one experiencing strong feelings towards the illness. "In my situation, Sadie only knew me as healthy and a runner and a Pelotoner and a dancer—and she only knew that," Applegate explained to friend Jamie-Lynn Sigler on an August 2025 episode of their MeSsy podcast. "So then when this came about in 2021, she was, like, stoic about it."

Yet, "I see her look at me when I’m in bed," Applegate continued, "and can't quite move or, I want to go say goodnight to her in her room but I can’t quite get down the hallway for whatever reason my legs aren’t working that day."

The whole experience has "broken" her teen, Applegate noted. "It was like losing the mom she had to this f--king thing. And the more she’s gotten older now, I think the more it’s hurting her."

After a receiving standing ovation at the 2023 Emmys, the Married...With Children alum quipped, "You're totally shaming me with disability by standing up."

"I make these jokes because if I don't, I'll suffocate," Applegate shared on a March 2024 episode on Armchair Expert, explaining why she often pokes fun at her condition. "I'll be done."

So, yes, she's going to talk about the fact that she's already purchased burial plots.

"My friend and I are going to go take a picnic there," Applegate revealed on a March 2026 episode of NPR's Wild Card. "It's really pretty where it is." She just has to pick out a tree "'cause it's really sunny," she continued. "And I want my visitors to not be sweating."

"I have 30 lesions on my brain," she noted on Armchair Expert. "My biggest one is behind my right eye, so my right eye hurts a lot."

Legions are caused by the immune system attacking the myelin sheath around nerves, according to the Multiple Sclerosis Trust.

Applegate hasn’t held back when it comes to sharing her journey.

"It sucks," the Anchorman actress told late night talk show host Jimmy Kimmel of her daily struggles with MS. "I'm not going to lie. I think anyone who has MS isn't going to be like, 'This is the best thing that ever happened to me!'"

Besides, she'd taken that tack when discussing her breast cancer battle back in 2008. "I went out, and I was the good girl talking about 'Oh, I love my new boobs' that are all scarred and f--ked up. What was I thinking?" she said on Armchair Expert. "Everything I was saying was a freaking lie. It was me trying to convince myself of something, and I think that did no service to anyone."

While she started a foundation and "raised millions of dollars for women to get MRIs who were at high risk," she noted, "at the back of it, I was taking off my bra and crying every night. And I wish that I had said that."

When talking about her day-to-day life with the disease, Applegate told Good Morning America in March 2024, "I live kind of in hell."

"But I might get to a place where I function a little bit better," she added. "Right now, I'm isolating, and that's kind of how I'm dealing with it—by not going anywhere because I don't want to do it. It's hard."

Christina credited her Sweetest Thing costar Selma Blair—who was diagnosed with MS in 2018—for urging her to get tested for the disease.

"She said, 'You need to get checked for MS,'" Christina recalled during her GMA interview. "If not for her, it could've been way worse."

Applegate confirmed that Dead to Me will likely be her last onscreen credit, telling Vanity Fair in May 2023, "I can’t even imagine going to set right now."

"I’m probably not going to work on-camera again, but I'm so glad that I went out with someone who is by far the greatest actress I’ve ever worked with in my entire life," she said of costar Linda Cardellini, "if not the greatest human I’ve ever known.

For Applegate, contracting sapovirus—a virus commonly transmitted through fecal-oral contact—meant having to rely on adult diapers.

"I woke up at three o'clock in the morning in a pool of s--t," she explained on an April 2024 episode of her MeSsy podcast. "I didn't know what happened, and having MS at three o'clock in the morning and trying to change your sheets, it's not fun. But this brings me to my next point, which we can talk about: I'm wearing diapers."

Their discussion was "so liberating," her co-host Sigler noted. "This is something you do by yourself and it feels terrible when you're doing it. I've done that hundreds of times in my MS journey through the years when I've experienced a flair or when I was sick."

"I am in a depression right now, which I don't think I've felt for years," Applegate confessed to Sigler on another 2024 episode of their MeSsy podcast. "Like a real, f--k it all, like real depression where it's kind of scaring me too a little bit because it feels really fatalistic."

Three years into her diagnosis, she continued, "And I'm still sitting here like ‘Boohoo, woe is me.' I'm still mad about it." But the truth was, added, "I don't enjoy living—I don't enjoy things anymore."

Applegate acknowledged the reality of living with MS can be grim. “Everybody has different ways of it showing up,” she noted on a November 2024 episode of her podcast. "I lay in bed screaming. Like, the sharp pains, the ache, the squeezing."

At times, the discomfort even makes it hard for her to enjoy her one source of comfort, the reality TV that at times is "on 24/7 in my room," she noted. But, "I can’t even pick up my phone sometimes," Applegate added, "because now it’s traveled into my hands. So I’ll try to go get my phone or get my remote to turn on the TV or whatever, and sometimes I can’t even hold them."

Since her 2021 diagnosis, Applegate has gotten used to the feel of an open-front gown. "I've been in the hospital upwards of 30 times from throwing up and diarrhea and pain," she relayed on her pocast in March 2025. "That is unimaginable, OK? They’ve done every test known to man on me, put so much radiation into my body from CT scans to everything else."

In the plus column, she noted, she has connected some of her gastro issues to her larger illness. "I have noticed that—and I’m going to be really honest—if I have to poop, I puke," she detailed. "And when I puke, I get all the pain and then all the things happen."

Mere months after her hospital confession, Applegate was checking back in. Returning from a family trip to Europe, "I'm like, 'I wanna be admitted. I'm staying here because I want answers,'" she recounted that August of what she told staff. "'I want every test that you can possibly think of or ones that you haven't even thought of, and I want them done.'"

Following a middle-of-the-night CT, she learned, "I had kidney infections."

More than five years after sharing her battle with multiple sclerosis, she learned that she was misdiagnosed.

"She came into my room and said, 'Congratulations, you don't have MS,'" she recalled of her doctor's visit in the October 2026 podcast MeSsy. "I was, like, not happy at all. Everybody else was so happy."

Applegate emphasized that the confirmation of the misdiagnosis hadn't changed how she's continued to struggle physically.

"I haven't gotten better," she said. "If anything, I've gotten worse. So I'm back at square one where I don't know what's going on with me. That's going to be an online investigation that my doctors are going to be getting into just to find out what has been going on since 2021."

#3789. Midterms 2026: Jon Ossoff’s TikTok Campaign, Ken Paxton’s Texas Tour, and More Political Powermaxxing

In just nine days, we’ll learn if the next mayor of Los Angeles will be a Democrat or a democratic socialist. The Iron Lady of Maine, Susan Collins, will prove once more that she cannot be beaten—or, in defeat, that some waves crush everybody. We’ll see if massive sums of tech-oligarch cash can sway the future, and just how much can one get away with as a Republican in Texas with Donald Trump’s endorsement. (Ken Paxton intends to find out.) Georgia senator Jon Ossoff, meanwhile, will seek to prove that the strength of his short-form content can power him to reelection—and perhaps, though he denies any such ambitions, to the presidency in 2028.

Today, Vanity Fair launches our midterm spectacular, a series of eight stories across four days—plus a couple fun extras—that take stock of our national moment and where we might go next. Throughout this week, we’ll be popping around the country to examine the hope and mire of the campaign trail and report it all back to you.

All the while, the president will make his own mad dash around the nation to rally Republicans for the upcoming midterm elections. So far, he’s been content to play the hits. You’d hardly know that he’s grappling with an intractable war in the Middle East and sky-high energy prices, or that he’s staring down an endless stream of congressional investigations as his party braces for what could be a historic wipeout in November. On the stump, he’s buoyant. And increasingly candid.

“I looked at his ass,” Trump said of Senate candidate Jon Husted last week, “and then I looked at his legs. I said, ‘His ass, shit, it’s like—it’s like a pile of muscle.’” The crowd went wild. This was the eighth time in just over a week that Trump marveled at the appearance of another man. Maybe he’s experiencing an outbreak of geriatric candor. “It’s amazing,” Trump said at another rally earlier this month. “When you stand up here, it’s just like, the love. I could say the stupidest things and you’d clap.”

Outside his rallies, across the country, the mood is less permissive. A Reuters/Ipsos poll released this week put Trump’s approval at a record low: 31%. Sixty-six percent disapprove. For the first time in more than a decade, another poll found, Americans trust Democrats more than Republicans on the economy. Things are getting so gnarly that conservative radio host Erick Erickson has taken to comparing Trump to Jimmy Carter.

This has all but ensured that Democrats will retake the House in November, but the Senate rests on a knife’s edge. Democrats really shouldn’t stand a chance of winning the upper chamber: The party is deeply unpopular to begin with, and doing so would require flipping at least two states Trump won by double digits just two years ago. But the collapse of the president’s approval has turned even some ruby-red states into battlegrounds.

This is the primordial soup that this election cycle is swimming in, the matrix in which Vanity Fair’s midterm coverage will live over the next two weeks. We launch today with examinations of Ossoff, the short-form candidate; the Maine campaign of Susan Collins; and the LA mayoral candidate Nithya Raman. This week we’ll follow up with a dispatch from Paxton’s campaign bus as the scandal-plagued attorney general works to keep Texas red.

Paxton’s challenger, James Talarico, is among a fresh crop of young Democrats who have mastered the new media climate that allowed for Trump’s rise in the first place, using viral short-form video as a means of catapulting their campaigns to national attention and building massive war chests in the process. Ossoff has pulled in $110 million this election cycle thanks in part to this very modern media strategy. And Michigan Senate candidate Abdul El-Sayed—the subject of our upcoming video documentary, which follows the brash Democrat on a trip to the state’s Upper Peninsula—is one of quite a few progressive populists looking to prove they can ride social media stardom to victory in a swing state.

Increasingly, these younger candidates are backed by online personalities with rising power, digital commissars made for our logged-on era. As you’ll read in our dispatch from the online fever swamps, a lefty streamer like the hugely influential Hasan Piker suddenly has no corollary on the other side, as the dark and dingy precincts of the far right, led by voices like Nick Fuentes, have devolved into a kind of anti-electoral, nihilistic self-absorption that might just prove these nihilists are the true inheritors of Trump’s political legacy. Though even the president, for a time, cared about getting Republicans elected.

This election will ultimately serve, like every midterm before it, as a referendum on the man in the White House. Should Democrats win control of the House of Representatives, the next two years of Trump’s administration will look very different from the first two, when he operated with unprecedented power and impunity. November 3 will undoubtedly mark a turning point not just for this new flock of Democrats hoping to descend on Washington in January, but also for a White House that has sought to alter the fabric of the country and its relationship with the world.

#3788. This Company Is Paying You To Play GTA 6, But It’s A Long Shot

A company, Virgin Media, has announced a promotional campaign where one lucky person will get paid to play GTA 6.

The promotion, which is limited to Ireland, requires people to explain in 100-200 words on this contest site why they deserve to get paid to play GTA 6. Virgin Media will then select a winner, and if that's you, Virgin Media will get you a copy of the game and your only requirement is to play it for eight hours and post a review on Instagram or TikTok, tagging Virgin Media and disclosing that it's a paid ad.

Virgin Media is paying €100/hour ($111 USD), so that works out to €800 ($891 USD) for the full session.

"We will buy the game, and let you split those eight hours across the week however suits," Virgin Media said.

Applications close on November 15, and Virgin Media will pick the winner by random and contact them within seven days.

You can visit the contest site to learn more, and see the full terms and conditions here. One thing to note is that Virgin Media will pay for the game's $100 Ultimate Edition, but you need to have a PS5 or Xbox Series X|S to play it on.

GTA 6 releases on November 19 for PS5 and Xbox Series X|S. The game's massive file size has been revealed.

#3786. Celebrity Pregnancy Announcements of 2026: Savannah Chrisley and More

Many stars are expanding their families in 2026.

In the first half of the year, actresses Bonnie Wright, Aubrey Plaza, Natalie Portman and Anne Hathaway all revealed that they are pregnant. From across the pond, Princess Eugenie also shared the news that she was expecting her third baby with husband Jack Brooksbank.

The second half of the year kicked off with Counting On alum Joy-Anna Duggar announcing in September her pregnancy with baby No. 4 while Ciara and Russell Wilson also revealed they are expecting their fourth baby together, her fifth. The next month, Tyler Cameron and fiancée  Tate Madden revealed they are expecting their first baby, as is Southern Charm alum Naomie Olindo. Savannah Chrisley also announced that she is expecting her first baby.

Keep scrolling to see which stars are expecting babies in 2026:

#3785. How The Red Rising Movie Will Differ From Hunger Games And The Long Walk

Francis Lawrence might not be a director whose name you know offhand, but you've definitely seen his work. Not only has Lawrence helmed projects like "I Am Legend" and "Water for Elephants," but aside from the first film in the Hunger Games franchise, which was directed by Gary Ross, Lawrence has directed every single film in the franchise, including the first prequel "The Hunger Games: The Ballad of Songbirds and Snakes" and the forthcoming Haymitch Abernathy origin story "The Hunger Games: Sunrise on the Reaping." (That character was originated by Woody Harrelson in the Hunger Games trilogy, and is played by Joseph Zada in the prequel.) I recently got the chance to speak to Lawrence about his recent string of dystopian movies focused on teenagers living under fascism, and he talked about his work in the Hunger Games universe, his excellent 2025 Stephen King adaptation "The Long Walk," and the fact that he's set to adapt Pierce Brown's novel series "Red Rising" into a movie.

Lawrence was thoughtful and careful while expressing that "Red Rising" does differ from the other two properties in one important way: the protagonist, Darrow, must penetrate a higher class to discover the truth. As he told me:

"Red Rising, on the other hand, even though there's similar elements [to Hunger Games and 'The Long Walk'], to me, is an amazing infiltration story. So I think that brings its own set of parameters and moral choice dilemmas when you are sneaking into a society that you're not supposed to be a part of, and what happens when you start to like the enemy and you're not supposed to like the enemy and the choices that you make. So again, they have similar things, but for me, they're completely different stories."

As Francis Lawrence told me while we discussed the Hunger Games film franchise, "The Long Walk," and a future potential Red Rising franchise, even though he can see the similarities — "in competition, youth, high stakes, obviously all those kinds of things" — he sees the stories "completely differently."  "The Long Walk," as Lawrence tells it, is about the relationship between Ray and Pete, the characters played by Cooper Hoffman and David Jonsson. Lawrence continued:

"I fell in love with the relationship between [Ray and Pete] and the rest of the boys and how they became like a brotherhood and fell in love with one another in a way where you'd expect there to be more competition. And so I found a real beauty in the relationships. So for me, it's less about the games and more about the relationships and what that means and what the long walk means in terms of a metaphor for existence."

So what about the world of the Hunger Games, where teenagers are forced into an arena every single year so they can fight to the death until one victor emerges, all for the amusement of the all-powerful Capitol of the dystopian state of Panem? Lawrence is spot-on once again. "And Hunger Games are very different in that they tend to be, book to book, about very different kinds of themes," he told me. "['Sunrise on the Reaping'] is about implicit submission and this idea of not being apathetic and finding your voice and standing up and fighting for what you believe in, even if it's at great cost. So that's very specific."

When I initially asked Francis Lawrence about the narrative overlaps between many of his recent projects, I also asked if he's comfortable with a large section of his career being tied to fascism, dystopias, and the oppression of young people. Not only did Lawrence say that he was, but he also revealed something fascinating about his preferences as a filmmaker:

"I am. And what's weird is that I think the dystopian aspect of these worlds, a lot of it, for me, is about the world-building. I like the visual aspect of filmmaking, so world-building is a very, very important part of what I love to do. And so if somebody were to hand me a contemporary heist movie, I wouldn't do it. I just have no interest in just going out in the streets and shooting some kind of — I just feel like there's not enough for me to do. So I'd say that's part of it."

This is reminiscent of the innovative horror director Robert Eggers telling Rotten Tomatoes in 2025 that "the idea of having to photograph a car makes me ill, and the idea of photographing a cellphone is just death," putting Lawrence in excellent company here. Throughout his work on projects like the Hunger Games films and "The Long Walk," Lawrence has handily proven his skill for telling a specific type of story, but his attention to world-building also shouldn't be overlooked ... and it's great to hear that he hopes to tell stories like this for a very long time.

"The Hunger Games: Sunrise on the Reaping" hits theaters on November 20, 2026.

#3784. Megan Fox Went Full Twilight In Her Latest Post, And Halloween Couldn’t Have Come Sooner

Aside from her resume of acting gigs, Megan Fox is also known for her Halloween costumes. Even though Fox has gotten in hot water over her costumes choices, fans love to see what she dresses up as every year. She delivered early this time around, going full Twilight with a vampy photo dump on social media. Let's break it all down.

The Twilight movies were a sensation when they were in theaters, and are one of the most popular book to screen adaptations ever. Characters like Edward and Bella remain popular Halloween costumes all these years later, and Fox put her own spin on them a series of steamy gothic images. You can check them out for yourself with the link below:

Megan Fox is known for posting striking images of herself on social media, and this latest early Halloween photo dump is no exception. The images are quickly making their way around the internet, including her caption "who’s going to be the Bella to my Edward?"

She posted for her whopping 23.9 million followers on Instagram, many of whom took to the comments section. The responses are a mixture of compliments, jokes, and Twilight references. Some of which read:

Fans regularly re-watch Twilight, and it looks like Megan Fox does the same thing. Instead of wearing a costume that replicates the ones worn by the likes of Edward, Alice, and Rosalie the Transformers actress continued the sheer dress trend with a see-through white number that shows her red undergarments. She's surrounded by candles, one of which she uses to light a cigarette. I wonder what the Cullen family would think of this look.

Megan Fox didn't appear in the Twilight films, but she's no stranger to spooky vibes in her career. She famously played the title role of Jennifer's Body, which might finally be getting a sequel from Diablo Cody. She also starred as a killer robot in Subservience, and voiced the vampiric Nitara in Mortal Kombat 1. She's got experience a blood sucker, even if it wasn't in live-action. I have to wonder if this also inspire her latest Halloween costume.

As previously mentioned, Twilight fans regularly re-watch the film franchise despite it ending back in 2012. The same cant be said for the books, especially since author Stephenie Meyer released Midnight Sun back in 2020, which tells the story of the first novel through Edward's perspective.

The Twilight movies are streaming now with a Netflix subscription. We'll have to wait and see what acting projects Megan Fox picks up; I'm personally hoping she gets another cameo in Overcompensating Season 2.

He's since been able to work himself up to reviews, phoners, and press junkets-- and is now able to appear on camera with some of his favorite actors... just not as he would have predicted as a kid. He's particularly proud of covering horror franchises like Scream and Halloween, as well as movie musicals like West Side Story. Favorite interviews include Steven Spielberg, Spike Lee, Jamie Lee Curtis, and more.

#3783. 3 Magnificent Stocks Trading Near Their 52-Week Lows

Many top stocks are down this year, even though the S&P 500 has continued to soar to new heights. That can happen when a good chunk of stocks are up big, including Micron Technology, AMD, and CrowdStrike -- they've all more than doubled in value thus far in 2026.

At the other end of the spectrum, however, are some more troubled stocks. And for long-term investors, this is where the deals can be found: in stocks that aren’t doing well of late but still have strong businesses worth investing in. Three magnificent stocks that are trading near their 52-week lows that could be great long-term buys right now include Netflix (NFLX -2.16%), American Express (AXP -0.68%), and Uber Technologies (UBER -0.53%).

Shares of streaming company Netflix are down more than 30% this year. Concerns about its slowing growth are weighing on the stock, and with Paramount and HBO content now coming together under Skydance, competition may also intensify in the near future.

However, the business is still growing in double digits, with Netflix forecasting 13% revenue growth for the full year. Paying 18 times earnings for that kind of growth is more than reasonable, especially with the company still pursuing growth opportunities in live sports. Plus, all it takes is one hot new series or movie to get people excited again. Over the years, Netflix has shown it can produce its own content well, proving it can compete with the top studios in Hollywood.

It hit a new 52-week low on Tuesday, and with a light valuation, this could be an excellent stock to buy right now.

Another stock that hasn't been doing well this year is American Express. Shares of the credit card company have declined by over 15%, and this is even as the business has proven to be fairly robust and resilient amid challenging economic conditions. By targeting more affluent customers, the business is in an excellent position to do well even when other companies struggle.

Last week, the company released its third-quarter numbers, which showed solid 9% growth in its most recent period. Adjusted net income of $3.2 billion was up 11% year over year, as American Express demonstrates that it can continue to deliver solid numbers, even when economic conditions aren't ideal.

Currently, the stock is trading at a price-to-earnings multiple of 19, which is a fair bit lower than the S&P 500 average of 23. Although it has been rising in value recently, the stock is still about 8% away from its 52-week low of $290.97.

One of the cheapest growth stocks to buy right now is Uber, which is trading at less than 15 times its trailing earnings. The company, known for its popular ride-hailing app, has been a growth beast over the years, and it still has ample opportunities to grow in international markets and via robotaxis.

The company's revenue rose by 12% in the June quarter, and bookings increased by 24%. The platform continues to be a go-to option for people all over the world, providing them with plenty of flexibility and convenience. And that isn't likely to change anytime soon.

I believe the market is wrong about the risk autonomous driving poses to Uber, which appears to be weighing on the stock, as the company already has an excellent platform that users go to in search of rides, and adding an option for driverless taxis can be a way for it to naturally complement its business. Rather than a risk, autonomous driving could be a huge opportunity for Uber, and it's one that it has been investing heavily in.

Uber is an excellent stock to buy right now as it's currently trading right around its 52-week low.

#3782. This Philips Fingerprint Door Lock Set Lets You Ditch Your Keys for Less Than $90

Pradershika Sharma is a tech deals writer for Lifehacker. She has a Master’s degree in English Literature, a B.Ed., and a TESOL certification. She has been writing professionally since 2018, creating product reviews, affiliate articles, and search ads for global clients while working with Rubix Agency and Cognizant. Previously, she spent a year teaching English at the junior high level.An avid reader since childhood, Pradershika's idea of extreme sports is staying up to read “just one more chapter.” She lives in India.

Amazon has dropped the price of the Philips Keypad Front Door Lock Set to $89.98 from $142.99, taking $53 off and bringing it to its lowest price ever, according to price trackers. For that, you get an electronic deadbolt along with two matching lever handles in a matte black finish, so you can replace your existing front door hardware without having to find coordinating pieces separately. The set also makes a good case for itself if you've been considering keyless entry but don't necessarily want another device connected to your home network, since this lock operates entirely offline. Being offline doesn’t limit your ways in, though; you can use a fingerprint, a PIN, or one of two backup keys.

Philips claims the fingerprint reader works in 0.3 seconds, which could be handy when you're carrying groceries or don't feel like digging around for your keys. You can also register multiple fingerprints for family members and generate unlimited one-time PINs for visitors—so your dog walker can get in without needing your personal code. There's also an adjustable auto-lock timer that secures the door after 30 to 180 seconds, along with alerts for incorrect entries, low batteries, and a jammed lock. Privacy, away, and silent modes add further control.

Getting all of that onto your door is fairly simple and takes just a screwdriver, provided your door meets the requirements: 1⅜ to two inches thick, with a one-inch edge bore and a 2⅜- or 2¾-inch backset. The biggest limitation is its lack of connectivity—you can't unlock the door remotely, check its status from your phone, or integrate it with other smart home devices. Anyone who needs those features will have to look elsewhere, but for straightforward keyless access without connectivity, this Philips set covers the essentials.

#3781. GTA 6 Developers Are About To Do Something Historic

Workers at GTA 6 developer Rockstar will soon get to vote on recognizing a union.

The union, IWGB Union at Rockstar, is for Rockstar's UK-based developers. Rockstar North, based in Edinburgh, Scotland, is one of Rockstar's biggest outfits in the UK, and the lead developer on GTA 6.

The negotiating process between the union and Rockstar management took months, and the union, if recognized, would give workers a stronger seat at the table for key issues like pay and bonuses, overtime, generative AI policies, work-from-home setups, and situations involving layoffs.

The IWGB Rockstar Game Workers Union would become the largest in the UK video game sector, if it's voted in.

The voting period--November 12-26--overlaps with the release of GTA 6 on November 19.

At the same time, an employment tribunal over claims that Rockstar fired developers for trying to form a union is coming to a close, with final arguments set for November 17 and November 18. More than 30 IWGB members were let go from Rockstar in the UK in October 2025, with the union saying Rockstar fired them for trying to form a union. Rockstar, for its part, said the staffers were dismissed for leaking key details about Rockstar's upcoming games.

IWGB said the Rockstar case for unionization is an especially important one in the UK because the profile of Rockstar North--the developer of what is expected to be the biggest game of all time--could lead to a more widespread unionization push across gaming.

Rockstar publishing boss Jennifer Kolbe told Bloomberg recently, "We go where the team goes. So if they want a union, they will have a union.”

Rockstar Game Workers Union Committee member Shanti Easton-Steel said the studio has "the chance to making gaming history" with the upcoming unionization vote and the tribunal decision.

"As all our hard work, skill and creativity are experienced by GTA 6 players for the first time, our voices will now be heard for the first time too," Easton-Steel said. "When management choices are kept hidden, we can’t properly address issues like the gender pay gap, the uneven and ever-changing policies on overtime pay, or the concerns many co-workers hold around job security post-release."

IWGB Union president Alex Marshall added, "For anyone who cares about the games industry or the people who keep it running, this vote is just as historic as the game release. But for workers at Rockstar, it is so much more personal: it is fundamentally a choice about whether you get to have a say in how your day-to-day life is organized."

While the movie industry in Hollywood and beyond are largely unionized, the same is not true for video games. Unions are far more rare, but that's changing somewhat, as more and more developers in the US and worldwide are unionizing. For example, more than 1,900 workers at Blizzard recently reached a unionization contract with Microsoft, with workers winning things like guaranteed raises, guardrails on AI, and more.

#3780. Jon Ossoff Harnesses Short-Form Vertical Video on TikTok and Instagram to Reach Voters in the Georgia Senate Race

Behind a podium onstage at the second Georgia Senate debate, Senator Jon Ossoff made an appeal to a very specific audience. “I want everybody who’s clipping this debate for television news across Georgia and online tonight to roll that tape,” he said as he ridiculed his opponent, Mike Collins, for appearing to reverse his previously stated stance on abortion.

Ossoff knows his audience. Over the course of his campaign for reelection to the Senate in the battleground state of Georgia, the 39-year-old Democrat has vaulted to national stardom thanks in no small part to the power of short-form video. His pervasive presence on social feeds everywhere from Berkeley to Boston is a consequence of the clip-centered nature of his campaign, which churns out atomized video from his rallies and debates, and it has established Ossoff in the common liberal imagination as a great Democratic hope of 2028.

If 2024 was the podcast election, wherein Donald Trump’s return to power was fueled by his appearances with and support from garrulous commentators who built up vast audiences by talking about energy drinks and who constructed the pyramids, then these midterms have undoubtedly been the short-form video elections. Political stars are born by virtue of their campaign’s ability to produce clips designed to go viral, earn media attention, and rake in donations from voters who’ve learned to love the candidate through their feeds. Ossoff’s campaign has proved particularly adept in this regard: Its media strategy has cemented his reputation as one of the party’s most effective messengers on the stump, helped to raise an astonishing $110 million during this election campaign, and thrust his name to the forefront of the 2028 conversation.

For many Democrats, their love-at-first-sight encounter with Ossoff likely involved looking down at a video shot through a lens looking up at him. His rallies are cinematic productions. There are multiple camera shots, and one in particular stands out: the camera tilted upward at the candidate, the soft glow of the lighting accentuating his jawline, his eyes looking out beyond the crowd. This is called the hero shot, and it’s become a trademark of his media strategy, a visual grammar now synonymous with the young senator from Georgia and reminiscent of that of a young senator from Illinois. It helps that, like Barack Obama, Ossoff is tall and handsome, his shirtsleeves perma-rolled to the elbows as a kind of sartorial cue that this is a serious man getting down to business.

The rallies are for a Georgia audience, the voters Ossoff needs in order to do the immediate job of winning reelection in November. His campaign is determined to maintain the perception that this race is a local one, with the senator hyperfixated on the concerns of his constituents and uninterested in either national ambitions or the chatter among prospectors in Washington.

Yet Ossoff’s media strategy of filling social feeds with digestible hits from his rallies is inevitably a national one. And it’s been swell for fundraising. He raised $33 million in the third quarter, adding to his gargantuan total election-cycle haul of well over $100 million. His campaign says nearly 97% of the third-quarter contributions were small-dollar commitments of less than $100. (For comparison: Collins, his opponent, has raised $9.2 million in total this cycle.)

Sound bites have been a part of American politics for centuries, since before Abraham Lincoln told Republicans at the Illinois State Capitol that “a house divided against itself cannot stand.” What’s new is the format and how it has seeped into every crevice of American life, conquering our collective attention. “We’re in a clip-based economy,” says Keith Edwards, a progressive political commentator who oversaw content strategy on Ossoff’s 2021 runoff campaign. “Clavicular is a clear example of that. He really came out of nowhere just from the clips that surfaced online.”

Only a small fraction of America regularly watches the streams hosted by Twitch personalities like Clavicular and Hasan Piker, but our feeds are inundated with content from these figures, churned out by so-called clippers who are often paid to blast the videos out into the world for engagement revenue.

“[Ossoff] understands this in a way that [Donald] Trump used to. I think Trump just doesn’t have it in him anymore,” says Edwards. “Sure, it’s a speech. But really, the rally is a clip-making factory. When I go live on Tuesdays,” he adds, referring to his own stream on YouTube and Twitch, “maybe 4,000 people are watching concurrently, but the cut-downs and the clips that we make from it generate millions of views. So it’s just about being really focused with your time and understanding that what happens in that room is a production process to generate as many clips as possible.”

Ossoff is well suited to these shifts in media consumption. When he first ran for Senate in 2020, recalls Hussien Salama, the lead video producer for the campaign during the runoff election in early 2021, his campaign embraced TikTok in an effort to appeal to younger voters at a time when many politicians were keen to avoid the platform over its ties to China. Ossoff, then in his early 30s, was more natural in front of the camera than other candidates Salama would go on to work with, and he had a close relationship with his video team. But the candidate started paying real attention to social strategy after a video compilation of his stump speeches assailing his then opponent, David Perdue, went viral on TikTok. “That success and hearing from students, young voters on the ground being like, ‘We love your TikTok,’ made him engage much further,” Salama says.

Before getting into politics, Ossoff was a documentary producer, and his work often investigated public corruption. This helps to explain his preternatural ability to weave broader indictments of Trump’s administration into a persuasive narrative. There are characters, like “Prince Don and Prince Eric,” who are making foreign business deals while their father makes American foreign policy. Or Natalie Harp, Trump’s peculiar executive assistant whom Ossoff mentioned at a rally in August: “He doesn’t want to do the job. He wants to build his ballroom and travel with Natalie on their apparently defenseless flying palace gifted by the emir of Qatar.”

Ossoff's political persona has evolved—and exploded—since his first congressional run in 2017, though questions remain about his retail politicking.

The curious existence of Natalie Harp had been covered before by reporters in Washington. But it wasn’t until Ossoff made that passing mention—and clips of the remark drew millions of views—that she was thrust into the spotlight. The moment elevated Ossoff on the national stage as Trump and the White House went on the counterattack and late-night shows ruthlessly mocked Harp’s idiosyncrasies.

Ossoff’s rhetoric is crisp and prosecutorial, blending the mettle voters say they want from Democrats facing down the White House with the kind of intellectual critique that distinguishes him from Democrats who have sought to respond to Trump’s crass demagoguery with cringe sloganeering. “Donald Trump was in Dalton yesterday, oblivious to the collapse of his presidency,” Ossoff said at a rally in Cobb County this past weekend. “A crowd smaller than this one endured two hours of senile babbling while the price of oil hit its five-month high.” A video of Ossoff’s merciless interrogation of Jay Clayton during his confirmation hearing to serve as director of national intelligence has been viewed nearly 5 million times on YouTube. Chris Hayes, the MS NOW host, has said he now jokingly refers to Ossoff as the “Lisan al-Gaib” in his team’s Slack chat.

“He pays attention,” says Salama. “There are some candidates who I work with who don’t watch their things or aren’t aware of what their social does. Jon cares about how he’s being presented to the world.”

Ossoff’s campaign has been selective about the kind of media—and scrutiny—it allows for the candidate. The campaign has spent months declining my requests for an interview, and Ossoff has not sat for a profile with a news outlet this election cycle. One producer of a popular podcast with a national audience, speaking on condition of anonymity, complained that they spent a year and a half trying to convince Ossoff to appear on the show. In the end they were only able to corner the senator for a quick interview in the halls of Congress.

This strategy allows the campaign to exercise tight control over messaging, says Adrienne Elrod, the veteran Democratic strategist: “Everyone’s talking about him. It is working. And it’s not just because he’s talented; it’s because his team knows how to control the message and the framing and the content in a way that is digestible and energizing to Democrats, independents, and, frankly, some Republicans.”

“A lot of candidates are going to adopt that philosophy,” Elrod adds. “Come to our YouTube page, come to our Instagram page. We will control the content. We will not let a third-party journalist do it.”

What the Ossoff campaign has focused on is a particular flavor of short form. Most of his viral videos are clipped from scripted campaign speeches. He generally steers clear of long-form podcasts embraced by politicians like Gavin Newsom, who hosts his own, and James Talarico, the Texas Senate candidate who rose to prominence thanks in part to a sit-down with Joe Rogan. His operation does not pump out the kind of semiscripted videos that made Zohran Mamdani a star. And the impromptu quick hits with iPhone-camera-wielding reporters on Capitol Hill—the ones that Alexandria Ocasio-Cortez has perfected with a smile, a flick of her hair, and a game answer to whatever nonsense TMZ DC has cooked up this time—have proven more daunting terrain for the Georgia senator.

In a recent clip, Ossoff was confronted over his support for the Laken Riley Act, a vote that has drawn the ire of progressives over its consequences for immigration enforcement. The senator gave a curt answer before hastily escaping the shot. He said he had to go to a committee meeting.

“Like all politicians, Tony has seven or eight kinds of smile,” Martin Amis once wrote of Tony Blair. Jon Ossoff has none.

Across the endless stream of content bearing Ossoff’s face, blaring over news feeds and televisions, one rarely sees his teeth. What the Democratic strategists and donors who have interacted with Ossoff behind the scenes worry about is whether the candidate can excel at the off-the-cuff retail politics traditionally required to mount a bid for the presidency. “He’s a robot,” says one top Democratic strategist who has interacted with Ossoff. “He can do the speeches. But he can’t do the retail politics at all.” This concern has been simmering behind the scenes of Democratic Party politics since Ossoff’s explosion onto the national stage over the summer, and it was pushed into public conversation by David Axelrod, the former Obama campaign strategist, in an interview on Pod Save America earlier this month.

“I think that he is a star,” Axelrod said, reacting to Ossoff’s first debate with Collins. “You would teach off of this debate… I thought they were gonna have to remove Collins on a stretcher.” But: “I haven’t seen him smile. I’ve never seen him smile. He’s always very severe. So there are other elements of campaigning that I would like to see.” (Ossoff did crack a smile during one exchange over the summer, after a TMZ reporter asked him about people’s belief that he has “BDE.”)

Ossoff has cultivated a trademark seriousness in his campaigning.

“That was not my experience with him at all,” says Edwards when I ask about these concerns. “I have clips from the 2020 campaign on my phone, of him dancing, having fun with voters. Let’s just step back for a moment. It’s a very serious time. I think he’s actually one of the few people who is adequately defining the moment, and that’s why he’s resonating, because it is such a dangerous and corrupt moment.”

In this theory of the Ossoff case, his resting stone face serves a potent political purpose: proving to Democratic voters that he’s serious about their concerns. “He is serious because we’re dealing with some very serious issues right now, including a leadership vacuum in the executive branch of government,” says Elrod. “He is meeting that moment in a very serious way.”

This is an attitude that was first adopted by Ossoff during the 2020 campaign, when he had to convince voters—particularly the older Black Democrats of Georgia—that he was a real candidate in spite of his youth. “There is a particular requirement to signal that this guy knows what he’s talking about,” says Salama. “This guy is not here to screw around.”

Six years later, Ossoff has come to offer something Democrats have been craving: a candidate who is fluent in the messaging of the moment and gives them hope for a world after Trump. In former president Joe Biden, Democrats may have seen steadiness as an adequate antidote to the chaos, but they did not see much beyond that. In Ossoff, weary liberals have someone who speaks like Obama. His crowds seem to achieve the decibels of an Obama rally. Even his campaign logo, a navy “O” with three red stripes jutting out of it, looks an awful lot like Obama’s iconic 2008 insignia.

Ossoff has insisted that he will not run for president in the next cycle, putting his well-worn seriousness to work on questions around his own ambitions. “I have no interest in running for president in 2028—and, to be very frank, with just 16 days until the polls close, I want to urge everybody out there to remain focused on the task at hand,” he told Al Sharpton in a recent interview on MS NOW. “The Trump administration is out of control. They’ve lied us into war, they’ve sent prices skyrocketing.”

It was all Ossoff: sober, prepared, disciplined, punchy. In the short-form era, it’s also the kind of clip that will haunt Ossoff should he change his mind for 2028. Or not.

“I’m old enough to remember when Bill Clinton ran around, running for reelection in 1990, saying he wouldn’t run in ’92,” says Paul Begala, Clinton’s longtime strategist. “I’m old enough to remember when Barack Obama, in 2006, told Tim Russert and the whole country, ‘I’m not going to run in ’08.’ And he did. I’m very glad they broke those promises. I suspect they meant them in the moment. I’m sure Ossoff means it in the moment.”

#3779. Ryan Edwards’ Wife Amanda Conner Speaks Out After Cheating Claim

Ryan Edwards’ wife Amanda Conner set the record straight on her claim that she caught the Teen Mom alum cheating on her: “I should have never came to the internet with it.”

Amanda Conner is giving fans a reality check about husband Ryan Edwards.

One day after the influencer accused the Teen Mom alum of cheating on her, she took to social media to “clear the air” about her previous claim.

“I was very upset and I stated that my husband cheated on me,” Conner said in an Oct. 26 TikTok video. “Okay, so we’ve been dealing with this for a long time. Somebody has been giving Ryan’s number out and putting them in this escort website and these women call and text his phone, send him pictures.”

After seeing the messages on his phone, Conner says she “flipped the f--k out” and “came to the internet” before getting more clarity on the situation, emphasizing now that “Ryan did not physically step out on our marriage.”

“He didn’t emotionally or through text message cheat either,” she explained. “It was the ordeal of whoever keeps putting his number on these websites and it was those b--ches texting him.”

ICYMI, Conner—mom to 20-month-old daughter Presley with Edwards as well as 11-year-old son Brandon from a previous relationship—stated in a since-deleted TikTok video Oct. 25 that her husband of one year had cheated on her, alleging that “this is not even the first time it has happened.”

But now that the 35-year-old has the full picture, she shared her regret over acting on it so quickly.

“First of all, I should have never came to the internet with it,” she said. “Second of all, I should have done a little bit more digging and just slowed down a little bit before I flipped the f--k out like I did. But I’m a woman, that’s my husband.”

Edwards—also dad to kids Bentley, 17 with ex Maci Bookout as well as Jagger, 10, and Stella, 5, with ex Mackenzie Standifer—tied the knot with Conner in September 2025 in Tennessee. “Ryan is not a bad person,” she emphasized. “I would never get up on here and bad mouth my husband, bad mouth anyone in his family, I would never do that.”

In 2016, the couple wed and welcomed Maverick, their second child together. in 2016, the couple had their third child Maverick McKinney. In addition to filming for Teen Mom OG, the pair also maintain a fashion line called Things That Matter.

Farrah had Teen Mom's saddest story, her boyfriend having died in an accident two months prior to their daughter Sophia's birth. She later wrote a tell-all novel, My Teenage Dream Ended and made headlines after making a sex tape with porn star James Deen. She scored her own TV special, Being Farrah, in 2014. Three years later, she was axed from Teen Mom OG, allegedly because of her job as an adult entertainer. The reality star filed a $5 million lawsuit against MTV's parent company Viacom for "sex shaming" her porn career and has since dropped the suit after reaching a settlement. Farrah has remained in the spotlight, starring on reality shows such as Ex on The Beach, Celebrity Big Brother and Botched.

Mom to Leah Shirley, Amber has had her share of media attention since her initial debut on 16 and Pregnant in 2009. She was accused of domestic abuse against her daughter's dad, Gary Shirley, and the oft-combative teen mom spent 16 months in prison on drug-related charges. She was released in November 2013 and now stars on Teen Mom OG. In 2015, she got engaged to Montana radio DJ Matt Baier, but after years of cheating rumors about her then-boyfriend, the TV personality called it quits. Fortunately, Amber overcame the failed relationship with the love and support of cinematographer Andrew Glennon, and in May 2018, the pair welcomed son James. In 2019, the couple broke up while Amber was arrested and pleaded guilty to domestic violence following an altercation with Andrew. She was put on probation. In 2020, she debuted a new boyfriend, Dimitri Garcia.

#3778. Raye Wins 2026 Mercury Prize

Raye has won the 2026 Mercury Prize for her second album, This Music May Contain Hope. She clinched the award over competition including Sir Paul McCartney, Olivia Dean, Florence and the Machine, Jade, and Nia Archives. It’s Raye’s first time winning the prize; her 2023 album, My 21st Century Blues, also made the shortlist, but lost out to Ezra Collective.

Accepting the award during a ceremony in Newcastle, Raye said, "Albums are so important, and not to be bitter–because I feel like I’m mostly at peace with my past–but to come from a place where it’s all about singles and chasing the next thing, I just feel so grateful to be recognised. Hope is a very cheesy word, especially for us British people, we don’t do that deep stuff like that, but hope is something we all need…so I hope that message comes across to anyone who needs it.”

Elsewhere in the ceremony, Raye performed This Music May Contain Hope’s "I Hate The Way I Look Today” accompanied by a full big band. A number of other nominees also took the stage: Jade performed “Angel of My Dreams” from That’s Showbiz, Baby, Dean sang "Let Alone the One You Love," and Florence and the Machine offered a rendition of “One Of the Greats.” Pulp also played "Begging for Change" off the War Child Records compilation Help (2), which raised money for children living in war zones.

#3775. You Can Make Your WoW: Forever Character Now And Be Ready For Launch

World of Warcraft: Forever's character creation and name reservations are now live, letting you save some time on launch day by making your characters now rather than on launch day.

To access name reservations, you'll need to have Forever preinstalled on Battle.net and have purchased at least the $30 Skyborne Heroic Pack, which grants access to the game's new optional playable race, the Skyborne elves. Once you've done that, you can simply log into the game, select the ruleset you want to play on, and create your characters.

You can create and name up to three characters ahead of launch day. Unlike in other versions of developer Blizzard's MMORPG, Forever requires both a first name and last name for characters, giving you more options and creative freedom when it comes to names. 

When making a character, make sure you create them on the server ruleset you intend to play on. Once the game is live, Blizzard said there won't be a way to transfer characters to different rulesets. It's also worth noting that on player vs. player rulesets, you will only be able to create characters of a single faction on that specific ruleset, so make your choice of Alliance or Horde wisely. 

One might think with the game's beta over and around a week to go until Forever's release that Blizzard is slowing down its communication. Instead, Blizzard is releasing all kinds of Forever news and updates, including major changes to racial abilities, promises to improve its controversial layering system, and the last-minute addition of another server option. 

In case you weren't excited enough, Blizzard additionally just dropped a Forever PvP-focused animated music video to hype you up even more. The trailer shows a battle between the Horde and Alliance amidst a nu-metal soundtrack performed by vocalists Amy Lee, Poppy, and Courtney LaPlante.

Forever launches November 4 on PC and for the first time in WoW history will be fully playable on a controller.

#3774. I Was Impressed By Wildwood, And Its Creation Has Truly Inspired Me

Join The Club for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation, and sign you up to our newsletter.

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

The Wicker Popcorn Bucket Needs Its Own Intimacy Coordinator

A+ To Whomever Thought About Making The Wicker Poster Look Like A Harlequin Romance Cover

I've Seen The Prestige A Bunch Of Times Already, But Heck Yeah, I'll See It In IMAX

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

The process made it even more of a masterpiece.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Many animated films are innovative, beautifully drawn or designed, and full of rich storytelling. They’re true works of art. However, I think as adults we often skip the genre, especially if we don’t have children. There is an assumption that these films will only appeal to kids. Wildwood goes against that argument.

It begs to be seen by adults. This dark fairy tale is visually stunning and has a storyline that may make Wildwood one of the best fantasy movies of all time. I saw the film at an early fan screening. That show included an introduction by Wildwood director Travis Knight. He explained the film’s process and gave a shocking detail. It stuck with me and inspired me.

Films often take years to film, edit, and reshoot, but it’s rare that a film takes almost two decades to complete and release. The stop-motion process is time-consuming because of all the details necessary to make it work, so it’s understandable that it took a long time to create and release. However, I was shocked to learn that it took 16 years to make, but I felt a sense of power in this detail.

I think as humans, we often worry about not living our lives to the fullest. We worry that our time is fading, and that we won’t leave anything of greatness on this earth. I think artists and creatives especially feel the pressure of time. However, Wildwood is a reminder that yes, time on earth is short, but sometimes mesmerizing art needs time to develop.

This could apply to the art or the artist. We shouldn’t see time as this suffocating thing but as something that we’re lucky enough to get to use wisely (and sometimes unwisely). The Wildwood initial box office numbers may not be stellar, but everything about the movie is a masterpiece.

High box office numbers are nice, but long-lasting, impressive art will always be more powerful.

I am an entertainment reporter and freelance writer by trade. My goal is to be a successful fiction writer. Other fellow writers may know how daunting the idea is of putting pen to paper and continuing long enough to complete a story.

Hopefully my next project doesn’t take 16 years to complete. However, if a project isn’t completed in a few weeks, months, or even a year, Wildwood helped me get comfortable with the idea of the long-haul project. We live in an instant gratification society, but that’s not necessarily the best way to create everlasting art. Immortalization takes time.

So many decisions and details are put into Wildwood. You feel that in every frame of the movie. It’s a movie made with precision and love. Something AI cannot do. Wildwood feels like an anti-AI movie in how it shows that man-made art is needed and beautiful when given the chance.

In an interview with Variety, Travis Knight spoke about his feelings towards AI. This quote sums up why AI should never replace human-created art.

It’s an amazing piece of technology, but the concern is that when it supplants the artist, then we have an issue because the computer cannot speak about humanity at all. It doesn’t know what it feels like.

Make sure Wildwood is one of the 2026 movies that you add to your must-watch list.

Spent most of my life in various parts of Illinois, including attending college in Evanston. I have been a life long lover of pop culture, especially television, turned that passion into writing about all things entertainment related. When I'm not writing about pop culture, I can be found channeling Gordon Ramsay by kicking people out the kitchen.
