#3773. You Should Enable This Chrome Feature That Flags Suspicious Websites

Emily Long is a freelance writer based in Salt Lake City. After graduating from Duke University, she spent several years reporting on the federal workforce for Government Executive, a publication of Atlantic Media Company, in Washington, D.C. She has nearly a decade of experience as a freelancer covering tech (including issues related to security, privacy, and streaming) as well as personal finance and travel.

In addition to Lifehacker, her work has been featured on Wirecutter, Tom’s Guide, and ZDNET. Emily has also worked as a travel guide around the U.S. and as a content editor. She has a masters in social work and is a licensed therapist in Utah.

If you use Google Chrome, you can turn on a new safety feature that will warn you if you navigate to a suspicious website that looks like others already known for malicious behavior. The alerts are part of Enhanced Protection mode in Chrome's Safe Browsing, which checks your activity against potentially harmful sites and warns you if you're at risk of encountering malware, phishing, intrusive ads, or social engineering attacks.

Most internet users are familiar with the full-page alerts that appear if you navigate to a site that's known to be malicious. These red "dangerous site" warnings indicate a high risk of harm to the user and can't really be ignored since they take up the entire screen and urge you to go back to safety.

The new "suspicious site" alerts indicate possible risk of harm based on patterns consistent with other known malicious sites. This means that Google isn't as confident that the website is actually dangerous, but it looks a lot like others that are spreading malware or running phishing scams. These suspicious site warnings are smaller pop-ups with the text "This site doesn't seem right" and two button options: Mark as safe or Back to safety. The different colors, text, and formatting make it easy for users to distinguish between what Google deems dangerous versus suspicious.

Safe Browsing in Chrome has three options: standard protection, enhanced protection, and no protection at all (which isn't recommended). Standard protection checks your browsing activity, extensions, and downloads against Google's list of unsafe sites, which is updated periodically. By contrast, enhanced protection works in real time and uses AI analysis. Note that enhanced protection does require you to share more information about your activity to Google, and the data are linked to your Google Account.

While privacy-conscious users may be hesitant to send more info to Google, enhanced protection is likely worthwhile for those who aren't as savvy to advanced scam tactics (and therefore more likely to enter credentials or financial information on or download a file from a malicious website). To enable Enhanced Protection and receive suspicious website warnings, go to Settings > Privacy and security in Chrome and toggle Enhanced protection on under the Safe Browsing section.

Emily Long is a freelance writer based in Salt Lake City.

#3772. 3 Questions to Ask Yourself Before Doing a Roth Conversion in 2026

If your traditional IRA or 401(k) balance keeps growing, you may be headed toward a good but frustrating problem. Eventually, you'll be forced to take required minimum distributions (RMDs), which could drive up your taxes in retirement and potentially cost you more in Medicare premiums. But a Roth conversion could come to the rescue.

With a Roth conversion, you move funds from a traditional IRA or 401(k) into a Roth IRA. From there, your money gets to grow tax-free, withdrawals are tax-free, and you don't have to concern yourself with RMDs.

But a Roth conversion isn't the right choice for everyone. If you're thinking of initiating a Roth conversion this year, make sure to run through these key questions first.

Your tax bracket should play a big role in your Roth conversion decision. If your current one is high, a Roth conversion may not make sense.

Let's say you're a high earner in the 32% bracket. That means for every $1 you move to a Roth IRA, you'll owe $0.32. A $100,000 Roth conversion could cost you $32,000 in taxes, which makes it a less appealing prospect.

There's more to consider than just your current tax bracket before doing a Roth conversion. Also consider your future one.

If you expect to be in a lower tax bracket in retirement than you're in today, a Roth conversion may not pay off at all. Going back to our example, if you convert at 32% today but only end up in the 24% tax bracket in retirement, you'll have paid the IRS extra on your savings.

Of course, the total math isn't that simple because there are other factors to consider, like the benefits of having tax-free retirement income. Tax-free withdrawals could help keep your Medicare premiums down and potentially get you out of paying taxes on your Social Security benefits.

Still, the tax bracket question of now versus the future is an important one to answer. Some people may find themselves in a higher tax bracket in retirement due to accumulated savings. It's not a given that you'll always end up in a lower one, so think about your specific financial circumstances.

If charitable giving is a big part of your retirement plan, a Roth conversion may not make sense. Qualified charitable distributions allow you to transfer funds to charities directly, thereby bypassing taxes while satisfying RMDs.

It doesn't pay to do a Roth conversion if you intend to donate most of the money to charity anyway. In that situation, you're paying taxes instead of waiting to unload that money without an increase in your tax bill.

Roth conversions can be a strategic move. Just make sure a Roth conversion is right for you before taking the plunge.

#3771. Corruption Allegations Aren’t Susan Collins’s Biggest Problem. That’s Donald Trump.

Susan Collins was concerned. Not in the publicly hand-wringing way for which the Maine Republican senator has been repeatedly mocked, most notoriously for trusting then Supreme Court nominee Brett Kavanaugh’s statement that Roe v. Wade was “settled law.” No, this was a serious political concern.

In late September, ProPublica published a story alleging that Donald Trump’s Department of Justice killed an investigation into whether Collins had given a military contractor favorable treatment in exchange for campaign money. The bombshell dropped just as Collins was heading into the final weeks of her toughest reelection race ever, a contest that could decide whether Republicans retain a Senate majority. The corruption accusations threatened to damage Collins’s strongest appeal to voters: her ability to deliver jobs and funding to Maine. Should she issue a statement denying any wrongdoing and then ignore the ProPublica story as much as possible, trying to cut off its oxygen? Or should Collins punch back hard?

Those strategic choices were the immediate, high-stakes priority. Yet even as her camp calculated, an absurdity hovered in the background: After years of Trump bluntly insulting Collins, could he really have gone out of his way to try to do her a favor? “I don’t know whether that notion,” a Collins insider says, “is ironic or comic.”

Collins and Trump tangled frequently during Trump’s first term as president, peaking with Collins voting in favor of his second impeachment after the January 6 insurrection.

Troy Jackson is the Democratic nominee, a late replacement for the disgraced Graham Platner. The former logger and state legislator can be a stiff speaker, but in four contentious debates, Jackson has effectively blamed Collins for the soaring cost of everything from home heating oil to blueberry processing—by tying Collins to Trump’s tariffs and the president’s war with Iran. Because no matter who the Democrat on the ballot is this November, in many respects, the greatest obstacle to Collins winning a sixth term has long been the leader of her own party.

The two tangled frequently during Trump’s first term as president, peaking with Collins voting in favor of his second impeachment after the January 6 insurrection. The invective resumed when Trump returned to the White House. Along the way, he has castigated Collins as a “disaster,” a “loser,” and “absolutely atrocious,” and declared that she should “never be elected to office again.” Those are just some of the public attacks. In private, the president reportedly tore into the senator in a “profanity-laced rant” over the phone, after she voted in January in favor of curbing Trump’s war powers.

“Like most things with the president, he says exactly what he thinks,” a senior Republican strategist says. Steve Bannon, the on-again, off-again Trump adviser, sees something in Trump’s antagonism that goes beyond the political. “She’s the kind of person he has no time for—the mousy personality,” Bannon tells me. “She’s just a non-event. Even when she’s against something, she’s not really against it. And it’s not like she’s calling him and explaining why she’s not on board. It irks him.” Collins has sidestepped questions about whether she would campaign alongside Trump if he turns up in Maine this fall, but she pointed to a scheduling conflict to explain her absence from an appearance by Vice President J.D. Vance in Brewster in August.

Yet Trump has lately bitten his tongue about Collins, even as she strenuously tries to distance herself from him on everything from July’s fatal ICE shooting in Maine to Trump’s recent imposition of punitive tariffs on Canadian goods. Insiders say the president’s silence is strategic: He needs Collins to win if the Republicans are going to hold their Senate majority. “He’s pragmatic, a practical guy, always has been,” says the senior Republican strategist, who knows Trump well. “Is there any other Republican who could win in Maine?”

Mike DuHaime, a national Republican strategist who directed Senate campaign spending in previous cycles, believes Collins’s familiarity to Mainers will insulate her from the corruption allegations. “The biggest factor in that race,” he says, “is whether voters who still like Susan Collins are going to try to send a message to Trump at her expense.”

You may have heard Maine’s Democrats have had an exciting year. First came the meteoric rise of Platner, an Iraq veteran turned oyster farmer who seemed to be the next great populist hope until he was undone by an unceasing run of scandals, from the Nazi tattoo on his chest to accusations of sexual assault. (Platner has said he wasn’t aware the tattoo was recognized as a Nazi symbol and has since covered it. He has denied the sexual assault allegations.)

Platner’s noisy ascension and implosion didn’t just leave the Democrats looking untrustworthy. For months the saga consumed nearly all the media coverage in the Maine race, turning Collins, the five-term incumbent, into an afterthought. Whenever she did enter the conversation, Collins was commonly reduced to a caricature: the disingenuously naïve, dithering swing vote.

The people in Maine and DC who know her best, though, describe Collins as far tougher than she looks. “People always expect her to break,” a senior Democratic senator says. “She is more savvy and has more integrity than most people give her credit for.” Even a national Republican strategist with ties to the White House offers grudging respect: “She knows exactly what she’s doing and why she’s doing it. She is a damn good politician.”

Indeed. Collins made some mistakes during four debates with Jackson in the past month, including garbling the name of a Maine man shot and killed by ICE. But in the final showdown she also nimbly improvised a response to a protestor, provoking Jackson into telling Collins to “shut up,” a moment that generated helpful headlines.

Collins grew up just about as far north as you can get on the East Coast and still be in the United States, roughly 15 miles from Canada, in the town of Caribou, Maine, population 7,396. In a region known for its poor potato farmers, Collins’s family, as owners of a hardware store and a lumber mill, were relative royalty. “They had serious money,” says Gerry Conley Jr., a former second-generation Democratic Maine state legislator. “And she had a lot of connections.”

“She has not gotten to where she is on charisma," says Republican strategist and Maine native Lance Dutson. "She has gotten there on diligence. In some ways, she still thinks like a staffer.”

Still, Collins’s talents seemed, initially, best suited to working behind the scenes. Her first political job was as a staffer for 12 years to Maine Republican senator William Cohen. She returned north to serve as Governor John “Jock” McKernan’s commissioner of the Department of Professional and Financial Regulation, a role that put Collins at the center of a 16-day state government shutdown that was prompted by a dispute over workers’ compensation rates. “She was by far the smartest person in that administration,” Conley says. “She was dangerous, a tough negotiator.”

Bookish and bespectacled, Collins didn’t seem like an obvious candidate for office herself. It was something of a surprise, then, when Collins ran for governor of Maine in 1994; it was little surprise that she finished a weak third to Angus King, an independent, who won.

But Collins has always been a driven student. While working for Cohen, she met his chief of staff, Thomas Daffron. Daffron—who has shifted between jobs as a lobbyist and a congressional staffer—became Collins’s political mentor and, in 2012, her husband. In 1996, she returned as a more polished campaigner and won the Republican nomination to replace Cohen, whom president Bill Clinton appointed secretary of defense. Collins effectively styled herself a moderate in the vein of Maine senatorial predecessors Cohen, Olympia Snowe, and Margaret Chase Smith. She also demonstrated, not for the last time, a knack for facing an underwhelming opponent. Collins dispatched Joe Brennan, a retread Democrat, by slightly more than five points. In the three decades since, she hasn’t had a genuinely close race.

The battle against Jackson, though, looks like it will go down to the wire: a Washington Post poll, released October 9, showed the Democrat edging to a four-point lead. Bannon, interestingly, believes the Republicans’ intense focus on saving Collins could very well backfire for the GOP, not just in Maine, but across the country. “Iowa is in play, Kansas is in play, Nebraska is in play—because of Susan Collins,” he says. “The Senate majority didn’t move MAGA legislation, like the Save America Act, because John Thune was adamant about protecting his weakest members. And Collins is always number one on that list. If you don’t give the grassroots, low-propensity voters some reasons to show up in a midterm, they just don’t.”

In Maine, Collins’s problem isn’t the MAGA faithful. It’s independents, who make up nearly 33% of the state’s registered voters, and who have soured on Trump. So Jackson and Democrats are relentlessly trying to lash Collins tightly to the president. “Donald Trump owns Susan Collins,” Jackson told reporters recently. With independents, Collins is counting on the thing that has sustained her all these years: Whether you agree with her policy choices or not, she’s stable, sane, and at heart a “county girl,” a humble local, a true Mainer. Platner is gone from the race, but the turmoil he generated still resonates and makes Collins look dependable compared to the Democrats. The crescendo of outsider spending and criticisms could help bolster her appeal. “There’s a cultural norm, deeply rooted in Maine, that she’s one of ours and we’re going to stick with her,” says Tom Connolly, a Portland lawyer and longtime Democratic activist. “She has remarkable survival skills.”

Collins frequently walks through the Senate hallways carrying a large stack of briefing books. This isn’t an affectation. “She’s the hardest working person I have ever experienced in my life,” says Lance Dutson, a Republican strategist and Maine native who was a senior aide on three Collins Senate campaigns and also worked in her Washington office. “She has not gotten to where she is on charisma. She has gotten there on diligence. In some ways, she still thinks like a staffer.”

Collins travels from Washington to her home in Bangor nearly every weekend. In mid-July she marched, to a mix of cheers and boos from the 10,000 spectators, in the 42nd edition of the Moxie Festival Parade, an annual event in Lisbon, Maine. “She didn’t hang out or anything afterwards,” says Angie D’Amours, the event’s cochair. “Angus King [now Maine’s other senator] does, typically. She was out of there fairly quickly.”

Collins’s team did not return requests for comment for this story. The senator has energetically crisscrossed the state this fall, though a local reporter has been struck by how tightly controlled Collins’s camp keeps her appearances. “Platner was making himself available for hours in breweries and pizza places, and it stoked this enormous hunger for town halls,” the journalist says. “Susan hasn’t had one. When she does go to events, they tend to be way up north in the reddest, safest, most provincial towns, where she gets a warm welcome and knows enough people personally to feel insulated from any public negativity, of which there’s plenty.”

Protesters gathered outside Collins’s Maine office after July’s ICE shooting.

Maintaining a sense of normalcy and a human connection with her constituents has become increasingly difficult as opponents have dialed up their ire—most recently, flocking to Collins’s Maine office to loudly protest July’s ICE shooting. Even the sidewalk outside her home has become a flashpoint: When someone chalked a pro-choice message on the cement in 2022, Collins called the cops.

“The money she delivers, it really matters to small towns, and she and her staff are really masterful at letting people know that she was instrumental in bringing this funding home to Maine,” says Susan Young, the opinion page editor at the Bangor Daily News, who has known the senator for more than 25 years. “Susan Collins is a fabulous technocrat. But if you want someone who is a visionary, who will speak truth to power—there are more times she needs to buck the administration.”

Indeed, one payoff for Collins sweating the legislative and bureaucratic details has been a literal payoff for Maine. The senator, now chair of the powerful Senate Appropriations Committee, has steered nearly $1.5 billion to the state in just the past five years, funding everything from hospitals to the Bath Iron Works. Over her career, Collins has made an art form of leveraging her votes, and the possibility that she might cross partisan lines, to support Maine projects, often to the frustration of Democrats. “Harry Reid used to say, ‘Susan Collins is always there when you don’t need her,’” says Jim Manley, a longtime top aide to the late Democratic Senate majority leader.

Trump’s arrival, though, blew up Collins’s preference for precision and process. The Capitol Hill game shifted from conventional horse-trading to a cult of personality, and Collins seemed out of her depth. But as long as she could occasionally appear to distance herself from Trump and maintain her image as a tortured, well-meaning moderate—while ultimately voting with Republicans 95% of the time—she could protect herself with enough Maine voters.

Now, however, Collins’s talent for funneling money home has collided with Trump’s destruction of governing norms. There’s no indication the president knew about the DOJ pulling the plug on its investigation of Collins in 2025—but the Trump administration has, more broadly, dialed back the pursuit of white-collar crime.

When the ProPublica story broke, an FBI spokesman said the bureau had “found nothing” to show that Collins or her campaign had broken any laws. Of course, that hasn’t stopped Jackson’s campaign and its allies from hammering Collins over the allegations. The senator’s team has chosen to respond aggressively. Annie Clark, a top aide, called the accusations “categorically false.” Shortly after Maine Democrats staged a press conference outside Collins’s office in Portland to trumpet the issue, Collins’s campaign manager Steve Abbott held his own, dismissing ProPublica’s reporting as relying on “allegations from a guy who was…trying to get his sentence reduced, as the article states directly.” Collins herself went on a Wall Street Journal podcast to label the story “a disgraceful smear job.”

The forceful counterattack makes sense in such a close race. And the controversy, the Collins insider says, may turn out to be perversely beneficial. “I have no question about the senator’s integrity,” he says. “But this whole thing could end up reminding people she’s relentless in pursuing money and jobs for Maine.” The state’s Democrats had their own scandal, and it drove their first nominee out of the race. Collins may not be corrupt. But at least her mess is on brand.

#3770. Wizards of Waverly Place’s David Henrie Reveals Shocking TV Residuals

Wizards of Waverly Place alum David Henrie admitted he got "nothing really" in terms of residuals while discussing his paychecks for the hit Disney series, which aired 2007 to 2012.

David Henrie is pulling back the curtain on the not-so-magical side of show business.

Indeed, the Wizards of Waverly Place alum recently revealed if he still gets residual paychecks over a decade after filming the hit kids' show on the Disney Channel from 2007 to 2012.

"Not really, no," Henrie confessed during the Oct. 26 episode of the Good Guys podcast. "Disney was very, I think it was after at that time they hadn’t combined, so there really wasn’t much residuals. Maybe a little bit here and there, but nothing really."

E! News reached out to Disney but did not receive an immediate response.

But while Wizards wasn't exactly the money-maker fans might have expected, his other TV gigs—which included NCIS, Without a Trace and a recurring role on How I Met Your Mother as Ted Mosby’s future son—were much more spellbinding on his bank accounts.

"The network stuff that I did, like How I Met Your Mother, was a completely different thing," Henrie—now a father of four with wife Maria Cahill—shared. "Network TV took really good care of their people, but I don’t know what it was about kids television."

So the Wizards Beyond Waverly Place star can tell a story about how he ultimately did meet a solid residual check. After all, though Henrie shot 30 episodes of the sitcom, which ran nine seasons, "then they cut them into 90. Isn’t that crazy?"

And more episodes means more magic in the paycheck.

"I remember one time I literally I thought it was a joke," Henrie recounted. "I went and got a residual check out of the mail and I saw it and I thought it was a joke."

"So, I called my like business manager," he continued, "and I was like, 'Hey, they made a mistake. Like that there’s no way. I think they added a zero on accident.' And they’re like, 'Nope, that’s it.' I was like, 'Unbelievable.'"

Henrie isn’t the only star to conjure up a confession about his paycheck. Keep reading for more stars who’ve detailed how they have—or have not—cashed out in Hollywood…

Jackass star reflected on the "comical" amount of money he was paid for doing death-defying stunts on the MTV series' first season in 2000.

"When it was all said and done, after taxes, I was paid less than $1,500 for the entire first season of Jackass," the daredevil told Playboy in June 2026. "Before the show came out, my sister kicked me out of the house. I was broke, unemployed, and homeless, and a star on this big MTV show."

Oscar winner Jennifer Lawrence was paid $25 million to star in Adam McKay’s ensemble disaster comedy Don’t Look Up. But her costar and fellow Oscar winner Leonardo DiCaprio got $30 million.

“Look, Leo brings in more box office than I do,” Lawrence told Vanity Fair for its’ December 2020/January 2021 issue. “I’m extremely fortunate and happy with my deal. But in other situations, what I have seen—and I’m sure other women in the workforce have seen as well—is that it’s extremely uncomfortable to inquire about equal pay. And if you do question something that appears unequal, you’re told it’s not gender disparity but they can’t tell you what exactly it is.”

Her sanguine attitude was hard-fought: When the disparity between Lawrence’s compensation and that of her male costars for 2013’s American Hustle was revealed in the 2014 Sony email hack, it prompted an industry-wide conversation about the gender pay gap in Hollywood.

"I'm so fortunate to have my job. My problem is not money," Lawrence told the U.K.’s Channel 4 News in 2017. "I wasn't upset that I only got this many millions for a movie. That's ridiculous. I was angry about the unfairness and inequality."

The *NSYNC alum revealed that he and his fellow bandmates JC Chasez, Joey Fatone, Chris Kirkpatrick and Justin Timberlake didn't make much money during their years with the group.

“We had a per diem: $35 a day,” he revealed on The Zach Sang Show in 2026. “Our first check was a $10,000 check after years of No. 1 albums, sellout tours. I don’t know how many millions they made off of merchandise because merchandise was insane.”

“And then they finally gave us a check for $10,000,” he recalled. “You’re like, ‘Oh, thanks.’ We should be grateful for that, been working for free for two, three years now.”

Less comparable to DiCaprio’s salary: The $60,000 “before commissions and taxes” that Jonah Hill was paid for 2013’s The Wolf of Wall Street, while the titular wolf made $10 million.

“They gave me the lowest amount of money possible,” Hill recalled on The Howard Stern Show in 2014. But it was the chance to appear in a Martin Scorsese movie, so the Moneyball alum wanted to get the deal done before anyone could change their mind.

“I would sell my house and give him all my money to work for [Scorsese],” explained Hill, who earned his second Best Supporting Actor nomination for his turn as a squirrelly substance-abusing stockbroker. “This isn't what you make money for. You do 22 Jump Street or you do other things, and you can pay your rent. I would have done anything in the world. I would do it again in a second. This isn't about money. You should do things that you care about."

While there's no cash prize for nabbing Dancing With the Stars' mirrorball trophy, “whoever makes it to the finale, you all get paid up until then," Jenna Johnson, who won season 33 with partner Joey Graziadei, shared. "And you do get a nice bonus.”

"If you win," Johnson, who noted pro contracts are dependent on seniority, continued. "it’s not like you get a million dollars. You’re just getting a cute trophy together.” As for those who say farewell to the ballroom early, well, as she explained, "You’re guaranteed until a certain amount of weeks.” According to multiple reports, dancer salaries start at around $1,200 to $1,600 an episode, which can increase to up to of $100,000 per season. (ABC has not confirmed these reports.) On the flip side, celebrities receive a starting salary of $125,000. As they cha-cha further into the competition, stars  receive additional bonuses with the semifinalists and finalists getting paid up to $50,000 extra.

The actress seemingly confirmed that the Friends cast—including Jennifer Aniston, Courtney Cox,David Schwimmer and Matt LeBlanc—get $20 million in residuals per year from the show re-airing, quipping to The Times in April 2026 that if she had to guess why, it's “Because Phoebe Buffay was so great?"

But in actuality, she praised the talents of her costars for their performances.

“I felt I did OK, but Jennifer and Courteney? Amazing,” she raved. “David and Matt? They had me laughing so hard. And then Matthew [Perry]—he was just beyond us all.”

To hear Joe Giudice tell it, his and ex-wife Teresa Giudice's salary for season one of The Real Housewives of New Jersey was nothing to flip over. "We got paid nothing the first season," he detailed on daughter Gia Giudice's Casual Chaos podcast in August 2025. "I'll even say, it was like $25,000. It was ridiculous. But, whatever."

And the ingredientses they put into that initial fame-making season weren't cheap. "I think the first year we had that big party in my great room," the father of four reflected. "I think that show cost me $150,000 the first year."

Oprah Winfrey happily accepted only a little bit of green to star in 1985’s The Color Purple.

“They were only offering $35,000 to be in this film, and it is the best $35,000 I ever earned,” the billionaire media mogul told Essence in 2023. “It changed everything and taught me so much.”

When she scored the role of late Tejana singer Selena Quintanilla in the 1997 biopic Selena, Jennifer Lopez became the first Latina actress to earn $1 million for a film.

“I was too young and didn’t know what the hell was going on,” Lopez told Variety in 2019, reflecting on her breakout movie. “It was great they offered me a million dollars. I feel like everyone was making a statement.”

And it paved the way for her to make hundreds of millions more, though sometimes her participation doesn’t cost a thing.

“I do things because I love them,” Lopez explained to GQ in 2019. “I didn't get paid a whole bunch of money for Hustlers. I did it for free and produced it. I bank on myself, you know? Like Jenny From the Block—I do what I love."

Adrien Brody didn’t do The Brutalist for the money. Rather, he took on the role of a Hungarian architect suffering for his art in post-WWII America for $250,000 and won his second Oscar for Best Actor.

“I need a studio movie now, because I’ve poured it all into this,” Brody quipped to Variety, referring to the “barn that looks like a castle” he’s renovating in upstate New York.

“Do you guys know what they pay us?” Pete Davidson cracked to New York magazine when asked ahead of Saturday Night Live's 50th anniversary special how he spent his money when he was first hired at the age of 20 in 2014. “It’s like three grand an episode, so I think I got dinner.”

Interestingly, the salaries have remained proportional for SNL newcomers, with season one stars John Belushi, Jane Curtin, Gilda Radner, Dan Aykroyd and Chevy Chase reportedly earning $750 an episode back in 1975. Which was roughly equivalent to $3,300 in 2014, according to the Consumer Price Index Inflation calculator. (Though the $750 of 1975 is $4,428 in 2025.)

While Kenan Thompson makes a lot more than $3,000 per episode of SNL now that he’s in his record 22nd season, the former Nickelodeon star had to start somewhere.

“That first commercial when they paid me, it was $800,” the All That alum revealed in Demi Lovato‘s 2024 documentary Child Star. “I was 12 so that may as well have been a million dollars."

It turns out Gilmore Girls’ status as a beloved rewatchable classic is literally priceless.

“There really are no residuals on Netflix,” Lauren Graham, who played Lorelai Gilmore for seven seasons and then reprised the role for 2016’s Gilmore Girls: A Year in the Life, said on Jimmy Kimmel Live in March 2025. “But I’ve been paid in love and appreciation.”

Feeling not so appreciated, Tommy Dorfman got specific about her 13 Reasons Why paycheck during the 2023 SAG-AFTRA strike—which was prompted in part (as was the WGA strike) by streamers like Netflix not making it a practice to pay additional compensation to cast and crew once a show had become a big hit.

"my earnings for the entire first season of 13 reasons why were $29,953.24 prior to agency and manager fees (20%) and taxes," Dorfman wrote on Threads July 24. "8 episodes over six months." She continued, “i did all of the promo and had KEY ART for this show, flew round trip from NYC to SF to shoot for every episode, was kept for days without pay/working. i barely qualified for insurance."

And, Dorfman added, “within the first 28 days of release, the show's season 1 garnered a total of 476 million view hours. this is why we strike."

Without naming names or numbers, Dax Shepard told fellow Parenthood alum Lauren Graham when she appeared on his podcast that he knew he made “among the lowest of all the actors” on their NBC drama, which ran for six seasons.

But, the Armchair Expert host stressed that he did not mind.

“Every job I had ever had in my life prior to Parenthood, I made a point to find out what everyone was making,” he said. “I'd always figure it out—either a conversation where I get it out of them or I backchannel through an agent."

So he made a point to purposely not seek out such info about his Parenthood family, which helped with enjoying the experience even when he did get an inkling of where he was on the pay pyramid.

“I got a dollar for writing the script,” George Clooney told the Los Angeles Times in 2005 about the financial strings he pulled to get his film Good Night, and Good Luck made. “I had to endorse my check for directing and turn in my acting salary. [Actor, cowriter and coproducer Grant Heslov] and I each made a buck for doing it.”

His labor of love about CBS News journalist Edward R. Murrow speaking truth to power in the 1950s had legs, though: Making his Broadway debut, Clooney wrapped his months-long stint playing Murrow in a stage adaptation of the film  in June 2025. And with the play having grossed a Broadway record (for a nonmusical) $3.3 million during a preview week before it officially opened April 3, he made a lot more than $1.

For his chilling turn as Patrick Bateman in 2000’s American Psycho, Christian Bale was paid “the absolute minimum they were legally allowed to pay me” because no one in the production really wanted to hire him except director Mary Harron, the Welsh actor told GQ in 2022.

He made so little, Bale shared, that he remembered “sitting in the makeup trailer and the makeup artists were laughing at me because I was getting paid less than any of them.”

No, the Game of Thrones cast didn't get paid $300,000 per episode, according to Emilia Clarke.

“We didn’t earn that much," she told Variety in 2026. "Can you imagine? I’d have been driving a couple of Porsches!"

However, the actress—who played Daenerys Targaryen for eight seasons between 2011 and 2019—noted that the money she made throughout the HBO show did provide her with enough financial security to pay off her parents’ mortgage.

His uncle may have been Rico, but Jon Heder confirmed in 2010 that he was “initially” only paid $1,000 for Napoleon Dynamite.

After the irreverent comedy became a sleeper hit, he was able to renegotiate for a piece of the profits, telling the New Zealand Herald, “They went a little bit higher.”

Djimon Hounsou said he’s “definitely underpaid” in Hollywood despite his prestigious list of credits, including Amistad and Blood Diamond.

"I've been in this business and making films now over two decades," the Beninese actor told CNN’s Larry Madowo in January 2025. "And still—with two Oscar nominations and been in many big blockbuster films—and yet I’m still struggling financially to make a living."

Rebel Wilson detailed in her 2024 memoir Rebel Rising that she was paid $3,500 to do Bridesmaids, “a fee that I then had to pay directly to the Screen Actors Guild to join the union. So really, I got paid nothing.”

Still, she wrote, the experience was “everything!”

And the Australian actress’ star rose ever a-ca higher, with her fee going from the SAG minimum scale of $65,000 for 2012’s Pitch Perfect to $10 million for 2017’s Pitch Perfect 3. Wilson wrote that she raked in $20 million total for the threequel, Isn’t It Romantic and The Hustle, though—reality alert—she “lost almost 50 percent to taxes, 10 percent to agents and 5 percent” to her lawyer. But even after those obligations and paying her publicist, business manager and assistant, Wilson “netted what to me was an absolute fortune.”

In late 2017, Ellen Pompeo negotiated a new Grey’s Anatomy contract for $575,000 per episode, a seven-figure signing bonus and two full backend equity points worth an estimated $6 million to $7 million.

“I’m 48 now, so I’ve finally gotten to the place where I’m OK asking for what I deserve, which is something that comes only with age,” Pompeo told The Hollywood Reporter in 2018. “Because I’m not the most ‘relevant’ actress out there. I know that’s the industry perception because I’ve been this character for 14 years. But the truth is, anybody can be good on a show season one and two. Can you be good 14 years later? Now, that’s a f--kin’ skill.”

Reflecting more recently on how she always made less than costar Patrick Dempsey, even though she was the Grey in question, Pompeo said she didn’t begrudge him his money.

But, she said on a March 2025 episode of Call Her Daddy, “Just being that I was the namesake of the show, I deserved the same and that was harder to get. I wasn't salty about him getting what he got. I was salty that they didn't value me as much as they valued him and they never will.”

Jamie Lee Curtis recalled getting paid $2,000 a week—so $8,000 in total—for 1978’s Halloween, her movie debut. “I mean,” she told Rotten Tomatoes in 2018, “nobody got paid, anything.”

Yet while the scream queen earned four figures, director John Carpenter’s friend Nick Castle felt like a king making $25 per day to share the role of Michael Myers. (It’s Anthony Moran whose face you see when the killer’s mask comes off for a freaky second, and editor Tommy Lee Wallace also logged screentime as Michael.)

“That was a lot at the time!” Castle, who attended film school at USC with Carpenter, told Vanity Fair in 2018. “You have to remember: My interest in doing the film was being on set, so I could demystify the experience of filmmaking and directing. I expected to hang around the set for no money. But hey, $25 per day, and all I had to do was wear a rubber mask.”

Taraji P. Henson earned a Best Supporting Actress Oscar nomination for 2008’s The Curious Case of Benjamin Button, but years later she was still fired up by the $150,000 she accepted to be in the movie—after producers rejected the $500,000 quote she brought to the table.

As for the film’s leads Brad Pitt and Cate Blanchett, “You don’t hear a lot of $20 million quotes anymore, but at this time that was happening,” Henson said on the Ladies First With Laura Brown podcast in 2021. “And rightfully so—I’m not saying they shouldn’t have paid Cate and Brad what they deserved.”

But, she continued, “I’m bringing a certain amount to the seat too and I felt like what I was asking at that time in my career was fair, was fair to the ticket sales that I would contribute to this big film. Wouldn’t do it.” Henson was “gutted” when they offered $100,000, she recalled. “When it was all said and done I got $150,000, but I had to swallow my pride, baby.”

Breaking it down, “I know people go, ‘$150,000, that’s a lot of money!’” Henson acknowledged. “I don’t ever want people to think that I’m ungrateful because that is not me.” But, she calculated, “Uncle Sam is going to take 50 percent of that, so now you’re left with, what? $75,000. Now before Uncle Sam takes the money, I have to pay my team before taxes, 30 percent. So once Uncle Sam takes his 75, then I got another 30 that’s coming off of that 75, so I may have made $40,000?”

While Blanchett has made plenty of money over the years, it didn’t all come from playing royal elf Galadriel in The Lord of the Rings franchise, though it grossed $2.9 billion at the worldwide box office.

“Are you kidding me? No,” she retorted on Watch What Happens Live in August 2024 when Andy Cohen guessed LOTR was her biggest paycheck. “No one got paid anything to do that movie…I mean, I basically got free sandwiches, and I got to keep my ears.”

They weren’t paid a precious amount, that is. Orlando Bloom, who played elven warrior Legolas, shared on The Howard Stern Show in 2023 that he made $175,000 for the whole trilogy.

#3769. Listen to Two New Kacey Musgraves Songs

With little warning, Kacey Musgraves has revealed the existence of an entire companion album to her new LP Middle of Nowhere. Billed as Middle of Nowhere Vol. 2: Tex Mex Tapes, the 16-track release consists of 11 new songs and five covers, including a Spanish rendition of “Uncertain, TX” from the original record. It’s out January 15 via Lost Highway. You can hear the first two songs from the project, “Weekend in Texas” and her version of the norteño standard “Mi Tesoro,” below.

Middle of Nowhere was led by the single “Dry Spell” and its title track. Since its May release, Musgraves has also shared a video for “Mexico Honey.” She’s currently winding down her North American tour in support of the album, which wraps up with two shows at Climate Pledge Arena in Seattle on October 26 and 27.

#3768. Busy Philipps' New Show Has 'Girls5Eva' Reunion With Renee Elise Goldsberry

Busy Philipps‘ new show, Cupertino, is staging a reunion with Renée Elise Goldsberry two years after Girls5Eva was canceled — and Us has the first look.

In an exclusive sneak peek at the Thursday, October 29, episode, Natalia (Philipps) and Nichole (Goldsberry) spend quality time at the firm while Michael (Mike Colter), Olivia (Rachel Keller) and Christy (Ella Stiller) are away.

Natalia, who is boxing, discusses Bo (Rob McClure), who Nichole claims is the property of the company she works for.

“If you lost track of your IP it is your fault, not ours,” Natalia replies before Nichole suggests they “move this conversation to court.”

According to the official episode synopsis, Michael and Olivia “defend the young founders of a prediction market site, one of whom has been arrested while the other flees on his private jet with Christy in tow after a night out together.”

Philipps, 47, and Goldsberry, 55, previously shared the screen on Girls5eva, which aired on Peacock from 2021 to 2022. The musical comedy then moved to Netflix and released a third season before the show officially came to an end.

Cupertino, meanwhile, marks Philipps’ return to TV after she underwent surgery for a brain tumor in early March. The show stars Colter as a Silicon Valley attorney who, after being fired by his employer, forms a firm with another laid off lawyer (Keller) to represent people harmed by the technology industry.

For Philipps, the show’s focus on questioning the use of AI hit close to home.

“I’m not an AI user. We don’t use that in my home. I actually am obsessed with my daughter’s eighth grade — and with the entire school. They are hand writing all of their assignments this year,” Philipps, who shares two kids with estranged husband Marc Silverstein, told Us. “They’re not using computers at all in response to the proliferation of [AI] — and I’m sure that kids will cheat but she’s not.”

Phillips continued: “And this has really reconfirmed my belief that billionaires should not exist and that we need to have regulations in place. And also I just trust no one.”

The actress said working on the show has taught her some important lessons.

“My absolute favorite thing to watch — and now my favorite thing to play — is a woman who becomes empowered after the age of 40 and finds her own voice,” Phillips exclusively told Us earlier this month. “That’s what I’m getting to do on this show. It shows that you can have a second act as a woman. Life doesn’t end just because your marriage does.”

Cupertino airs on CBS Thursdays at 10 p.m. ET before streaming the next day on Paramount+.

#3767. This Star Trek Actor's Canceled Sci-Fi Show Has A Terrifying Premise

The ABC mystery-box sci-fi series "FlashForward," based on the 1999 novel by Robert J. Sawyer, was co-created by "Star Trek" luminary Brannon Braga (who briefly quit the franchise after "Star Trek: Voyager") and celebrated superhero scribe David S. Goyer, and, boy howdy, does it have an intriguing premise: On October 6, 2009, every single person on Earth mysteriously faints and stays unconscious for exactly two minutes and 17 seconds. When everyone comes to, they have all had visions of their own futures, specifically what they will be doing on April 29, 2010, about six months from the present. Too bad the short-lived show couldn't foresee its own fate.

John Cho (himself a "Star Trek" veteran who joined the property without even reading a script) and Joseph Fiennes star in "FlashForward" as Demetri Noh and Mark Benford, two partnered FBI Special Agents who are assigned to find out what caused the bizarre flash-forward. Half of the series is devoted to untangling the cosmic mystery, while the other half consists of all the personal drama that arise from the characters knowing what will happen to them in the near future. Special Agent Janis Hawk (Christine Woods), for instance, will have a vision of getting a pregnancy sonogram, despite her being single at the time of the blackout. Meanwhile, Dr. Olivia Benford (Sonya Walger) sees herself having a romantic evening with a man who is not her husband.

Most chillingly, Noh's flash-forward reveals nothing at all, making him suspect that he is destined to die within the next six months. As for Benford, he sees himself investigating the flash-forward event when men with guns burst into his office.

The setup for "FlashForward" is perfect for manufacturing personal drama, has some philosophical sci-fi intrigue (Can you change the future?), and contains an ineffable mystery to be solved. But in spite of its terrifying ideas and opportunities for mystery and drama, the show just didn't fare well enough ratings-wise to last beyond a single 22-episode season. In the end, it proved to be one of many "Lost" imitators that came and went in the wake of that ABC series' massive success.

Like "Lost," the mystery in "FlashForward" gets pretty convoluted pretty quickly. Dominic Monaghan and Jack Davenport co-star as Dr. Simon Campos and Dr. Lloyd Simcoe (respectively), two individuals who may have caused the blackout or possibly just enhanced its effects. In addition, at least one character is in possession of a high-tech ring that allowed them to stay awake during the blackout, and there is, perhaps predictably, a shadowy cabal of government agents who seek to control any future blackouts. Indeed, the FBI is searching for an enigmatic individual nicknamed Suspect Zero, as well as D. Gibbons or Dyson Frost, a figure who definitely has some kind of arcane knowledge that adds to the mysteries.

"FlashForward" is dripping with conspiracies, so it's difficult to talk about without giving away its many secrets. For me, though, the most interesting aspect of the series is its constant pondering of predetermination. Philosophically, "FlashForward" is all potential energy and, in a refreshing twist, not all of the flash-forwards are intriguing. For example, FBI Assistant Director Stanford Wedeck (Courtney B. Vance) merely sees himself merely sitting on a toilet reading a newspaper. It seems to me that his would be the more common experience.

"FlashForward," in addition to being a little too convoluted, was also overpopulated. Yes, it was a global series, and everyone on the planet was wrestling with the same questions of destiny, but the main cast included at least 10 characters, which was a lot of ground to cover. On top of that, the show snagged a huge roster of captivating guest stars, which only made things more complex. Pay attention, though, and you'll be impressed by visits from the likes of Michael Ealy, Gabrielle Union, Annabeth Gish, and the late, great Ricky Jay.

Structurally, the first and only season of "FlashForward" was presented in two parts. Part 1 ran from September to December 2009, while Part 2 aired from March to May 2010. The series cleverly butted up against its own future.

Critically, "FlashForward" received generally good reviews. Writing for Salon, Heather Havrilesky listed it as the best of that year's fall TV, comparing it favorably to shows like "Lost" and "Fringe." As she put it:

"You have to love a fall pilot that can incite paranoid thoughts within minutes. And who doesn't enjoy the eerie mood and the sense of mystery and doom built into this picture? No one has any idea what's going on. And in contrast to the limited scope of similar serial dramas, this phenomenon affects everyone on the planet."

That said, Havrilesy admitted that the "FlashForward" pilot is "wildly cheesy, cheesy in ways that only an aggressively slick, suspenseful heir to '24's' throne can be." Similarly, many critics loved the show's premise but tended to agree that its plotting is weak. At the very least, everything pays off when the series catches up to the forward flashes.

→ Baca artikel asli

#3766. What to Wear When Exercising Outdoors, Based on How Cold It Is

I fell in love with cold weather running while I was training for a spring marathon. It wasn’t a choice at first: Some weeks, the weather was bad for days on end, and if I stayed home, I would fall behind in my training. Everybody else in my training group was braving the elements, so I did too—with no regrets.

I got to run on quiet mornings while snow was softly falling. I signed up for a winter race that herded finishers into a heated barn for hot chocolate and soup. I set a personal record at a spring race, during which I pushed myself hard without breaking a sweat. I now find it sad when somebody tells me they’re afraid of running in the cold, or that they would run in the winter but don’t know what to wear.

No, cold weather doesn’t have to stop you from running, but getting dressed can be a puzzle: How do you keep from freezing your butt off at first and avoid overheating once you start to sweat? Fortunately, it isn’t as tough as it looks. With the right gear, your fingers and ears can stay toasty while you jog through a winter wonderland. Below, I’ll give you a temperature-by-temperature running cheat sheet, and then dive into the specifics on how to buy the best socks, shirts, and more.

When I get dressed to run in winter, I take a look at the “feels like” temperature, not the actual high or low. I also make sure to check the weather for the time I’ll go running, not for the whole day. It doesn’t matter how warm it will be at 2 p.m. if my run will be happening at 6 a.m., before the sun is up. I use the Carrot Weather app, but any app that gives you hourly feels-like temps will work just fine.

Next, use the 20-degree rule. Imagine that the temperature is 20 degrees (Fahrenheit) warmer than it really is. What would you wear then? Here's an example: if it’s 50 degrees out, you should dress as if it were 70: in a T-shirt and shorts, most likely. If it’s 30 degrees, you’d dress as if it were 50: long pants and maybe a light jacket. Get the idea?

Everybody has their own preferences. Some people wear shorts even when it’s snowing, while others will pop a beanie on as soon as there’s a detectable chill in the air. I’m a pretty middle-of-the-road runner when it comes to keeping warm, so here’s what I wear at each “feels like” temperature, starting with warmer weather and working our way colder. I'll give a guide to each type of garment or accessory at the end.

At 50+ degrees Fahrenheit, I’m wearing basically the same things I would for a summer run.

Head and hands: Nothing special. No gloves and no hat, unless it’s a baseball cap to shade my eyes from the sun.

We’re starting to feel the first bit of a chill. This is where I may start bringing gloves, although they’ll probably get stuffed into a pocket by the end of the run. Remember, with the 20-degree rule, it will feel like 60 degrees by the time you're warmed up.

Bottoms: Leggings, lightweight long pants, or shorts.

Top: Something with long sleeves, but ideally made of thin material (think long-sleeved T-shirt, not a full-on sweatshirt or jacket). If you wore a sun shirt in the summer, that's a good layering option for these cool temps. I like my REI Sahara Shade hoodie here, but any long sleeve will do. If you run fall and winter races, you'll probably be handed a free long-sleeved version at some point.

Hands: I usually just pull my shirt's sleeves over my hands, but you could wear knit gloves if you want. Consider something like Flipmits for an intermediate option (they can be folded down into a wrist cuff when your hands warm up).

Head: A headband if you need protection for your ears.

You may see a few snowflakes drifting down, but don’t bundle up in a snowsuit. Once you’re warmed up, it will only feel like 50 degrees—so go for something in “light jacket” territory.

Bottoms: Leggings or running tights. The fleece-lined kind can be nice. (I have these.)

Top: You’ll want some kind of jacket or sweatshirt. A lightweight, reflective rain jacket is great here, or just throw on a regular hoodie over your T-shirt. (I go with cotton sweats in dry weather or light snow, but I'd go for synthetics or wool if there's rain or sleet.)

Hands: Definitely wear gloves. The cheap knit ones are usually good enough, but consider treating yourself to a nice pair of running gloves.

Head: Your choice of hat or headband; I’d stick with the headband.

Feet: This is around the temperature you may start to want warmer socks. That goes double if snow is in play: snow that falls onto your shoes will work its way through the mesh, melt, and make your feet wet. Wool is a good material here, so consider something like these from Smartwool.

This is where I’ll officially say it’s “cold.” Whatever warm-weather gear you were getting away with at warmer temps, you’ll probably have to swap it out now. Shorts people will break out their long pants. Headband people will wear a real hat.

Bottoms: Leggings and shorts. I don’t care if you think it looks goofy to wear both; this is an A+ hack. Or try a puffer skirt.

Top: It’s definitely jacket weather. Rather than getting a warmer jacket, I like to wear the same light jacket as I did at 30 degrees, but this time with a long-sleeved fleece or wool base layer underneath.

Hands: Real gloves, please. You can keep wearing your little knit gloves if you’re able to tuck your hands inside your jacket sleeves, or if you have a pair of mittens to wear over top; otherwise, get windproof gloves.

Head: A hat, and maybe also a buff for your nose and mouth. (If you have any cloth masks still hanging around from the early COVID days, they’re perfect for cold weather runs.)

Feet: Warm socks for sure. If you’ll be doing a lot of running in these temperatures, you may want shoes that are specialized for the elements. Look for ones with Gore-Tex uppers, like the Brooks Ghost GTX.

This is where I tap out. If a run is just barely in the teens, I’ll make do with the 20-degree items I mentioned above, adding an extra layer wherever needed. But if that isn't enough, I opt for the treadmill. You certainly can run in this weather if you are determined. You’ll want windproof everything, some kind of face covering, and you may need eye protection such as goggles.

Whenever the weather gets to your own personal “too cold” temperature, remember that you still have options. The early afternoon is often the warmest part of the day, so a run may be tolerable then. There’s also no shame in using a treadmill (even if you have to join a cheap gym for this reason alone).

Everybody has different preferences, and with practice you’ll figure out if you’re the kind of person who wears three shirts before it hits freezing or the type to run through the snow in shorts. But this should give you a starting point when you head out for your first (or next) cold weather run.

As you continue on your cold-weather running journey, you may need to buy some new gear or repurpose things from elsewhere in your closet. And yes, the first few minutes of your run will feel chilly. But if you’re wearing the right shoes and clothes (and planning routes with a few extra factors in mind, which I’ll discuss below), winter running is not just feasible; it’s actually fun. So here are the items you may want to consider.

Your feet need to be warm and, if possible, dry. Warm socks go without saying, but there are some extra considerations when you’re picking socks to run in. Wool socks are ideal because they keep your feet warm even when they get wet. You may not think of winter as a wet time, but any snow that meets up with your body heat will turn into water. If you don’t have wool socks, synthetic wicking fabrics are a good second choice. Cotton is a no-no

First, the socks have to be able to fit into your shoes. If you’ve been running in super thin cotton or synthetic socks all summer, thick socks may not fit. Smartwool run socks are available in a thin version that can fit no problem—but in colder weather you’ll want some thickness for extra warmth. That thickness, in turn, may mean that your winter running shoes need to be roomier than the shoes you wear in summer. If you’re buying a new pair, make sure to try them on with the type of socks you intend to run in.

If your warm socks fit, you can make do with the same shoes you wore in the summer. Your feet will stay dry if the weather is dry and you stay out of puddles. If you encounter snow and slush, though, your feet will have to endure warm-but-wet conditions for the rest of your run. That can be okay for short runs, but for serious winter running, consider upgrading your shoes.

Some running shoes are available with water-resistant uppers. This level of water resistance will keep stray snowflakes out of your socks, but it isn’t reliable if you’ll be stepping in slush puddles. In that case, you’ll want something more waterproof, like Gore-Tex shoes, many of which are made for running trails.

If you expect to run on a lot of ice or packed snow, especially on trails, spikes can give you a little extra grip.

Yaktrax makes a rubber harness that stretches to fit your shoe, with steel coils and small spikes, for about $40.

Microspikes are a notch up from there, costing around $90 for what look like a miniature version of the chains you’d put on car tires.

And if you’re really dedicated—and are seeking a purpose-built pair of snow shoes—you can attach individual spikes: either these traction spikes from IceSpike, or via a DIY approach using sheet metal screws. (There’s a tutorial here.)

The versions that include rubber, like Yaktrax, can wear out quickly on pavement, so it’s best to save these for runs on trails or on unplowed paths. You’ll also want to be careful about the fit. I tried a pair that were similar to Yaktrax, but they were a tad too big. Shortly into my run, I found that the rubber stretched enough to pull the front set of spikes up over the top of my shoe. I've since gotten better-fitting ones, and they work great.

Spikes and specialized shoes help you go more places, in worse weather—but remember that they’re not required to just head out on a chilly day. Regular shoes are fine if the weather is cold but dry, or if you’re willing to put up with a little moisture.

I actually love when the weather gets cold enough that I need a jacket, because then I get to wear something that gives me extra pockets! Stick with thin jackets that have some room inside. By the time temps dip into the 20s or below, you’ll be doing some serious layering: for example, an undershirt, a long-sleeved warm layer, and a jacket. A great option for that warm layer is a wool base layer like this one. (Don’t worry, it’s not itchy.) If you can’t wear wool, a synthetic fleece is a good alternative.

Before you run out to buy all new clothes, experiment with what’s in your closet. Try repurposing a thin sweater, for example. Some people would never wear cotton for running (it can be cold when wet), but I wear it as my undershirt layer and have lived to tell the tale. Just make sure to shower or change after your run, so you aren't sitting around in cold, wet clothes.

The jacket is the most important layer. You can grab a sweatshirt in a pinch, but to be really well-equipped for nasty weather, you’ll want a jacket that is windproof and water-resistant. Jackets made for running are lightweight, for layering. Consider ones that are visible at night, with bright colors and reflective areas, and that come with zippered pockets.

Once it’s too cold for shorts, you’ll want to start layering your bottoms, too. Loose pants let cold air in, which is why leggings or “running tights” like these are standard winter wear for all genders. Running tights come in different thicknesses; go for something fleece-lined for your coldest runs, or layer two pairs.

You need to keep your ears warm, but don’t reach for a winter hat right away: an ear warmer or headband can cover your ears while still letting heat escape from the top of your head. And if anything is falling from the sky (say, snow), a baseball cap will keep it out of your eyes.

Fleece or knit hats are great for cold enough days, of course. The dividing line between earwarmer weather and hat weather is a personal one, and depends on whether you’re overheating. If you’re wearing a hat but sweating into your base layers, the hat is probably overkill.

Gloves, however, are essential. I keep a pair of cheap knit gloves in my jacket pockets, but windy or very cold days call for something stronger. You can layer gloves; I find gloves under mittens work well. You can also get windproof insulated gloves, which are warmer than any number of layers of holey knit gloves.

Your face will freeze on windy or very cold days, so this is where your old stash of pandemic face masks really shines. Consider a balaclava or even a fleece-lined bandana. A neck gaiter stays on better than a scarf if your neck is cold. At this point, every inch of you is covered but your eyes. Sunglasses can take care of that.

It’s that time of the year again for a new Sexiest Man Alive to be announced by People magazine. The publication historically keeps the identity of the latest recipient heavily under wraps and, this week, fans now know who holds the coveted title for 2026. Noah Wyle is officially this year’s SMA, and it marks yet another notch in the fan-favorite actor’s professional belt. Unsurprisingly, fans are sounding off on social media, and I’m chuckling over how they’re referencing Wyle’s hit show, The Pitt.

Wyle’s Sexiest Man Alive status was officially unveiled on Tuesday morning, and People signified the occasion by sharing its exclusive interview with Wyle about his new title. The veteran actor seems flattered by the moniker, and he also confirmed that he accepted in great part due to his wife, Sara Wells. The Emmy winner said Wells was “amused” by the whole situation and, based on the comments of the Instagram post, she’s not the only one. Check out the love Wyle received from fans of The Pitt (and ER):

In taking on this title, Wyle now joins the ranks of past recipients like Denzel Washington, Brad Pitt, Michael B. Jordan and Chris Evans. The 2025 winner of the title was Jonathan Bailey, who notably became the first openly gay SMA honoree. While plenty of people now seem to be pleased with Wyle holding the title, some fans had other thoughts. A few of those people named another member of The Pitt’s cast as someone who was deserving of the title:

Night Shift Lovers, Noah Wyle Revealed A Detail About The Pitt Season 3 You Need To Pay Attention To

There are plenty of fans who love Wyle’s Dr. Robby Robinavitch, and for good reason. However, Shawn Hatosy’s Dr. Jack Abbot has also emerged as one of the show’s breakouts, and Hatosy’s own star notoriety has been rising as well. At this point, Hatosy has even become something of a sex symbol, and the actor has since said that he’s happy to “embrace” that status. While it wouldn’t have been unreasonable for Hatosy to have been named SMA, I’m not mad at Wyle earning it.

On an ER-related note, it’s also worth mentioning that Wyle now has something else in common with his old co-star, George Clooney. Still known for having played Dr. Doug Ross on NBC’s hit medical drama, Clooney was named Sexiest Man Alive in 1997 and 2006. The two former co-stars still like to troll each other (and that was the case when Wyle joked about Clooney appearing on The Pitt). With that jokey relationship in mind, another Instagram user said this about Wyle’s title:

George Clooney will never let him live this down….

I don’t know about any of you, but I’m definitely hoping Clooney will weigh in on his old buddy’s Sexiest Man Alive title sometime soon. For now, though, congratulations are definitely in order for Noah Wyle and, amid the funny comments, he seems to be taking all of this in stride. Of course, fans can check out the new SMA on both seasons of The Pitt, which is streamable with an HBO Max subscription, and look out for Season 3, which will premiere in January 2027.
