#3764. SunCoke Energy (SXC) Q3 2026 Earnings Call Transcript

Need a quote from a Motley Fool analyst? Email [email protected]

SunCoke Energy, Inc. (SXC +7.95%) reported third quarter results that exceeded internal expectations, leading management to raise its full-year free cash flow guidance and project earnings above the high end of previous ranges. Management reported that the company reached a confidential settlement with Algoma Steel regarding its 2025 claim, which contributed to the improved cash flow outlook. The company stated that it focused on a balanced capital allocation strategy during the period, prioritizing debt reduction through revolver repayments while maintaining its multiyear dividend program. Operational results were supported by higher handling volumes in the Industrial Services segment and a favorable sales mix in Domestic Coke.

Operator: Thank you for standing by. My name is Jael, and I will be your conference operator today. At this time, I would like to welcome everyone to SunCoke Energy's third quarter 2026 earnings call. All lines have been placed on mute to prevent any background noise. After the speaker's remarks, there will be a question and answer session. If you would like to ask a question during this time, simply press star followed by the number one on your telephone keypad. If you would like to withdraw your question, simply press star one again. We would now like to turn the conference over to Sharon Doyle, Investor Relations Manager. You may begin.

Sharon Doyle: Thanks, Jael. Good morning, and thank you for joining us to discuss SunCoke Energy's third quarter 2026 results. With me today are Katherine Gates, President and Chief Executive Officer, and Shantanu Agrawal, Senior Vice President and Chief Financial Officer. This conference call is being webcast live on the investor relations section of our website, and a replay will be available later today. Following management's prepared remarks, we will open the call for Q&A. If we do not get to your questions on the call today, please feel free to reach out to our investor relations team.

Before I turn things over to Katherine, let me remind you that the various remarks we make on today's call regarding future expectations constitute forward-looking statements. The cautionary language regarding forward-looking statements in our SEC filings apply to the remarks we make today. These documents are available on our website as are reconciliations to non-GAAP financial measures discussed on today's call. With that, I'll now turn things over to Katherine.

Katherine Gates: Thanks, Sharon. Good morning, and thank you for joining us on today's call. This morning, we announced SunCoke Energy's third quarter results. I want to share a few highlights before turning it over to Shantanu to discuss the results in detail. We are extremely pleased with our third quarter results, with strong consolidated Adjusted EBITDA of $76.9 million. Both of our operating segments delivered excellent performance. Domestic Coke benefited from a favorable mix of contract and spot coke sales and favorable coal-to-coke yields, while Industrial Services benefited from one additional month of Phoenix results compared with the prior year period and higher terminals handling volumes.

We also announced this morning that we reached a settlement agreement related to our 2025 claim against Algoma Steel. The agreement was executed in October, and we can confirm that we were made whole. Turning to capital allocation. Earlier today, we also announced a quarterly dividend of $0.12 per share payable to shareholders on December 1st, 2026. This is our 29th consecutive quarterly dividend. While the dividend is evaluated on a quarterly basis by our board, we expect the dividend to continue as part of our well-balanced capital allocation strategy. Our operating segments continue to perform strongly and consistently.

Based on our year-to-date results and outlook for the remainder of the year, we expect to modestly exceed the -high end of our full-year 2026 consolidated Adjusted EBITDA guidance range of $250 million to $265 million. With that, I will turn it over to Shantanu to review our third quarter earnings in detail. Shantanu.

Shantanu Agrawal: Thanks, Katherine. Turning to slide four. Net income attributable to SunCoke was $0.25 per share in the third quarter of 2026, down $0.01 from the prior year period. The benefits from our operating performance and the insurance recoveries from the 2023 Jewell flood event were offset by the absence of capital investment tax credits recorded in the third quarter of 2025. Excluding that item, the underlying earnings performance improved materially. While the insurance recovery was a substantial benefit to our net income and earnings per share during the quarter, the majority of the recovery proceeds were attributable to property damage and therefore excluded from Adjusted EBITDA.

Consolidated Adjusted EBITDA for the third quarter of 2026 was $76.9 million, an increase of $17.8 million from the prior year period. Domestic Coke increased by $7.1 million, primarily driven by a change in the mix of contract and spot coke sales and favorable coal-to-coke yields, partially offset by lower coke sales volumes due to Haverhill I shutdown. Industrial Services increased by $13.9 million, primarily driven by one additional month of Phoenix results compared with the third quarter of 2025 and higher terminal handling volumes. Corporate and other expenses increased by $3.2 million, driven by higher employee-related expenses. Moving to slide five to discuss our Domestic Coke business performance in detail.

Third quarter Domestic Coke Adjusted EBITDA was $51.1 million, and coke sales volumes were 875,000 tons compared to $44 million and 951,000 tons in the prior year period. The increase in Adjusted EBITDA was primarily driven by a change in the mix of contract and spot coke sales and favorable coal-to-coke yields, partially offset by lower coke sales volumes due to the Haverhill I shutdown. We are pleased with the excellent performance from our coke operations and with a solid outlook for the remainder of the year, we are well-positioned to achieve the high-end of our full-year 2026 Domestic Coke Adjusted EBITDA guidance range of $172 million to $178 million.

Now moving on to slide six to discuss our Industrial Services results. Our Industrial Services segment generated $32.1 million of Adjusted EBITDA in the third quarter of 2026, compared with $18.2 million in the prior year period. The increase primarily reflects one additional month of Phoenix results compared with the third quarter of 2025 and higher terminals handling volumes. Third quarter terminals handling volumes were 6.4 million tons and steel customer volume serviced were 5.9 million tons. Our Industrial Services segment continues to perform very well, and we now expect to exceed the high-end of our full-year 2026 Industrial Services Adjusted EBITDA guidance range of $110 million-$115 million. Now turning to slide seven to discuss our liquidity position for Q3.

SunCoke ended the third quarter with ample liquidity of approximately $276 million. Gross leverage was 2.34 times on a trailing 12-month Adjusted EBITDA basis. Net cash provided by operating activities during the third quarter was $94.8 million. We used $53.5 million to pay down our revolver balance, spent $20.9 million on capital expenditures, and paid $10.1 million in dividends at the rate of $0.12 per share. We also received $6.9 million of insurance recoveries related to the 2023 Jewell flood event. SunCoke has a strong track record of generating steady free cash flow, and we expect the trend to continue through the balance of the year.

Year-to-date, we have reduced debt substantially while simultaneously increasing our free cash flow outlook to $180 million-$185 million, which reflects the impact of the settlement agreement with Algoma. As Katherine mentioned earlier, we intend to continue utilizing our free cash flow to pay down debt, as well as to reward our long-term shareholders via dividends, which is reviewed and approved on a quarterly basis by our board of directors. With that, I will turn it back over to Kat.

Katherine Gates: Thanks, Shantanu. Wrapping up on slide eight. As always, safety is our first priority, and our team remains committed to maintaining strong safety and environmental performance throughout the year. Robust safety and environmental standards set SunCoke apart and are central to our reliable delivery of high-quality coke and Industrial Services. As we look ahead, we continue to be confident in all aspects of our business. Our long-term coke business is underpinned by the three pillars of Indiana Harbor, Middletown, and Jewell Foundry, which have consistently delivered excellent performance and results. With our Haverhill II and Granite City cokemaking contracts in place and all spot blast and foundry coke sales finalized, we are sold out for the full-year.

Our Industrial Services segment is also well-positioned going forward. Phoenix is performing as we expected, and 2026 will benefit from a full-year of Phoenix Adjusted EBITDA. We also see solid market conditions at our terminals and are focused on growing both our Phoenix and logistics businesses. As always, we take a balanced yet opportunistic approach to capital allocation. Our focus will remain on utilizing our free cash flow to support our capital allocation priorities, including paying down our revolver balance. We plan to continue returning capital via the quarterly dividend approved by our board, which has always been well-received by our long-term shareholders. Our focus is also on profitable growth opportunities to further reward our long-term shareholders.

This commitment to maximizing value for all stakeholders means operating and investing in our assets in the best and most efficient way possible to support our customers and to further grow the business. Based on our strong year-to-date performance and outlook for the remainder of the year, we now expect full-year consolidated Adjusted EBITDA to modestly exceed the high end of our guidance range of $250 million-$265 million. In addition, we are increasing our free cash flow guidance to a range of $180 million-$185 million. Let's go ahead and open up the call for Q&A.

Operator: Thank you. The floor is now open for questions. If you have dialed in and would like to ask a question, please press star one on your telephone keypad to raise your hand and join the queue. If you would like to withdraw your question, simply press star one again. If you're called upon to ask a question and are listening via loudspeaker on your device, please pick up your handset and ensure that your phone is not on mute when asking your question. Your first question comes from the line of Nathan Martin of StoneX Financial. Your line is open.

Nathan Martin: Thanks, operator. Good morning, everyone. Can we get some more details on the settlement agreement with Algoma that was reached this month? Katherine, I know you said you were made whole, but what was the amount, and how will that impact the fourth quarter results?

Katherine Gates: Thanks for the question. You can appreciate that this is a confidential settlement, and beyond that, we don't talk about the economics of specific customers or specific contracts. What I can say is that we're really pleased with the resolution of the matter. We were made whole, and the impact of the relationship that we have with Algoma as it related to both the steps that we took to mitigate the effect of the contract rejection and the settlement itself is totally reflected in the guidance for 2026.

Nathan Martin: Okay. Any of the previous kind of working capital headwinds you've talked about there, all that has been reflected now that you have the settlement in place?

Nathan Martin: Okay. Got it. Then somewhat related, does SunCoke still expect the insurance proceeds from the turbine failure at Middletown to arrive sometime here in the fourth quarter?

Katherine Gates: Great question. We're obviously really pleased that the turbine returned to service in May and power resumed and everything is working just as we expected. The claims process for the turbine may not be complete at the end of 2026. This may go into early 2027. As a result, the insurance recovery for the turbine is not included in our guidance. If we receive payment for the claim in 2026, we will significantly exceed the high end of our guidance range.

Nathan Martin: Okay. Got it. Because previously it was, I think, included in your guidance range. Is it almost the case that maybe the Jewell proceeds you got there have kind of offset potentially the proceeds you originally expected from Middletown?

Shantanu Agrawal: No. Nate, on that, as I mentioned, the Jewell proceeds, a significant chunk of that is excluded from Adjusted EBITDA. We received total $8.7 million from the Jewell flood event. Only $1.8 million of that in Adjusted EBITDA. The remainder, $6.9 million, as I mentioned, is running through the net income, but it is not included in Adjusted EBITDA. The increase in the guidance as we are guiding towards modestly exceeding the consolidated Adjusted EBITDA guidance, that is driven by the performance along with the Algoma settlement.

Nathan Martin: Okay. So that Algoma settlement is basically potentially going to offset any delay in the insurance proceeds from the turbine failure.

Shantanu Agrawal: That is kind of what is contemplated in the guidance.

Nathan Martin: Okay. Got it. That's helpful, Shantanu. Appreciate that. Maybe just looking at the Industrial Services segment. Great quarter there. I think last quarter we talked about how even the new higher full-year guidance still kind of assumed a slowdown in the second half versus the first half. I think you said levels probably look more like the first quarter levels. Clearly, volumes for both the terminals business and Phoenix remained elevated. Now I guess you're expecting to exceed the high-end of the full-year guidance range. Can you help us frame the magnitude there? Is it reasonable to expect fourth quarter Adjusted EBITDA looks similar again to the third quarter, just given strong demand?

We've got higher export thermal volumes and prices as well. Just how should we think about that? Thanks.

Shantanu Agrawal: So great question, Nate, on that. Again, as we have talked about, the Industrial Services segment is the segment where there is the most volatility in our business, right? If the steel mills are running really hard and if there is a lot of bulk products moving through the rivers, bulk product moving out of the U.S., you see additional volumes and additional profitability come through that. As we think about Q4, what is in our guidance today, both our full-year volume guidance for the terminals as well as for Phoenix, we expect to exceed the 24 million and the 22 million that we laid out at the start of the year, right? That's a given.

Now to your question of what is the magnitude of Q4, again, there is a little bit of uncertainty, but the expectation is as we go into the holiday period of Thanksgiving and Christmas, the steel mills are going to slow down. They shut down their volumes a little bit. Same thing on the little bit on the terminal side, not to that extent, but you see that a little bit in the Q4 where the volumes are a little bit lighter than Q2 or Q3, right?

If you want to think about what Q4 looks like, I would say somewhere closer to Q1 or in between the Q1 and the Q3 range would be my expectation for Industrial Services segment in Q4.

Nathan Martin: Okay. Got it, Shantanu. Just to confirm, I'm assuming just given where export thermal coal prices are today that you guys are expecting you should receive the price kicker there at Convent?

Shantanu Agrawal: Yeah. If you recall, we changed our kicker. Instead of being an export coal-driven, it is more the New Orleans price-driven. Now that price has come up as well, given what is happening in the U.S. export market. The coal is moving. So we are getting the kicker, but it is a different kicker versus what it was in the previous years.

Nathan Martin: Sure. Right. Yeah, I recall. Okay, I will leave it there. Pass it along. Appreciate the time and best of luck in the fourth quarter.

Operator: Your next question comes from the line of Henry Hearle of B. Riley Securities. Your line is open.

Henry Hearle: Good morning everyone. I guess just on the Domestic Coke business, Adjusted EBITDA per ton came in at $58.40, which is your strongest in recent quarters. How would you attribute the improvement between the contract and spot sales mix shift and also the coal-to-coke yield benefit?

Shantanu Agrawal: Henry, thanks for the question. There are timing differences when you move quarter-to-quarter where the mix between what you're shipping in a quarter, what is contract versus what is spot changes. In Q3, obviously there was a bigger portion of contract coke going out, versus the spot coke, and that's why you see the Adjusted EBITDA per ton being higher. As per coal-to-coke yields, that has some seasonality in it, where Q1 and Q4 being our cold and wet quarters, you historically see higher yields in Q2 and Q3 versus Q1 and Q4.

I would say both of these factors kind of played into it, but obviously the contract versus spot coke mix is a bigger factor versus the yield if you're comparing previous year quarter to this year quarter.

Henry Hearle: Got it. Thanks for that, Shantanu. Looking towards 2027, I believe you have a couple contracts expiring in December this year, 2026. Are you confident that those will be extended going forward? Any color there would be great. Thanks.

Katherine Gates: We are in active discussions. If you are thinking about the Javelin contract at CMT and the contract with U.S. Steel Nippon at Granite City Works, we are in active discussions with both of those parties and expect to move forward with them in 2027. Obviously, we cannot say more at this point in time, but overall, if you are thinking about 2027, we expect to have a strong finish here to 2026, and Shantanu has sort of walked through some of the nuances of that. Q4 being a bit softer, but overall, very strong finish to 2026 and we have good momentum going into 2027.

Henry Hearle: Understood, Katherine. Thanks for that color. I think I will leave it there. Continue. Best of luck, guys.

Operator: Thank you. With no further questions, that concludes our Q&A session. I will now turn the conference back over to Katherine Gates, President and CEO, for closing remarks.

Katherine Gates: Thank you all for joining us today and for your continued interest in SunCoke. Let's continue to work safely today and every day. Thank you, guys.

Operator: This concludes today's conference call. You may now disconnect.

#3763. Annie Meyers-Shyer and the Secrets of the Nancy Meyers Aesthetic

Annie Meyers-Shyer was taking a bath when she made This Oak House. She already had an Instagram—a private, personal Instagram. Recently, however, she found herself wanting to share more. Not photos of herself. (God no.) But photos about her house.

She and her husband, lawyer Robby Koch, had just inherited his mother’s four-bedroom Beverly Hills home. Originally built in the 1940s by famed architect Gerard Colcord, the Colonial house was idyllic, but also very much in need of a renovation. And as anyone embarking on a renovation knows, it can be cathartic to talk about it. Plus, Meyers-Shyer had always been a visual creature: Before taking a professional break to care for her young children, she served as an accessories editor at Harper’s Bazaar and then as West Coast public relations manager at Chanel. “I put up a little thing and I said, ‘I created this account. Maybe I’m going to post, maybe I’m not. Come follow.’ That was the end of it.”

Spoiler alert: That was not the end of it. Three years later, This Oak House has more than 254,000 followers and counting—as well as a newly published book, This Oak House: A Renovation Story With Interiors to Inspire Your Own Home.

In this late-stage social media era, where Instagram is beyond saturated with influencers clamoring for likes, it’s difficult to build such a large following in a short amount of time. Yet it’s no surprise that Meyers-Shyer found her niche. For starters, she had zero interest in spon-con. “I wasn’t motivated in the way I think a lot of traditional influencers are. I was just sharing the process. People didn’t see me hawking products and things like that. I think there was an authenticity which people appreciated,” she says. Secondly? She’s a Meyers.

Annie Meyers-Shyer and her husband, Robby Koch's, home in Los Angeles. It inspired Meyers-Shyer's Instagram account, This Oak House.

Her mother is Nancy Meyers, the director behind films like The Holiday, Something’s Gotta Give, It’s Complicated, and The Parent Trap, and cowriter of Father of the Bride. They’re beloved by millions—both because of their delightful rom-com plots (who didn’t fall in love with Jude Law in The Holiday?) but also because of the worlds they create. Meyers’s movies are known for their cozy, quintessential California interiors, with bright, airy kitchens and gently sumptuous living rooms of white and cream. They’re perennially trending: In 2024, Pinterest announced that searches for “Nancy Meyers living room” were up 2,090%. In September, TikTok sent out a press blast announcing that searches for “Nancy Meyers Aesthetic” had recently risen 145%. “Nancy Meyers aesthetic is gaining traction on TikTok, with users embracing the cozy, nostalgic feel of her iconic films,” said a statement from the social media platform. Meyers-Shyer gets it. “I always found it interesting in her movies how the sets really further told the story of the character. You could learn so much more about the character by their home. What did the Father of the Bride house tell you about that family, or what did the in-laws’ house tell you about them? You could really learn. And so I think that’s what great houses do. They tell you about the person who lives inside of them.”

But here’s the thing. Nancy Meyers makes sets—temporary structures dismantled after a director yells, “That’s a wrap.” Meyers-Shyer, however, knows how to build the permanent thing. “I’m sure there are people who love my mom and her style and they came to me [because] she doesn’t really talk about that world that much.” And This Oak House gets down to the granular: There’s a whole page, for example, dedicated to the best paints. (Like Farrow & Ball Slipper Satin or Benjamin Moore Swiss Coffee.) Another section focuses on the best types of marble and the different types of wood.

Meyers-Shyer wasn’t looking to intentionally mimic her mom’s interiors. She emulates her mom in the way we’re all influenced by the way we grew up: Meyers brought her to markets in Provence, so she, too, fell in love with markets of Provence, and so on. Her parents, as many do, passed down some furniture and paintings. (“Every time I open the front door, I see it and it reminds me of my dad,” she says of one piece of art.)

This Oak House is half a how-to on how to achieve the elevated, cozy California style her mother Nancy Meyers often shows in her movies.

And it should be said the now famous Nancy Meyers aesthetic was influenced by someone else: Nancy’s mother (and Annie’s grandmother) who was an interior designer. “She was always refinishing something or moving furniture around,” Meyers-Shyer recalls. And her great-grandmother ran an antique store. Their decor taste has been passed down through the generations, descendant by descendant. “It’s a long lineage,” Meyers-Shyer says. So perhaps the “Nancy Meyers aesthetic” isn’t quite right: the “Meyers aesthetic” is.

Meyers-Shyer and I met up in West Hollywood, with a very specific agenda: Could she show me how one gets the Meyers aesthetic?

Not in a “do it for less” or “dupes” kind of way. Where does she shop? Where does her mom shop? What secret places—and people—make up the backbone of these magical California worlds the Meyerses create? (Look, if I was part of the Meyers female empire and the financial boon that came with it, I’d want the good stuff.)

Our first stop is Rose Tarlow House. Rose Tarlow is an IYKYK name—she might never have appeared on Million Dollar Decorators or had a line with Crate & Barrel, but she’s quietly one of the most influential names in interior decorating, designing the homes of David Geffen and Oprah Winfrey. (And not many more: Tarlow is known for keeping an extremely small client base.) “Tarlow has solidified her industry status as a true decorator’s decorator,” Architectural Digest says of her, and she is known for her use of wood and embrace of solid, neutral colors.

During Meyers-Shyer’s renovation, she used to spend hours at Rose Tarlow, skimming through the calm, earthy fabrics. (She selected Avignon Alpine Stripe Natural for her dining room curtains.) “She is the master. The absolute master,” says Meyers-Shyer. Last summer, Meyers-Shyer, her mother, and Tarlow went shopping in Provence. “Basically everything she pointed at and told me to get, I purchased,” she said.

It’s a short drive to Mike Moser’s studio on La Cienega. A minor character in This Oak House, Moser is Meyers-Shyer’s interior designer. She learned of him through her friend, Jamie Mizrahi, who styles celebrities like Adele, Jennifer Lawrence, and Jeremy Allen White. “What I loved about Jamie’s house was that it felt like Jamie, and that’s kind of Mike’s secret sauce,” she tells me as we meander through his space, which is half design office, half shop. Moser’s husband, Tyler Thomas, greets her warmly. They make small talk about their landscaping. Meyers-Shyer jokes that on a recent Ask Me Anything, she was asked to describe her landscape. “I wanted it to be like, ‘Pain in the ass, weedy, impossible to keep up,’” she says, laughing. “[I have to be] out there every day.” They then both ooh and ahh over a rare Warren McArthur sofa that Thomas and Moser have tracked down—their studio both designs furniture and sources it. In the coming weeks, they’ll be in Italy for a friend’s wedding. They’ll sneak in antique shopping when they are there. A lot of their stuff, however, they get at auctions. (As does Meyers-Shyer, who laughs when she tells me she has a terrible penchant for buying something on the cheap at a European auction—but then paying a small fortune in shipping. “The more difficult and expensive it is to get here, usually the better. I buy the item for $15,” she says.)

Her mom, meanwhile, is a client of Mark D. Sikes, known for his work redecorating the East Wing under first lady Jill Biden. “I think I hesitate to do color because I don’t know that I’d ultimately be happy with it,” Meyers told Architectural Digest of her light and creamy Los Angeles home. Sikes commended her attention to design details: “We may have looked at 50,000 different ivory linens.”

Then we’re off to Nickey Kehoe, the cult home store founded by Todd Nickey and Amy Kehoe. Nickey Kehoe sells things you didn’t even know you needed: a bronze bottle opener shaped like a mermaid, vintage steel lamps, a mirror with ceramic lemons by artist Charlie Dumais, and a 1950s red Chinois bar cabinet. “I love these chair paintings, by the way. This is from an artist in Provence whose work I just am crazy about,” Meyers-Shyer says as we pass a wall of oil paintings. She pauses by a collection of neutral welcome mats. “Do they shed?” she asks. “Once they shed, they shed.”

She points out the milky white ceramics of Astier de Villatte. “Almost every time I’m in here, I buy some little piece, like a little dish for my rings or a vase. I inherited a set of dishes from my father when he passed away,” she says.

Finally, we settle at Meg Turner Gallery. It’s a monastic, calm space, sparsely furnished with things like 19th-century Belgian console tables and beige cashmere blankets. Meyers-Shyer learned of it because it was one of the few (if not the only) places that sold John Julian silverware, which is based in rural Wiltshire, England. She loves John Julian, and wandered in one day to see it in person. Although today the shop assistant has something new for us to look at: Denis Colomb cashmere and camel hair blankets. I look at his website later: The throws retail from around $2,700 to $10,000 dollars. “I can see that she has Gustavian furniture with all these beautiful muted browns,” Meyers-Shyer says as she looks around, referring to the Swedish interpretation of 18th-century French neoclassical design. It’s a deep design reference—the type not taught in a rudimentary art history class but a master’s degree one—and shows just how much Meyers-Shyer has embedded herself in the design world.

“I realized I had a passion for something I didn’t know that I had, which is kind of cool at this age,” she says. Like great-grandmother, like grandmother, like mother, like daughter.

#3762. Ryan Gosling’s Band Dead Man’s Bones Tease Return

Dead Man’s Bones, the spectral rock duo of Ryan Gosling and screenwriter Zach Shields, are planning a comeback 17 years after their first and only album. Eva Mendes, Gosling’s wife, started teasing the return yesterday in a post designed to “summon new music from DEAD MAN’S BONES.” She followed it with two spooky visuals—one cross-posted with the new Dead Man’s Bones Instagram page—in which a voice ominously intones a spell to conjure the project for a twilight dance on October 27.

The original Dead Man’s Bones album was a 2009 collaboration with the Silverlake Conservatory Children’s Choir, a California education organization founded by Flea. The duo has lain dormant since its promotional run behind that LP, though one of its songs, “Pa Pa Power,” found its way onto Cat Power’s 2022 album Covers.

#3761. Kim Kardashian Reveals Grandma MJ’s Advice During Kris Humphries Split

Kim Kardashian revealed it was her grandmother MJ Shannon who gave her the most important piece of advice during her divorce from Kris Humphries: “You have to be happy.”

After Kim Kardashian ignored her cold feet, she got some important advice about listening to her gut.

While detailing her brief marriage to Kris Humphries to journalist Jonathan Van Meter for his book Citizen Kim: The Woman Who Created the Future, Kardashian revealed it was her late grandmother Mary Jo “MJ” Shannon who gave her the nudge she needed towards ending her union.

“My grandmom was like, ‘Listen. My parents saved up all their money so I could have a big wedding because I thought I was in love,’” Kardashian recalled of Shannon’s advice. “‘About a month or two in, I realized I’d made a terrible decision and I was so embarrassed. It was in the town paper! I just wasted everyone’s time and money and I was mortified.’”

Despite worrying that her parents would never speak to her again, Shannon, who died in July at 91, told Kardashian she was able to go through with her own split but just focusing on the fact she “knew” she had to get a divorce.

“And she said to me,” the SKIMS founder explained, “‘You have to be happy. You have to be.'”

Because as the reality TV mogul revealed, she knew before she and the NBA player signed onto what would prove to only be a 72-day marriage that the union was not going to be a slam dunk.

In fact, she told Van Meter she knew her and Humphries’ romance was over before they even said, “I do.”

“I just thought, ‘Well, it’s cold feet,’” the 46-year-old—who was married to Damon Thomas before Humphries and was subsequently married to Kanye “Ye” West for eight years—explained. “But your gut, your soul, is always right.”

But her internal feelings were warring with outside pressures.

“Then I thought, ‘Well, they’re filming for a show, and if I bail now, they’re going to call me “the runaway bride” and it will be an even bigger thing,’” Kardashian—who shares four kids with West—added. “But I should have just not gotten married.”

In fact, she wasn’t the only one who could tell the union was no match made in heaven—her mom Kris Jenner also tried to direct Kardashian to an exit sign.

“I knew she wasn’t happy,” Jenner told Van Meter, explaining she decided to talk to Kardashian during the rehearsal dinner. “I said to her, “Listen… I can just… I feel like… you’re not making the right decision.’ And I said, ‘If you want to cancel the wedding, just get out of here and I’ll figure it out.’”

But go through with it she did, ultimately pulling the plug less than three months later.

These days, however, the entrepreneur is happily racing off into the sunset with F1’s Lewis Hamilton. For a closer look at the Kardashian family's love lives, read on.

Why, yes, Kris Jenner and longtime love Corey Gamble are still doing amazing, sweetie. In 2014, the music industry insider approached the world's most famous momager at fashion designer Riccardo Tisci's 40th birthday bash in Ibiza and "I just tried saying, 'You're beautiful. I'd love to get to know you,'" he shared on the family's OG series, Keeping Up With the Kardashians, "and it went from there."

More than a decade in, Kris gushed about her "ride or die" in a 2025 birthday tribute, writing, "Thank you for all you do every single day, for the love you give, and for always being there for all of us. I’m so blessed to have you by my side."

With all due respect for all the small things, Kourtney Kardashian's husband has made some pretty grand gestures throughout their yearlong marriage. Take her 44th birthday that saw her and Travis Barker take a trip up the California coast to Santa Barbara. The Blink-182 drummer marked the occasion by filling their room at the famed Kennedy Cottage with rose petals and gifting her with a $161,819 piece by artist Yoshitomo Nara. The Poosh founder has fully embraced her new life as the girl at the rock show. And in November 2023, she and Travis welcomed their first child together, son Rocky Thirteen Barker.

As Kim Kardashian put it in The Kardashians trailer, "Things change really quickly." When season two wrapped, the SKIMS founder was still with then-Saturday Night Live standout Pete Davidson.

She got fans hoping she was getting back in the dating game with retired NFL quarterback Tom Brady.

Alas, the mom to North West, Saint West, Chicago West and Psalm West—with ex-husband Kanye West—wasn't on the hunt for a new teammate, a source telling E! News in May 2023, "They have friends and business partners in common."

By early 2026, she was racing into a new romance with Lewis Hamilton, hard-launching her relationship with the F1 driver that June.

After her divorce, she told The Times in October, "I wanted to completely heal and just work on myself. I wasn't ready to open up. I really wasn't even thinking about it. The universe works in your favor when you just allow it to."

In a 2023 episode, Kris shoots her shot while chatting with Khloe Kardashian about Tristan Thompson, asking, "How's everything going with Tristan?" Safe to say the Good American founder has the basketball player benched for now. Though the exes have worked out a game plan for raising daughter True and son Tatum, Khloe shot down the idea that they're back together in May 2023, calling the narrative "tiring."

She's more interested in starting a fresh story. After meeting Love Is Blind star Alexa Lemieux the following April, Khloe pitched a winning idea to Netflix, writing on Instagram, "I think some of us single K sisters should go on LIB. What do you think?"

Months after Kendall Jenner's split with NBA guard Devin Booker in fall 2022, the model's love life got an assist from friends Justin Bieber and Hailey Bieber. The married duo were spotted enjoying a double date with the reality star and musician Bad Bunny in February 2023. The following December, it was reported that the two had called it quits.

Then in the spring of 2026, fans started to wonder if a new romance was in bloom as Kendall was spotted hanging out with Jacob Elordi. The Euphoria alum was among those cheering for the model when she walked in Milan Fashion Week in September 2026.

Seems Kylie Jenner has found herself a new beautiful boy. Last seen publicly with rapper Travis Scott—dad to her daughter Stormi and son Aire—at an Art Basel party in December 2022, the beauty mogul, has first linked to Oscar-nominated actor Timothée Chalamet in April 2023.

Now, rise and shine because you don't want to miss the glimpses they've given us of their romance at New York Fashion Week and the US Open, as well as live on TV, when they showed PDA while seated together at the 2024 Golden Globes and 2025 Oscars.

Then, while accepting Best Actor at the 2026 Critics Choice Awards in January, he thanked his "partner of three years," for the foundation she provides, adding, "I couldn't do this without you. Thank you from the bottom of my heart."

#3760. Kim Kardashian Weighs In on Potential Run for Political Office: 'We Have Time'

Kim Kardashian cannot ignore the chatter from some people who speculate she may one day run for political office.

“People ask me all the time,” Kardashian, 46, told journalist Jonathan Van Meter in his new book Citizen Kim: The Women Who Created the Future. “I mean, the amount of people who wrote to me after [Joe] Biden’s disastrous debate? The fact that you guys think I’m capable of this? I am so flattered.”

Before she asks for anyone’s vote, however, Kardashian said she has some things on her to-do list that need to be accomplished first.

“I think that, No. 1, I have to pass the bar and become a lawyer first,” she continued. “And if you do run for office, you really can’t own and run a company. And I love Skims and what we’ve built. That is my focus.”

Skims was cofounded by Kardashian in June 2019 and has grown into a top-selling shapewear and clothing brand. The company is valued at approximately $5 billion and now includes shoes, slippers, bags, hair accessories and more.

“I definitely need some time,” Kardashian added. “But we have time.”

Perhaps one of those friends who wouldn’t be surprised if The Kardashians star entered political office is Van Jones.

A mentor and friend throughout Kardashian’s journey to becoming a lawyer, Jones, 58, predicts the businesswoman will one day open a law firm with a criminal justice reform pro bono aspect.

“And I’m sure it will be one of the most sought out and successful law firms in the country because she’s Kim Kardashian. She’s not going to not pass the bar ultimately. She’s not going to not become an attorney,” Jones said in Citizen Kim. “And then at some point, when you’re from the most famous family in the country, when you have the most famous name in the world and you’ve done great work, and now you’re not 41, but 51, or 61, at some point, somebody says, ‘How come you’re not a senator? How come you’re not the governor of California?’”

Kardashian and her inner circle were interviewed by Van Meter over the course of nearly 10 years for an in-depth biography called Citizen Kim.

When Kardashian asked Van Meter about the title of the book, he explained the significance.

“In this story, you are the mogul and the muse; you are the billionaire and the pretty young thing,” he wrote in the book’s prologue. “You don’t need a rich guy, because you’re the rich guy — Citizen Kim.”

Citizen Kim: The Woman Who Created the Future is available in stores Tuesday, October 27.

#3759. Elizabeth Holmes: Texas House Featured in 'You Can See Everything' For Sale

The Texas house where Elizabeth Holmes and her family were filmed in their final days together before her prison sentence in "You Can See Everything" is up for sale.

The 4-bedroom, 3-bathroom home hit the market for $2.25 million earlier this month ... and the real estate team trying to offload it is now playing up its appearance in the smash-hit documentary.

In case you haven't seen the Nathan Fielder and Lance Oppenheim joint yet ... it covers the month before Elizabeth's incarceration in federal prison. Most of that month is spent at a rented house in Del Mar, California ... though the family ultimately moved into this home in Austin, TX, to be closer to Federal Prison Camp Bryan in the Lone Star State.

The 2,483-square-foot home sits on a 7,100-square-foot lot ... and boasts a kitchen full of top-line appliances, panoramic views of the downtown skyline, a courtyard-style porch and more.

"You Can See Everything" has been a huge success both with audiences and critics. As of Monday, it has raked in $45 million at the box office and holds a 98% on Rotten Tomatoes.

It also inspired numerous look-alikes to storm Washington Square Park for a costume contest this weekend ... with both Elizabeth Holmes and Nathan Fielder doubles coming out to celebrate.

#3758. Decades Before Landman, Billy Bob Thornton Was In A Sitcom Featuring A TV Legend

Billy Bob Thornton might be 71 years old, but as his Paramount+ series "Landman" proves, he hasn't missed a step. Taylor Sheridan's oil drama has been the perfect showcase for Thornton's considerable talents, and despite the fact "Landman" has yet to earn any Emmy nominations, the show has been consistently well received, with Thornton garnering Actor Award and Critics Choice Award nominations. Long before he reminded us all of his acting prowess, however, Thornton appeared on a very different TV series when he supported sitcom royalty and the future co-star of his film "Sling Blade," John Ritter, on the early 1990s CBS sitcom "Hearts Afire."

The show was created by Linda Bloodworth-Thomason, who'd previously written for such well-known shows as "M*A*S*H" and had contributed an episode to "Rhonda" (one of many Emmy-winning sitcoms that we don't really talk about anymore). Of course, she remains best known for her 1980s/'90s sitcoms "Designing Women" and the Burt Reynolds-led "Evening Shade." In the case of "Hearts Afire," Bloodworth-Thomason teamed up with another established star in Ritter, who famously played Jack Tripper on the legendary ABC sitcom "Three's Company" between 1977 and 1984. She also drafted in Markie Post of "The Fall Guy" and "Night Court" fame.

The two stars play politically opposed individuals who develop an attraction to one another over the course of the show's first season. Thornton, meanwhile, plays Ritter's best friend, who divorces his wife and manages to stick around for the entire three seasons of "Hearts Afire," despite a major cast shakeup mid-way through.

In 1996, Billy Bob Thornton cast John Ritter as Vaughan Cunningham in "Sling Blade," the Oscar-winning movie that Thornton claims changed his life. By that point, the pair had worked together for three years on "Hearts Afire," and according to a 2003 Herald Times article, Thornton took the opportunity between takes on the sitcom to "crack Ritter up with the mentally challenged hillbilly character he'd use four years later as the centerpiece of 'Sling Blade.'" During their time on "Hearts Afire," then, Thornton and Ritter developed a close relationship that lasted until Ritter's passing in 2003.

At the time Thornton was cast on "Hearts Afire," he was still an up-and-coming actor. He'd made his screen debut in 1986's "Hunter's Blood" and racked up some small roles in TV shows including "Matlock" and Linda Bloodworth-Thomason's "Evening Shade." Thus, when Bloodworth-Thomason set to work on "Hearts Afire," she thought of Thornton.

The show stars Ritter as John Hartman, a father of two whose wife has recently left him for her therapist. John is also a conservative senator's (George Gaynes) aide whose political views aren't exactly aligned with those of Georgie Anne Lahti (Markie Post), a reporter who hopes to become the senator's new press secretary. Nonetheless, the two are drawn to each other.

Thornton, meanwhile, played Ritter's friend Billy Bob Davis. (He'd previously used his real name in "Hunter's Blood" and the pilot for a show called "Circus" as well.) In the series premiere, Billy Bob is kicked out of his house by his wife, Mavis (Wendie Jo Sperber). Meanwhile, Georgie moves in with John, and the first season follows their developing romance and experiences in Washington D.C. But when Season 2 arrived, the show underwent a major retooling.

"Hearts Afire" Season 1 debuted in September 1992 and ran for 23 episodes, ending in March 1993. When it returned for a second season in October of the same year, it was a different show. According to a contemporaneous report from Deseret News, the series' first season was "both too political and often of questionable taste" due to multiple jokes that were "completely inappropriate for an early time slot." Whatever the case, CBS decided the show needed reworking, and in the Season 2 premiere, the now-married John Hartman and Georgie Anne Lahti decide to move back to John's home town in the midwest with John's two children. There, they buy the local newspaper and attempt to create a wholesome life for their family in a town that John often doesn't recognize from his childhood.

While the political aspect of the show was abandoned along with several characters, Billy Bob Thornton's Billy Bob Davis made it to the second season, moving with the couple alongside his mother, Velma Davis (Maxine Stuart). It was a good thing the producers kept him on, too. As the Deseret News put it, Thornton's "deadpan observations and dry wit steal almost every scene he's in."

"Hearts Afire" ran for two more seasons, wrapping up after its third season in February 1995. During that time, the show benefited from the presence of another towering TV figure, Ed Asner, who played Georgie's father, George Lahti, in 12 episodes of the series. While the show didn't continue past Season 3, Thornton and Ritter's relationship very much did, with the pair re-teaming for "Sling Blade" and reuniting once again when Ritter was cast alongside Thornton in "Bad Santa."

→ Baca artikel asli

#3757. Anker’s New Soundcore Space 2 Headphones Are Already 18% Off

Anker released the noise-cancelling Soundcore Space One headphones a few years ago, and while they were a more budget-friendly alternative to premium models from Bose and Sony, their performance and fit still fell short in several areas. The brand’s updated Soundcore Space 2 headphones are a major upgrade across the board: They’re comfier, have better ANC, and are currently 18% off at $106.49 (originally $129.99), a rare price cut for a still-new release from a popular brand.

Battery life is one of the Soundcore Space 2’s most attractive features: They last up to 50 hours with ANC on and 70 hours with ANC off, and a five-minute charge gives you up to four extra hours. The noise cancellation is strong for the price, blocking out lower-frequency sounds well. Comfort is also a major upgrade from its sparser predecessor, with roomier, more plush memory foam earcups and a padded headband. They’re also travel-friendly, as the earcups rotate and fold.

The Soundcore app allows you to customize EQ and sound presets, giving you added flexibility if you don’t like the default tuning. Instead of touch-sensitive panels, these headphones use conventional buttons, which help prevent accidental commands while adjusting the earcups and are easy to operate. Along with supporting Bluetooth 6.1, they also have a wired-cable option, which isn’t always the case with modern wireless headphones. That said, they don’t support USB-C audio.

If you want over-ear noise-canceling headphones that perform well but don’t have $200 to shell out for a more expensive model, the Anker Soundcore Space 2 headphones are a comfortable, effective, and long-lasting alternative that hover around $100 at the current discounted price.

#3756. 10 New Albums You Should Listen to Now: Alchemical Sisters, Fine, Shabazz Palaces, and More

Octo Octa and Eris Drew’s B2B sets are the stuff of legend, but the married DJs had never collaborated on a record until they teamed up to remix Special Interest’s “Disco III” in 2021. For a joint moniker they chose “Alchemical Sisters,” a play, of course, on the Chemical Brothers. Nominative determinism at work: ’90s big beat—from Orb to Orbit—makes up the core DNA of their first full-length album as a duo. Pieced together in their New Hampshire log cabin, Alchemical Sisters is, writes Andrew Ryce in Pitchfork’s new review, “a four-dimensional record where the past endlessly loops back on itself,” where “originality and reference become one and the same.”

Listen on Apple MusicListen on SpotifyListen on TidalListen on Amazon MusicListen/Buy at Bandcamp

So insular is Fine Glindvad Jensen’s musical realm that one ends up reaching for increasingly tortured anachronisms just to map its periphery: Lana if she fronted the Warm Inventions, Hope Sandoval if she made Honeymoon, a Bond girl who’s also Julee Cruise. That’s not to say Jazz Dreams is unwelcoming; the second solo album from the Copenhagen artist known mononymously as Fine is plush, cozy even, steeped in paisley folk and jangle pop. But spend enough time in this incense-scented, velvet-upholstered lounge, and you may begin to see strange shadows crawling up the walls.

Listen on Apple MusicListen on SpotifyListen on TidalListen on Amazon MusicListen/Buy at BandcampBuy at Rough Trade

Longtime producer for the indie rock stars Ryan Hemsworth returns with his first solo album in nearly a decade to remind us what his own songwriting sounds like free from others’ ideas. Turns out, it’s caustic electropop, filtered through grainy fuzz, pop vocal hooks that could scale a mountain, and the type of slamming bass notes that could work just as well in EDM or synthpunk. Hemsworth doesn’t have anything to prove, though, which allows him to tap a few guest features—Tegan and Sara, Illuminati Hotties, MSPAINT, Allie—that showcase just how far a singer-songwriter’s ideas can extend in their own voice and beyond. It’s a mature choice, showcasing not just his own skills, but how strongly they lift friends around him, too.

Listen on Apple MusicListen on SpotifyListen on TidalListen on Amazon MusicListen/Buy at BandcampBuy at Rough Trade

On It’s a Style Sport, his first full-length in six years as Shabazz Palaces, Ishmael Butler slips back into the many guises of his multifarious rap project. Lead single “Ahh World (I Won The Alpha Bet)” sets lucid social commentary to ominous beats that sound like the very dystopian future he rallies listeners to prevent; “Jheri Curl Jump,” meanwhile, is a loquacious love story murmured over jittery electro-funk. Butler’s latest for Sub Pop follows the sibling mini-albums Exotic Birds of Prey and Robed in Rareness. The LP features, among others, Thaddillac, Evan Flory-Barnes, and Kassa Overall, pairing Butler’s own production with that of sometime Shabazz collaborator Erik Blood.

Listen on Apple MusicListen on SpotifyListen on TidalListen on Amazon MusicListen/Buy at BandcampBuy at Rough Trade

To say Josh Johnson has a lot going on would be an understatement. The Los Angeles composer and multi-instrumentalist—sax, keys, synth, and effects, if you're wondering—has released killer albums with his trio SML and Jeff Parker’s ETA IVtet, plus produced for Flea and Meshell Ndegeocello. Now he’s making his solo debut on International Anthem with a set of tracks that canonizes his pivotal role in the West Coast’s burgeoning experimental jazz revival. Per press materials, Johnson drew inspiration from his former teacher Wayne Shorter, the West African philosopher Malidoma Somé’s writings, and “non-Western ways of thinking about time and being.”

Listen on Apple MusicListen on Amazon MusicListen/Buy at BandcampBuy at Rough Trade

Caroline Rose loves a good character. The New York singer-songwriter’s taste for writing people “who are a little strange, who are outsiders, who seem to fuck up a lot,” per a recent statement, fits nicely with their taste for genre-hopping. (To their credit, Rose hopped on the “concept album about fame” train years before it got cool again.) Their new album Gentling the Horse at the Walt Whitman Mall, got them in the studio with the Paper Chase’s John Congleton, leaning into the breezy hooks and angsty energy of surf rock to translate personal encounters with the uglier corners of the Americana dream.

Listen on Apple MusicListen on SpotifyListen on TidalListen on Amazon MusicListen/Buy at BandcampBuy at Rough Trade

Brakence knew what he was doing when he titled his comeback single after four years of radio silence “Ready or Not.” Despite his extended absence (or maybe, in fact, because of it), the singer-songwriter-producer has held on to a fervent fanbase happy to keep running back Hypochondriac and supplementing with Quadeca and/or Jane Remover shows until he resurfaces. But he still has his own long shadow to stand up to, a challenge he meets with a hefty 18 new tracks that return to his glitch-crooner roots.

Listen on Apple MusicListen on SpotifyListen on TidalListen on Amazon Music

Bay Area duo Sour Widows drew inspiration for their latest from a pretty bleak source: an apocalyptic dream cofounder Maia Sinaiko had wherein “all traces of human civilization would soon be obliterated, our collective efforts lost forever; no one would ever know.” Sinaiko and bandmate Susanna Thomson lay out this premise in appropriately bleak tones of grunge, emo, and alt-rock. But working with Jay Som and Boygenius producer Melina Duterte, they also manage to infuse these heavy compositions with a sense of urgency, fleet-footedness, and, perhaps most importantly, hope.

Listen on Apple MusicListen on SpotifyListen on TidalListen/Buy at BandcampBuy at Rough Trade

After a 15-year career heavy with touring, it’s understandable that Loidis wants some peace. Lucky for us, that means continuing to make his special blend of minimal techno, ambient, and dub, as exemplified on the stellar 2024 album One Day. His next LP, From The Toolkit Of Lovers, was recorded in the summer 2025 and centers on drawn-out tracks with plenty of room for stretched-out builds and spacious transitions. In his own words, it’s ”a 110-minute meditation on life, love and dub.”

Listen on Apple MusicListen on SpotifyListen on Amazon MusicListen/Buy at BandcampBuy at Rough Trade

In the niche field of past-life regression therapy, “passing the cup of sorrows” describes a concept wherein two souls in the spirit world exchange energy, allowing one spirit to understand and forgive one who hurt them in a past life. These moments of spectral interplay inspired the avant-garde composer William Basinski’s first album in six years, a 40-minute suite of single-note piano and whispering electronics. He composed the piece almost entirely while watching testimonials of the therapy, using their relationship to fragmented and painful memories to guide his hand, mind, and heart.

Listen on Apple MusicListen on SpotifyListen on TidalListen on Amazon MusicListen/Buy at BandcampBuy at Rough Trade

#3755. Sunrise On The Reaping Director Francis Lawrence Has Secrets About The Hunger Games Prequel's Key Relationship [Exclusive]

"The Hunger Games: The Sunrise on the Reaping" is coming to theaters soon, and fans immersed in the world of the Hunger Games — specifically, the transformed North American continent known as Panem under a totalitarian regime — will finally get to see Suzanne Collins' 2025 book play out on screen. I recently spoke to Francis Lawrence, the director of every single Hunger Games adaptation aside from the first one (which was helmed by Gary Ross), about one of the most essential bonds in this story: namely, the one between our protagonist Haymitch Abernathy (Joseph Zada) and his fellow District 12 tribute Maysilee Donner (McKenna Grace).

As Lawrence revealed, he and his actors kept some things close to the chest. "There were a lot of conversations with both Joe and McKenna because we started with the games, and so a lot of our lead-up to shooting was rehearsals of fights, then action sequences and things in Spain where we were shooting," the director said. "And so I had the two of them a lot. And so we would just talk constantly about the two of them." He continued:

"Quite honestly, I think the three of us have some secrets about their relationship. I've just always found it really interesting that when she sees him, she slams the door in his face and they've known each other their entire lives. And I think there's probably other elements, too, to their story that Suzanne hasn't written about. So we all have our own secrets that we use to get underneath what the characters are and how they relate to one another and why they may have conflict and why they bond and all of that. So that stuff's really fun. And they're great at it because they're both great actors."

If you're about to yell "spoiler alert!" at me, calm down; the relationship between Haymitch and Maysilee is actually revealed in Suzanne Collins' first literary sequel, "Catching Fire," which was published back in 2009. As that trilogy's heroes and District 12 tributes Katniss Everdeen and Peeta Mellark (played on-screen by Jennifer Lawrence and Josh Hutcherson) prepare to head back into the arena for the Third Quarter Quell — a super-evil edition of the Hunger Games, which puts former victors back into the arena — they watch footage of the 50th Games, where Haymitch and Maysilee formed an alliance against double the number of tributes. That's how they watch Maysilee's bitter end: "Haymitch runs for her, anyway. He arrives only in time to watch the last of a flock of candy pink birds, equipped with long, thin beaks, skewer her through the neck. He holds her hand while she dies."

Maysilee is also important to Katniss in a fully unexpected way. As Peeta points out, her mockingjay pin came from the Donner family, and Maysilee's abandoned twin Merrilee (the mother of Katniss's friend Madge), who "spends half her life in bed immobilized with terrible pain, shutting out the world." Katniss continues:

"I think of how I never realized that she and my mother shared this connection. Of Madge showing up in that snowstorm to bring the painkiller for Gale. Of my mockingjay pin and how it means something completely different now that I know that its former owner was Madge's aunt, Maysilee Donner, a tribute who was murdered in the arena."

The entire premise of the Hunger Games is disgusting on several levels, but something particularly insidious about them — and something that Suzanne Collins explores in each and every book she writes — is the fact that the kids pitted against each other in the arena end up forming bonds. With four District 12 tributes in Haymitch and Maysilee's games instead of two, Haymitch initially tries to avoid any alliances. But the youngest of the group, Louella McCoy (Molly McCann in the upcoming film), already has a long-standing relationship with him characterized by Haymitch's protective feelings for the girl. When Louella ends up at the center of a tragic accident, the sickest thing in the entire Hunger Games franchise to date happens, Louella is replaced by a brainwashed lookalike, Lou Lou (Iona Bell), who's forced to compete in her stead.

Francis Lawrence told me that he "[mapped out] the relationships" between Haymitch and his fellow tributes, and because Haymitch and Louella were playground "sweethearts," he made sure to sketch that out. Beyond that, though, he pointed out the parallels between Louella and Katniss, both of whom are young brunettes that favor braids and find themselves in the arena. "There's the connections that if you know the other stories and movies, that you might make of Louella with the braid and Katniss, calling her sweetheart, and calling Katniss sweetheart and all of those kinds of things, which are a bit Easter eggy if you don't really know the books and things, but all that stuff is fun," Lawrence mused.

"The Hunger Games: Sunrise on the Reaping" arrives in theaters on November 20, 2026 — and you'll see all of these relationships play out on the big screen then.

It's a good time to be a Hunger Games fan. Those who loved the original movies (which are streaming with a Netflix subscription) will soon be treated to another book to screen adaptation with the movie version of Sunrise on the Reaping. Actress Mckenna Grace has been method dressing while promoting her role as Maysilee/ Merrilee Donner, and I have to give her flowers for the latest subtle reference to the franchise's history.

Mckenna's celebrity fashion has been on point as she promoted Sunrise on the Reaping, and her latest nod to the previous films is a bit more subtle. While appearing on Today, she rocked a vintage Betsey Johnson that seriously resembles what Rue wore in the first movie when being interviewed by Stanley Tucci's Caesar Flickerman. Check it out for yourself below:

At first glance this dress might just look like a pretty blue dress from Betsey Johnson. But hardcore fans of the Hunger Games clocked that it resembles that Amandla Stenberg's Rue wore in her televised interview with Flickerman. As a reminder, you can see a shot of her in that scene from the movie below:

While plenty of folks credit Law Roach for starting the method dressing trend in Hollywood, plenty of celebrities have embraced it while doing those long and grueling press tours. Mckenna Grace and her stylist embraced this for Sunrise on the Reaping, with plenty of looks inspired by costumes from the original movies. She's clearly got a love for The Hunger Games, which will no doubt affect her dual performance in the prequel film.

I love this subtle look from Grace, but not all Hunger Games fans agree. Some still thing it's "too soon" to reference Rue's death, despite the movie hitting theaters back in 2012. Fans are still upset about the way she perished in the games, mostly thanks to her young age and small stature.

Rue's death has always been a sore point for Hunger Games fans, and the scene went viral again a few years ago when they realized The Boys star Jack Quaid was the one who killed her while playing District 1 tribute Marvel.

Rue is a fan favorite character from the original Hunger Games, and book fans can also say the same thing for Maysilee in Sunrise on the Reaping. The pressure is on for Mckenna Grace to deliver, but if her press images are any indication she'll be able to to do just that. Luckily the wait for the movie is nearly over.

The Hunger Games: Sunrise on the Reaping will hit theaters on November 20th as part of the 2026 movie release list. We'll just have to wait and see if Grace gives us any more iconic method dressing looks until then.

#3753. Matthew Perry's Ex Says They Joined the Mile-High Club on Bruce Willis' Plane

Matthew Perry and his ex, René Ashton, once got freaky aboard Bruce Willis' private plane ... at least that's what she's hinting at.

René implied that she joined the mile-high club when she and Matthew were jetting off to Turks and Caicos with Bruce and his girlfriend at the time.

In the new 3-part Netflix doc, "The One About Matthew Perry," the actress said the invite came before the late "Friends" star went to shoot "The Whole Nine Yards."

She said Matthew was "so excited," recalling ... "He's like, 'Bruce wants us to go on his private plane. Bruce wants me to share a plane with him.'"

At one point, a producer off-camera asked how the flight was, leading René to quip ... "I joined a club."

And once they got to Turks and Caicos, René said the trip was "pretty amazing" and paparazzi-free, so they got to have a normal experience and enjoy their time.

She and Matthew dated for about a year back in 1999 -- René said she was "in love" with him when they were together.

#3752. Will Kim Kardashian Ever Run for Political Office? She Says…

In her new biography Citizen Kim, Kim Kardashian addresses whether she’d ever run for political office, cheekily joking of the speculation, “I am so flattered.”

Kim Kardashian isn’t looking to keep up with politics—at least right now.

The Kardashians star addressed whether she’d ever make a foray into the political sphere—admitting she’s not entirely ruling it out, especially after former President Joe Biden dropped out of the 2024 election. (Vice President Kamala Harris ultimately became the Democratic nominee, though she lost to President Donald Trump.)

“People ask me all the time,” Kardashian revealed to Jonathan Van Meter in his new biography Citizen Kim, which hit shelves Oct. 27. “I mean, the amount of people who wrote to me after Biden’s disastrous debate? The fact that you guys think I’m capable of this? I am so flattered.”

But Kardashian still has a few key to dos to skim through before she’d ever consider a campaign.

“I think that, number one, I have to pass the bar and become a lawyer first,” the 46-year-old admitted. “And if you do run for office, you can’t really own and run a company, and I love SKIMS and what we’ve built. That is my focus. So, I definitely need some time.”

Nevertheless, Kardashian—mom to North, 13, Saint, 10, Chicago, 8, Psalm, 7, with ex Kanye West—teased, “But we have time.”

After all, she hasn’t tagged the lawyer base just yet, although she has assumed the role of attorney—on TV.

In the biography Kardashian admitted to Van Meter she was “not sure” where her journey in earning her law degree—she graduated from a six-year law office study program in May—would lead.

One thing for certain, though, is she hasn’t fully given up on her lawyer dreams after failing the bar exam in July 2025—even if she has put the process on pause.

“Six years into this law journey, and I'm still all in until I pass the bar,” Kardashian wrote in a post shared to her Instagram Story last November. “No shortcuts, no giving up—just more studying and even more determination.”

Indeed, The SKKN by Kim founder considered her failed test “fuel” for her to push even harder toward her goal.

"I was so close to passing the exam and that only motivates me even more,” The All’s Fair actress continued, going on to quip of her role in the Ryan Murphy series, “I'm not a lawyer yet, I just play a very well-dressed one on TV.”

#3751. Pat McAfee References Charlie Kirk in Complaint About 'GameDay' Experience

Pat McAfee made reference to the assassination of Charlie Kirk while complaining about his experience at the University of Utah on ESPN’s College GameDay.

“I thought to myself, has anything, like, really scary happened at a college gathering in the state of Utah recently?” McAfee, 39, said Monday, October 26, on ESPN’s The Pat McAfee Show. “Do we think we should be amplifying reasons for why people hate my guts, as a university? I don’t know. There was a lot of mixed feelings for me going out there.”

He added, “It’s like, all right, I hate this place. I hate this place.”

McAfee was referencing a video released by the University of Utah before he and the GameDay crew arrived in Salt Lake City for the Utes’ game against the University of Houston on Saturday, October 24.

In the video, students were asked to give their best McAfee impression. One student called McAfee “corny,” while another urged him to stop taking his shirt off around college kids on the GameDay broadcast.

“If these kids would have done this on their own blogs, all good,” McAfee said on Monday. “Everybody’s taking shots at me, anyways, for everything. I get it. If the two ladies that were hosting that put that out on their own blog, I’d be OK with it. You’re allowed to do what you do.”

He continued, “The university puts that out? Like, as I’m about to go out and do an infomercial for the place? And then I’m walking in front of thousands of these people. This is how the university feels about it? It’s an uncomfortable feeling.”

McAfee referenced Kirk a second time later in his rant, mentioning “what happened 40 miles south of Salt Lake City a little over a year ago.”

Right-wing political activist Kirk was assassinated while speaking at Utah Valley University in Orem, Utah, in September 2025. He was 31.

McAfee said he “did not enjoy the morning there” and hopes GameDay never returns to the University of Utah.

The University of Utah issued a statement about the school’s since-deleted social media post on Monday.

“University staff are responsible for reviewing and posting content on official social media accounts and we take full accountability for that decision,” the school said. “University officials have reached out to Pat to express appreciation and regret. We sincerely apologize for creating any perception that Pat was unsafe or unwelcome on our campus. Nothing could be further from the truth. Pat and the entire GameDay crew are always welcome at the University of Utah and we deeply appreciate the opportunity ESPN provides to showcase our university and community.”

#3750. Travelzoo (TZOO) Q3 2026 Earnings Call Transcript

Need a quote from a Motley Fool analyst? Email [email protected]

Travelzoo (TZOO -2.64%) reported a strategic shift toward a membership-based revenue model, which resulted in a reported operating loss for the third quarter. Management indicated that the loss was primarily driven by increased marketing investments aimed at expanding the paid club member base. The company noted that membership fees are recognized as revenue ratably over a 12-month period. Member acquisition costs are expensed immediately. Management stated it expects this investment to drive higher recurring revenue and improved profitability in 2027 as renewals increase.

Almost 90% are likely to book an unplanned trip with an attractive offer. 62% plan to take 2 or more international trips in 2027. Information about their median household, income shows that they have the means to do so. it is especially given the outstanding value of our club offers.

Travelzoo members also hold on to leisure travel longer than the general population if household spending had to be cut. Finally, slide 27, we provide an overview of our management focus. We are working to grow the number of paying club members and accelerate revenue growth by converting legacy members and adding new club members. Retain and grow our profitable advertising business, accelerate revenue growth which drives future profits in spite of temporary lower EPS Grow Jack's Flight Club's subscription revenue, and launch Travelzoo Meta. Now Christina will provide an update on Travelzoo Meta and Jack's Flight Club.

Christina Sindoni Ciocca: We expect the first Travelzoo Meta experiences to become available in Q4 of 2026. Access to Travelzoo Meta will be an exclusive benefit of Travelzoo Club membership. For Jack's Flight Club, to align with Travelzoo and other investment priorities, our focus is on revenue growth, by growing members.
