#3749. Los Angeles Mayoral Candidate Nithya Raman Was Once Poised to Be the City’s Mamdani—Then She Broke With the Local DSA

“Hell no to communism,” a man on a tiny pink bicycle called as he pedaled past Los Angeles mayoral candidate Nithya Raman. His words lacked conviction—as if he wanted the satisfaction of saying them without the inconvenience of confrontation. If they registered with Raman, she didn’t let on.

The heckle briefly punctured the idyll at CicLAvia, the long-running open-streets bike that brings thousands of cyclists to different LA neighborhoods multiple times each year. Men on absurdly tall bikes rolled by. Intimidatingly cool teenagers zigzagged through the crowd on BMX bikes. A rider in a full Spider-Man suit cruised alongside parents pulling Radio Flyers. Raman was there petting dogs and chatting with cyclists. For a few hours, the streets looked like a temporary manifestation of the Los Angeles she has spent years trying to build: one with more public space, cycling infrastructure, and walkable neighborhoods. “This event fills me with so much joy,” one rider told her. Raman smiled and nodded.

But ask Spencer Pratt—or just about anyone in the conservative media—and that guy on the little bike had Raman pegged. Right-wing observers of this year’s mayor’s race have labeled her a card-carrying member of the “far left.” In his viral campaign videos, Pratt, the registered Republican who came in third behind Raman in June’s primary, portrayed her as a “corrupt communist” and “champagne socialist” who cares more about bike lanes than homelessness or crime. And Raman is frequently compared in the national press to New York City mayor Zohran Mamdani, whose rise like hers began with the Democratic Socialists of America.

However, Raman's fractured relationship with the DSA has become the defining paradox of her candidacy here in LA. The 45-year-old became a rising star on the left after riding a DSA endorsement to an upset city council victory in 2020, but Raman drifted from the organization on issues ranging from Israel to the Mansion Tax once she was in office. Now, large parts of the city's democratic socialist left no longer see her as their standard-bearer, even though she is routinely portrayed by critics as the face of that coalition.

Raman greeting City Controller Kenneth Mejia's corgi.

Her estrangement from the DSA was front and center in the final weeks of the campaign. Vanity Fair reported earlier this month that the organization’s national leadership was considering stripping Raman of the endorsement she received from its Los Angeles chapter in September, citing her refusal to explicitly embrace the Boycott, Divestment, and Sanctions movement against Israel.

Raman is getting by without them. She edged Pratt out in the primary, before DSA-LA endorsed her, by fewer than 30,000 votes, overtaking the brash newcomer as late-counted mail ballots vaulted her into the November runoff against Mayor Karen Bass. Just days after it was reported that her DSA-LA endorsement might be rescinded, Los Angeles County Supervisor Lindsey Horvath, a Democrat who was once served as the vice president of her college’s Republican club, endorsed Raman’s candidacy. So while Raman’s relationship with the DSA has faded, she’s attracting support from elsewhere in the Democratic establishment.

But rather than writing off the frustrations that fueled Pratt’s campaign, she believes there is meaningful overlap between Pratt and Raman supporters. “[We have] to try and tap into a very nontraditional coalition,” Raman tells me a couple days after the CicLAvia event.

“The success of Spencer Pratt’s campaign showed how much frustration there is here in the city of Los Angeles. And while I think that he was not the right candidate for this moment, I’m also in this race because I’m frustrated and because I think things can be better.”

By September, Raman opened up an 11-point lead over Bass among likely voters in a UC Berkeley Institute of Governmental Studies poll conducted for the Los Angeles Times. While 33% of voters surveyed said they were still undecided, the polling suggested that Raman could very well be the city’s next mayor. Some prediction markets are also giving Raman a slight upper hand, but at least one of those same sites showed Pratt advancing to the runoff over Raman in the lead-up to June’s primary.

Vanity Fair met up with the mayoral candidate at The Trails café in Griffith Park (her suggestion), not far from her home in Silver Lake, to learn more.

Raman sat down at the outdoor café in Griffith Park and placed a black moleskin notebook on the table. It looked like it could belong to a progressive college student, plastered with stickers championing conservation efforts in the Mojave Desert, a rainbow, and the Silverlake Conservatory of Music—an organization founded in part by Michael “Flea” Balzary, from LA’s own Red Hot Chili Peppers. She was wearing a black suit with black leather open-toe shoes. Raman had just come from a press conference where she received LA controller Kenneth Mejia’s endorsement, and admitted to being a little burned out.

Raman’s story begins thousands of miles from Los Angeles. Born in southern India in 1981, she moved to the United States at age six with her mother to join her father, who had come earlier to study engineering at the University of Louisiana at Lafayette.

“When we came to the US we had nothing. We had budgets for groceries every month. We lived in an apartment,” Raman told Vanity Fair. Family vacations were limited to road trips, and eating out usually meant Pizza Hut.

Her family eventually settled in Burlington, Massachusetts, where an influx of East and South Asian engineers was reshaping the mostly white suburb. Raman said she had “some friends, not a ton,” and recalled being “bullied intensively” as one of many South Asian children caught in the racial tensions. Her parents were very protective, Raman said. Did she date as a teenager? “No one would have ever asked me out,” she told VF with a laugh.

Nithya Raman with her grandparents as a child

“I just wanted to leave,” she said when asked about her career ambitions in high school. Hoping to escape to California, she applied to Stanford and Berkeley but ended up staying close to home at Harvard, where she still had little idea of what she wanted to do.

In Cambridge, she met her future husband, Vali Chandrasekaran, an editor at the Harvard Lampoon, made a lot of friends including Natalie Portman, and graduated with a small amount of student debt. She then went on to earn a master’s in city planning from MIT.

After working for a nonprofit in India and spending some time in New York—where Chandrasekaran wrote and produced for 30 Rock—Raman got married in 2010 and moved to Los Angeles in 2013. The couple first settled near Park La Brea and later Laurel Canyon. Chandrasekaran worked on Modern Family around that time. In LA, Raman said, they built “a really great network of smart, funny people.” The councilmember said she counts 30 Rock showrunner Robert Carlock, Modern Family cocreator Steven Levitan, and Tina Fey as friends.

Nithya Raman with her husband and children

In 2017, Raman became one of the founders of the SELAH Neighborhood Homeless Coalition, a homelessness nonprofit.

Raman’s frustrations with the government’s handling of the homelessness crisis ultimately pushed her into politics. In 2020, she pulled off one of the biggest upsets in recent LA political history, defeating incumbent councilmember David Ryu in a race that pitted the establishment against the insurgent progressive wing of the Democratic Party. On the council, Raman gave the DSA some of its biggest policy wins, strengthening renter protections and passing a significant reform to the city’s rent stabilization ordinance. She also voted against a billion-dollar police union contract in 2023, opposed the multibillion-dollar downtown Convention Center expansion, and defended the independence of the controller’s office, which experienced budget cuts under the Bass administration.

Raman speaks to media and potential voters.

But at the same time, she strayed from the DSA platform, and the organization’s grievances with Raman accumulated. “I did not join the DSA because I had a deep ideological drive, but I do really care about issues and they were the only political organization in LA talking about renters’ rights, the only one,” Raman told Vanity Fair. The mayoral candidate said she favors the Scandinavian model of social democracy, signaling a belief in a regulated free-market economy rather than the more explicitly anti-capitalist vision embraced by many in the DSA. (The DSA-LA platform calls for the expropriation of corporations in key industries including food, internet, and real estate.)

In 2024, DSA-LA censured her after she sought support from a pro-Israel political group (Democrats For Israel-Los Angeles) during her reelection campaign. Later that year, DSA-LA issued an “expression of disapproval” following Raman’s vote to approve LAPD Chief Jim McDonnell, who collaborated with ICE in 2015 and opposed a sanctuary policy at the state level.

Then in 2026, Raman proposed changes to Los Angeles’s voter-approved Mansion Tax after evidence suggested the measure generated far less revenue than had been projected and suppressed housing construction. “My desire for reform of the Mansion Tax really created some challenges with the [DSA], and people within it,” said Raman.

And the thousandth cut came when Raman launched her mayoral campaign without first consulting the local DSA chapter, angering organizers who felt the race stretched their resources too thin. The local chapter had already closed its endorsement process, and ultimately decided not to endorse a candidate for mayor.

In the end, none of the DSA-backed members of the Los Angeles City Council endorsed Raman’s mayoral campaign either, instead opting to ride with Bass. And neither Estuardo Mazariegos or Faizah Malik—both of whom ran for city council this year with a DSA-LA endorsement—endorsed Raman during the primary.

“​​I understand those fears and feelings and I hope that we can build trust and work together again,” said Raman when asked about her strained relationship with the DSA.

Even Jane Fonda, who swore Raman into office after her 2024 city council reelection, endorsed Karen Bass for mayor instead. Raman insists the two remain close. Additionally, several young left-wing comedians in Hollywood who this year rallied behind DSA candidates including Malik, Mazariegos, and city attorney candidate Marissa Roy did not endorse Raman.

According to Raman, housing policy is the key issue that distinguishes her today from the Democratic Socialists of America. The city, she said, must build—densely, quickly, and at every income level. That conviction has increasingly put her at odds with activists who view private development with skepticism or who prioritize protecting existing neighborhoods due to gentrification concerns.

“Building more housing is the key thing for LA’s recovery and resiliency,” Raman told Vanity Fair. “If we can invite more people to live here. If we can bring young people here. If we can make this paradise into a place that welcomes immigrants, creative people, entrepreneurs from all over the world. That’s what made LA into what it is, and we won’t have that anymore if we don’t build more housing.”

Raman, who serves as chair of the city council’s housing and homelessness committee, secured a small victory on Friday when the Los Angeles Homeless Services Authority released its annual point-in-time homelessness count. The data showed citywide street homelessness increased nearly 8% compared to the previous year, while it declined 49% in Raman’s own district.

“She got homelessness down in her district by treating each encampment as an emergency to be solved and going from person to person in that encampment, saying, ‘What do you need?’” said Adam Conover, a comedian and television host who met Raman when he volunteered for SELAH.

On the campaign trail, the city councilmember promised to cut unsheltered homelessness in half before the 2028 Olympics. It’s a big promise, and her opponent isn’t buying it. Bass has been forcing Raman to explain her own role in overseeing the city’s troubled homelessness response. At an October debate, Bass pointed out that Raman, as chair of the city council’s housing and homelessness committee, had repeatedly approved spending on Inside Safe, the very program Raman now criticizes as expensive and insufficiently accountable.

Homelessness could be the issue that gets Raman elected, but instead of neutralizing one of her biggest liabilities, Raman has stood by her opposition to a ban on homeless encampments within 500 feet of schools, opening herself up to attacks from Bass and Pratt.

“We look forward to winning a contest against an opponent who allows encampments near schools and fights against hiring more cops,” said Alex Stack, a campaign spokesperson for Bass, in a statement issued the day before Raman secured her spot in November’s runoff.

Raman’s break with Karen Bass might have been even more dramatic than her split from the DSA. Bass supported Raman during her 2024 city council campaign, and shortly before she entered the race, Raman endorsed Bass for reelection. The two women governed as allies through much of Bass’s first term, making Raman’s decision to challenge the incumbent all the more startling.

The mayor was given no indication from Raman that a challenge was coming, according to her campaign. Bass’s team alleges that she found out Raman was running from a reporter. Armed with that information, Bass called Raman on February 7, the last day candidates could enter the race, and asked the councilmember whether she intended to mount a challenge. Bass’s campaign says Raman “largely waffled on the call.” Raman entered the race hours later.

Raman told Vanity Fair she called Bass the morning of the filing deadline to let her know she was running. The following day, Bass invited Raman to her home for a private conversation, which had not previously been reported. “I didn’t want to move forward without having a conversation with her,” Raman told me. “I’m grateful that she made the time for me.”

She acknowledges that her entry into the race damaged relationships. Raman said she had expected a more established challenger—perhaps Horvath or 2022 mayoral candidate Rick Caruso—to emerge and didn’t seriously consider running until the final days before the filing deadline. “I don’t know how my decision-making process could have been different given the circumstances,” she said. “It was not an intentional effort to undermine our relationship or undermine our trust, but it was really just a product of that moment of the mayoral race playing out as it did.”

Lest anyone think Raman is on an island of her own, the city councilmember has begun winning over some powerful allies.

Raman with Congresswoman Laura Friedman, 30th District (left), and Supervisor Lindsey Horvath (right).

But the mayoral race has come to reflect a broader tension among Democratic officials navigating the party’s ideological civil war. An endorsement in this race could signal a preference for either the insurgent progressive wing of the party or for the more moderate establishment.

Meanwhile, the industry is showing Raman lots of love. Chelsea Handler, Mindy Kaling, Adam Scott, Fey, and Portman have all publicly lent their support to Raman or donated money, according to her campaign.

Conover, a member of the Writers Guild of America West’s board, spoke to Vanity Fair in an independent capacity. He said Hollywood’s creative class has embraced Raman because they view her as one of their own. “Her husband is a television writer,” he said. “This is the world that she comes from.”

As writers, actors, and crew members endure a prolonged slowdown in production, Conover said Raman’s Hollywood agenda—which calls for putting an experienced production hand in the city’s film office, streamlining permits, and cutting bureaucratic delays—resonates because workers may see in Raman somebody who “actually gives a shit.”

He contrasted that with Bass, whom he criticized for turning FilmLA into a “NIMBY” organization that’s more concerned with appeasing community complaints about production than driving it. He also is critical of Bass for failing to appear on the WGA or SAG-AFTRA picket lines while they were on strike in 2023.

“The improvements Mayor Bass made at FilmLA have resulted in permits being approved faster than at any point in LA history,” said Stack. “In nearly six years on the city council, Raman has never introduced a single piece of legislation to help the film industry—and when the council took up seven items in March 2026 to streamline film permitting and address the production downturn, Raman recused herself from four of them.”

In 2025, Raman supported a motion introduced by councilmember Adrin Nazarian that aimed to reduce fees and streamline other film production processes. But Stack also pointed out that Raman recused herself from votes on four other film-industry proposals, including measures to create regional permitting agreements with neighboring jurisdictions, establish a city film liaison handbook, develop a fast-track infrastructure program, and create a free micro-shoot permit for small public-space productions.

Conversely, most studio chiefs, agency executives, and major producers have not publicly lined up behind either candidate. “If I was putting myself in the mindset of a big executive at an agency or studio, I think I would feel pretty politically homeless, because the wealthy, center-right wing of Los Angeles hates Bass because they all lived in the Palisades,” said Conover.

The race has also attracted millions in outside spending from some of Los Angeles’s most powerful interests. According to the LA Times, the Western States Regional Council of Carpenters has put $9 million behind an independent campaign supporting Raman, two thirds of it supplied by hedge fund manager Aaron Sosnick, an advocate for expanding the city’s bicycle and bus lanes. On the other side, the Los Angeles Police Protective League has committed at least $3.5 million to supporting Bass, while the county’s powerful Federation of Labor is also backing the incumbent.

By early afternoon on July 19, the people who gathered for the bike ride were peeling away in search of televisions showing the World Cup final, and Raman headed toward Griffith Park to attend a watch party at the Autry Museum. At that point, Raman and her team had talked with hundreds of supporters. Dozens of riders had attached “Nithya for Mayor” posters to their bikes, giving the candidate lots of free advertising all along the six-mile stretch of open streets.

Twenty-nine-year-old Tony Ketcham told Vanity Fair that he’s supporting Raman because Los Angeles has “put up with mediocrity for too long,” echoing a critique Raman herself has made throughout the campaign. Standing beside Ketchum, Erika Witt, a 30-year-old policy professional who described herself as “a big policy wonk” and “an urban studies person,” said she’s been following Raman since before moving to Los Angeles because of the councilmember’s work on housing, public transportation, bike lanes, and homelessness. “She’s bringing about real change,” Witt said, arguing that while comparisons to Mamdani are understandable, “she’s our own LA special.”

Erika Witt with her bike and a Nithya Raman sign at the Los Feliz stop of CicLAvia.

Raman feels the same kind of way about those comparisons. “Mamdani is one of the most popular politicians in America right now, and I’m very grateful to be compared to someone who is that skilled as a communicator and a leader. But I am different. I am myself,” said Raman. Mamdani’s team did not respond to a request for comment.

Conover said Raman is more of a consensus candidate than Mamdani. “She’s not ideological. She’s a wonk,” he said.

Before entering the mayor’s race, Raman texted Conover a line that perhaps captures her more authentically than any campaign speech or debate has: “This is the greatest city in the world and people need to fucking remember that.”

#3748. 10 Shows Like 'A Different World' You Should Watch Next

Ross Johnson writes about television, film, and literature for Lifehacker. He has a degree in political science from the University of Rochester and has previously been a legal writer and editor for Thomson Reuters, for which he later traveled around India and the Middle East as an educator specializing in American English style and grammar for adults.

Ross has been an actor on stage, in commercials, and in independent films since childhood, and has written hundreds of articles and essays on genre literature and cinema for Barnes & Noble, Gizmodo, Kotaku, and Yahoo! Entertainment.Ross produces the Swan Songs film podcast and hosts The Sound of Tomorrow, a pop culture and current affairs radio show based in Rochester, New York, where he lives with his husband and dogs and supports the local arts and cinema scene. You can find him on Bluesky.

This is Streamalikes, where you can find a curated list of recommendations to stream based on what you already like.

The original A Different World was a groundbreaking sitcom in its era, spending six seasons highlighting Black academic drive among a cast of characters so indelible that we're now checking in on them decades later. Spearheaded by Queen Sugar's Felicia Pride, the update has been a critical and ratings hit for Netflix, earning the rarest prize of all in streaming television: a quick second season renewal. While you're waiting for season two, here are some other smart coming-of-age shows that blend heart and humor with realistic drama.

An easy pairing with A Different World, this show follows Black-ish's Zoey Johnson (Yara Shahidi) as she leaves home to attend a (fictional) California liberal arts college (the last couple of post-graduation seasons shift the focus to her brother Andre, played by Marcus Scribner). The emphasis is on driven young people who are capable of making big mistakes on the path to adulthood. A true comedy-drama, Grown-ish feels real(-ish) in a way that few other college shows have managed. Stream Grown-ish on Hulu.

Adapted from and expanding upon the 2014 movie, this show takes us to a (fictional) Ivy League school for a comedy-drama that tackles campus life and politics from a rather different perspective than that of Rooster. Logan Browning leads the ensemble cast as Sam White, who kind of inadvertently starts the titular radio show following a racially charged incident on campus. Far from the racial screed that some bad-faith YouTubers seem to find it, it's never shy about confronting the complicated and occasionally silly contradictions of campus activism, with each episode approaching life at Winchester University from a different character's perspective. Stream Dear White People on Netflix.

Kimberly (Pauline Chalamet) is an endlessly naïve scholarship student; Bela (Amrit Kaur) is an aspiring comedy writer on the make for the hottest guys; Whitney (Alyah Chanelle Scott) is an overachieving athlete and senator’s daughter; and Leighton (Reneé Rapp) is a closeted sorority girl. They're all randomly assigned to room together as freshmen at the fictional Essex College in Vermont. Created by Mindy Kaling and Justin Noble, this comedy-drama isn't nearly as salacious as its title suggests. There's sex for sure, but mostly it's a funny, queer-friendly show that's a little less about one overarching romance than it is about growing up, and the value of female friendship. Stream The Sex Lives of College Girls on HBO Max.

Even though we're backing up a couple of years to follow teens in their high school years, this British import's setting is a decent match, finding its students living away from home and family for the first time in their lives. At the fictional prestigious boarding school St. Gilbert’s, five Black teens are newly attending on scholarships, but their integration into the existing cliques is less than smooth—especially given that they're there largely as a PR stunt. Boarders blends teen romance, coming-of-age drama, and a delightful willingness to take the piss when it comes to the whole "rich private school" thing. Stream Boarders on Tubi.

Another modern take on a beloved '80s/'90s sitcom, this one's a bit different, serving as a total reimagining with no narrative connection to the original. And while A Different World is a comedy with dramatic heft, this one's more of a straight drama. It’s jarring for fans of the beloved original to tune in to find a serious show with the same premise as the Will Smith original—one that often leans into heavy realism. Jabari Banks plays, er, Will Smith, a 16-year-old from West Philadelphia who moves in with his auntie and uncle in Bel-Air following a gun charge and a run-in with a Philly drug lord. Banks is charismatic and believable, bringing a ton of personality to a show that occasionally veers down dark alleys. Stream Bel-Air on Peacock.

Comedian Benito Skinner plays himself, kinda, in this buzzy comedy that sees a former high school jock facing his freshman year in college while desperately trying to convince himself and everyone else that he's as straight as they come (relatable, except for the jock part). Much of the appeal is in its deft blending of tones: It's both a frequently raunchy college comedy and a sweet coming-of-age story about accepting yourself without worrying about what everyone else thinks. The cast includes Adam DiMarco (The White Lotus) and Rish Shah (Ms. Marvel), and, though it's more a story of one young man's coming of age, there's a strong ensemble backing him up. Stream Overcompensating on Prime Video.

The heart of All American (for most of its run) is Spencer James (Daniel Ezra), based on real-life NFL linebacker Spencer Paysinger. A star player for his high school team in Crenshaw, he's recruited by the coach (Taye Diggs) of a team in Beverly Hills to come and play for them, forcing Spencer to navigate two very different worlds while trying to build a life and potential career. Stream All American on Netflix.

Following a family tragedy, the already awkward 15-year-old Devi Vishwakumar (Maitreyi Ramakrishnan) has a pretty terrible freshman year of high school, and decides that she’s going to turn things around the following year, hoping that a nearly academic study of sex will get her the boyfriend(s) her raging hormones have begun to demand. It works, to a point—but getting a surprise yes to sex from her crush leads her to question what she’s really ready for. More about burgeoning sexuality than sex specifically, this Mindy Kaling-produced series is both honest and charming. Stream Never Have I Ever on Netflix.

For realism in a show about the college experience, you could do a lot worse than this docuseries following several students at Gallaudet University, a school for deaf and hard-of-hearing young adults. In one sense, it's a specific setting where perspectives on deafness vary wildly, even in Gallaudet's self-contained world (aside from being a show about academically driven and horny college students, this is also a great primer for hearing people about some of the issues). But even for all of that, the challenges in building a life and future away from home are pretty universally relatable. Stream Deaf U on Netflix.

There's a reason A Different World has returned in 2026, and a lot of that has to do with the fact that there was nothing else on TV quite like it back in the late '80s and early '90s. For all of its sitcom sensibilities, the show tried hard (especially after multi-hyphenate creator Debbie Allen arrived to rework things after its first season) to paint a realistic picture of college life and an HBCU. Of the few shows on TV with largely Black casts, almost none would go so far as to highlight achievement and excellence; A Different World managed to do so while building a cast of characters so memorable that we're more than willing to catch up with them nearly forty years later. Stream A Different World on Netflix.

Ross Johnson writes about television, film, and literature for Lifehacker.

#3746. Star Trek's Walter Koenig Was In A Forgotten '80s Sci-Fi Horror Movie With Bruce Campbell

We may receive a commission on purchases made from links.

Playing Chekov in "Star Trek" was far from Walter Koenig's only contribution to the science fiction genre. Sadly, Koenig, the original Chekov on "Star Trek," has died at the age of 90. While we'll always remember him for what he did within the landscape of that franchise, he certainly touched the genre elsewhere. "Babylon 5" is a prime example. But in the late '80s, he starred alongside another genre icon in a forgotten sci-fi movie.

Directed by Robert Dyke, 1989's "Mooncrash" starred Koenig as an astronaut with none other than "Evil Dead" franchise star Bruce Campbell playing his counterpart. One sci-fi icon and one horror icon came together for one oft-forgotten relic of genre filmmaking.

The movie centers on astronauts (Koenig and Campbell) who are sent to the moon to investigate what appears to be signs of life. They reanimate the body of a woman (Leigh Lombardi) who warns them that the moon is under the control of a race of alien cyborgs, who are waiting for the opportunity to invade Earth. They realize that desperate measures must be taken to prevent the cyborgs from leaving the moon

The premise is intriguing and its stars seem like they would make for a fun match. However, while there are plenty of '80s sci-fi movies that deserved more attention, the consensus seems to be that this isn't one of them.

"Moontrap" was a relatively low-budget affair and went direct-to-video. It also came at a unique time for both of its main stars.

Walter Koenig was in the midst of his later era as Chekov, with this movie released right between "Star Trek IV: The Voyage Home" and "Star Trek V: The Final Frontier." This was as the OG crew of the Enterprise was starting to run out of steam. William Shater has apologized for "Star Trek V: The Final Frontier," as it's not terribly well-regarded. The larger point is that, for as much as Trekkies love "The Voyage Home," it could be viewed as the last hoorah of the franchise's original cinematic era.

As for Bruce Campbell, he was firmly a B-movie actor at this time. Not yet a legend and still a working man's actor, he wasn't exactly in a position to be picky about the roles he was taking. The "Maniac Cop" trilogy was ahead of its time and Campbell was in those movies around this time as well, but they weren't exactly mainstream hits. He also starred in the underrated slasher "Intruder" in '89 which, again, wasn't a blockbuster by any stretch.

There's very little by way of critical opinion available online when it comes to "Moontrap," but it holds a not-so-good 27% audience score on Rotten Tomatoes. More than anything, it's a forgotten relic of '80s sci-fi that was largely allowed to exist thanks to the advent of VHS and the home video market. In there here and now, it would probably be a direct-to-streaming release on Shudder or something along those lines.

You can grab "Moontrap" on DVD from Amazon, if you're interested.

Since Pat Sajak left Wheel of Fortune, he’s actually been pretty busy. He did one more season of Celebrity Wheel of Fortune and even filmed some fun Wheel promo. However, Pat’s been mostly keeping things on lowkey, occasionally sharing some life updates as he enjoys his retirement. Pat and Maggie are perhaps one of the best father-daughter duos in the industry, so much so that Maggie even joined Wheel of Fortune a few years ago as the show’s social correspondent. Even after her dad left, she still has a big presence on the long-running game show, but she still makes sure that people are up-to-date on her dad. As of now, she’s staying as the show’s social correspondent while White continues her tenure, but you never know what could happen in the future.

Even though Pat isn’t one to post on social media, his daughter has certainly been taking up the job and making sure that people are updated on him. And that includes sweet birthday tributes. As someone who is close with her own father, I absolutely love them and how close they are. And I can only imagine how big the celebration really was in person for Pat’s big 80th birthday.

#3744. Trump Accused of Brutally Raping Former Miss Switzerland at Plaza Hotel in 1993, He Denies

President Trump brutally raped a former Miss Switzerland beauty pageant contestant at New York's Plaza Hotel in 1993 ... at least according to the accuser's account to Congress ... but Trump says it's all BS.

Beatrice Keul made the shocking claims in an interview with the House Judiciary Committee tasked with investigating Jeffrey Epstein's sex trafficking crimes.

Democrats on the committee recently released a report detailing all of Keul's allegations ... she claims Trump and Epstein preyed upon her and other vulnerable young women, ages 16 to 22, back in the day during the "Donald J. Trump American Dream Pageant," calling it a "hunting ground" for the two men.

According to the report, Keul said she was a 24-year-old model in November 1993 when she was recruited to go to the pageant by event promoter Jill Harth.

Keul said Trump came up to her during a luncheon at The Plaza to promote the pageant and told her he could pull some strings to get her a visa, as well as enroll her as a student at New York University.

Then, Keul said, Epstein approached her and offered her similar promises while inviting her to a soiree at Trump's Mar-a-Lago estate in Palm Beach, Florida.

That same day, according to Keul, a Trump emissary approached her with an invitation to meet with Trump upstairs inside a room. She said she went inside the hotel suite thinking Trump was going to help advance her career.

But Trump allegedly grabbed her from behind, ripped off her dress, and raped her, leaving bruises all over her body. Keul described the alleged attack to the House Judiciary Committee as "brutal."

Afterward, Trump allegedly again promised to help Keul obtain a visa and gain admission to NYU if she remained silent about the alleged rape, saying, "[T]he show must go on."

Keul said she completed the pageant in a "daze" and then flew back to Switzerland, refusing to enter into another Trump-sponsored pageant in Hawaii weeks later.

Months after the alleged assault, Keul said Trump phoned her and, once again, offered her the visa and NYU, but she said she wasn't interested.

A spokesperson for the White House tells TMZ ... "None of these allegations are true. This 'report' is nothing more than a desperate attempt by crazed Democrats to distract Americans from the surging Republican momentum driven by President Trump's historic achievements. The President kicked Jeffrey Epstein out of his club at Mar-a-Lago because he was a creep, and he has been fully exonerated of anything related to Epstein."

For the record ... Trump was never arrested or charged in connection with Keul's sexual assault allegations.

#3743. 9-1-1: Nashville's Hunter McVey Recalls Significant Weight Loss Amid Filming

9-1-1: Nashville‘s Hunter McVey is getting honest about how his “ever-changing” relationship with his body has influenced his career.

“It’s been a very interesting and changing relationship with health and how I view my body over the years,” McVey, 27, exclusively told Us while promoting his business, Bring Your Nutrition.

McVey recalled how he was “always insecure” as a child.

“Being heavier and bigger than everybody else, I always hated what I saw in the mirror. I was always self-confident and never liked the way I looked. Even when I would get skinnier or in shape or lose weight, I would be critiquing myself on, ‘Oh, I need to be skinnier around the waist,’ or whatnot,” he explained. “It’s not something that a lot of men talk about.”

The actor has heard similar stories from other men, saying, “I’ve talked to a lot of men over the years as well that are in the gym that look good and the funny thing is, the more and more you’re in the gym or in tune with your physique or your fitness, a lot of times the body dysmorphia continues to increase. Because then you’re being more picky and more picky.”

After booking his role as Blue on 9-1-1: Nashville, McVey’s relationship with his health evolved once more.

“Before I started filming 9-1-1: Nashville, I was about 225 pounds and that was mostly solid muscle. Because of how busy the 9-1-1 season was, I wasn’t able to eat as much, and instead of training six days a week, I could maybe train two. I went down to about 200 pounds,” he shared with Us. “I lost about 25 pounds of muscle all over. If you were to tell me that two years ago, I probably would have freaked out, because I tied a lot more of my value and my self worth to how I looked on the outside.”

He continued: “Now, I’ve just only — through experience and many, many years — been able to let myself know that I’m valuable even though I don’t have that extra 25 pounds of muscle on me. I still need to and like to stay in shape — obviously, not only for the camera, but for my peace of mind. The gym and fitness does a lot for my mental health, but I don’t tie my self worth, my identity and my confidence to how big or muscular or ripped I am, because if you compare yourself to others — especially in the age of social media — it’s a losing battle.”

McVey used his own experience with health and wellness as inspiration in his professional life.

“[It is] what I attribute some of my successes, discipline and good skills that have transferred to other areas of my life since I was 12. It was the first time I lost 40 pounds and started counting calories, macronutrients and going to the gym and working out,” he continued. “I had bounced up and down multiple times throughout my youth and through college. Then after school when I started doing social media and showing people how I’d lost weight, how I put on muscle, the transparency between between hormones, peptides and coaching people on how to improve their lives and some of the things that improved my lives, I wanted to create a business that provided value for people and helped people. That was one of my biggest goals. Because you can only post shirtless for so long on Instagram.”

In addition to starring on TV, McVey has been working in Bring Your Nutrition, which is set to launch a non-toxic food storage container line made from platinum silicone.

“The vulcanization process of that silicon allows these containers to heat up food in the microwave or the oven without leaching any toxins or microplastics into your food. Most Americans are consuming about a credit card worth of microplastics every single week and from many different sources,” he explained to Us. “Now, we’re finding that those microplastics are endocrine disruptors and we’re seeing in younger and younger people all of these issues — diseases, cancers, neurological disorders and problems — stemming from a lot of these microplastics.”

He added: “They’re meal containers that you can travel with and store your food in. The lid seal is perfect and keeps everything in there. You can bring them to the gym and they don’t break or they’re not heavy like glass. Then you’re not getting any toxins or any microplastics in your food when you heat them up in the microwave or in the oven up to 425 degrees Fahrenheit.”

McVey is proud of the career milestone, which comes as he has balanced acting with other responsibilities. “I’m focusing on doing the best job that I can with 9-1-1: Nashville, with being the best husband I can and with running the businesses the best I can,” he said.

The model also acknowledged, however, that finding balance isn’t always easy.

“I’ve always thrived in the chaos a little bit. I’m lucky that at this point in my life and in my 20s I have the energy and the push and the drive and the ambition to [start my own business]. The most important things that I prioritize in my life are time and family, so I figured that if I can spend the majority of my 20s building what I want to build then I can set myself, my wife and my family up,” McVey told Us. “I attribute the balance in being able to do what I do all to my wife Julia because I don’t know and haven’t met anybody else that could handle what my schedule is like on a daily basis. The support that I have out of my partner is how I’m able to keep going and how I’m able to do this and I wouldn’t be able to do it alone. She recognizes the goals that we have in mind of time and time with family and she knows that I enjoy these things and what makes me happy is creating. I’m very passionate about it and I love what I do, so that makes her happy.”

Bring Your Nutrition drops November 2, with early access available on November 1.

#3742. Barry Keoghan Underwent $6,600 Cosmetic Procedure to "Look Better"

After Barry Keoghan previously shared that he struggles with negative online comments about his looks, the Saltburn actor opened up about undergoing two skin-tightening procedures.

Barry Keoghan is giving a peek behind the blinds.

Nearly seven months after the Saltburn star said that he struggled with online bullying over his appearance, he shared he's undergone two rounds of XERF skin tightening—a needle-free cosmetic procedure that uses radiofrequency to lift and tighten skin.

"I've been hearing a lot about it," Keoghan explained in an Oct. 23 Instagram video posted by the Dermaspa owner Nilam Holmes. "Every time I come in here, it's very relaxing and therapeutic. Nilam looks after my skin."

He was eternally grateful that undergoing the procedures—which, as of September, costs $3,300 (£2,500) per visit at the clinic—gave him a much-needed self-confidence boost.

"I feel better about myself," the Peaky Blinders: The Immortal Man actor continued. "I look better. I think it's important to look after yourself inside and outside."

Meanwhile, spa owner Holmes—who founded the U.K.-based medspa in 2008—shared further insight into working with Kheogan, 34, amid his busy work schedule.

"I've been working with Barry for a while now, looking after his skin through different movies, campaigns and projects and the trust he places in me means a lot," she captioned the video. "His skin has really improved over the last few years, and he looks great."

While the Eternals star is more than pleased with his results, he previously admitted that negative comments made about his looks had taken their toll.

"I removed myself from online, but I'm still a curious human being that wants to go on and, if I attend an event or if I go somewhere, you want to see how it was received," he reflected during a March SiriusXM interview. "And it's not nice. There's a lot of hate online. It's a lot of abuse of how I look."

Keoghan noted that the constant criticism made him withdraw from the public eye.

"It's made me really go inside myself and not want to attend places, not want to go outside," he explained. "It's becoming a problem. I don't have to hide away because I am hiding away. I don't have to go to places because I actually don't go to places because of these things."

And being dad to 4-year-old son Brando with ex Alyson Sandro only made the strain on his mental health even worse.

"It is disappointing for the fans," the Oscar nominee added, "but it's also disappointing that my little boy has to read all of this stuff when he gets older."

But Keoghan isn't the only celeb to share insight into his cosmetic procedures. Keep reading for more stars who've detailed their own journeys.

The Oscar winner admitted she “can’t really get very much done” in order to maintain "movement in my face,” but she has volunteered as tribute on some procedures.

“I get baby Botox, but I can't do a lot,” she told Vogue in September 2026. “If my skin is going to be a little saggier than I would like, at least it looks dewy and pretty.”

But while she has she has gone through with the Botox, she won't bite the bullet on another popular procedure.

“I would love to have a buccal fat removal,” she added of the minor plastic surgery, where fat pads are removed from the lower cheeks for a slimmer look. “I can’t because everybody would know, but just know that I want that.”

#3741. Meet the Newsgirls, the Gen Z Duo Who Left ‘60 Minutes’ and Began Covering Epstein and Trump for a Young, Chronically Online Audience

Watching a Newsgirls video feels a little like being on FaceTime with a friend—one who’s great at doing winged eyeliner and speaking about Taylor Swift’s musical catalog or the last season of The Bachelor, but who’s also the type to fill you in on the latest global gossip. With grabby headers like “Trump Is Replacing the WH Press Room With a Swimming Pool?!” and “Hegseth’s Wife Spies on His Drinking & Women,” the founders behind Newsgirls hope to stop you mid-scroll for an easily digestible dive into the day’s most mind-boggling news stories.

“When we were launching Newsgirls, we were like, We have to start with a banger, really show people this is a cool thing,” says Wren Woodson, one of the two 30-year-old journalists behind the budding social media news empire. “We delusionally wrote these emails. We wrote [to] Bill Clinton…and we were like, ‘Hi, Mr. President.’”

“No,” her cohost, Mabel Kabani, interjects, “we didn’t say that. We actually said, ‘Hi, Bill.’ The worst thing ever.”

“So bad,” Kabani adds with a good-natured eye roll.

It’s easier to laugh off their naivete now that Newsgirls, their online news start-up aimed at Gen Z, has found an audience of nearly half a million followers across Instagram, YouTube, and TikTok. After bonding as the youngest hires at 60 Minutes—which marked each woman’s first gig out of college—Kabani and Woodson launched Newsgirls in 2024 “to talk to people our age…[who] are just not watching traditional outlets anymore,” Woodson tells Vanity Fair. “We think of ourselves as journalists, but creators too, doing credible investigative work.” They identify with the term “news creator,” says Kabani. “But we really like to go out of our way to tell everyone we’re journalists—because we’re doing the work as journalists. There are so many news creators out in the world that aren’t doing that work.”

Last week they launched Newsgirls Investigate—a YouTube and Substack series devoted to deep dives on substantive issues. “We haven’t seen this [kind of] rigorous, hard work from independent creators,” says Kabani. Their first episode, released last Thursday, features never-before-reported details about Pete Hegseth’s Department of Defense from former senior officials.

Before we go further, they address the name Newsgirls. “So many people have been like, ‘Would you consider changing the name? Would you consider not doing pink? Would you consider leaning in differently just to be taken seriously?’” says Kabani. “No, we’re confident in the work, and part of not being taken seriously means maybe more people will watch it.”

Kabani, who won a California press award after breaking the story of a neighborhood brothel in her Beverly Hills High School newspaper when she was 16, admits: “If you had told me in journalism school that picking a thumbnail, or deciding what to wear, or picking the first five seconds of a story was as important as the rest of it, I would’ve been like, This is a really weird and probably bad job. But the worst thing, as a journalist, is to do all the work and not have people watch it. So we’ve been putting so much effort into: How do you coat the broccoli in cheese? Some of that is how you look; some of that is the first five seconds. Some of it is…can you start with something gossipy to get people?”

Woodson, who “grew up in the middle of the woods in Wisconsin” and pivoted to journalism at Northwestern University following the 2016 election, says: “We want you to feel like you’re watching an Unwell podcast or something, but you’re getting Vice content.”

Over the course of their final two years working at CBS, the pair took dozens of pitch meetings “with all of the big outlets,” Woodson says, including Vanity Fair publisher Condé Nast. “Our plan was initially to embed as part of an institution. We believe in institutional journalism,” Kabani says. “The first inclination was never to just break off completely.”

They initially pitched the Newsgirls concept to then 60 Minutes executive producer Bill Owens, who exited his post as leader of the CBS news program in 2025 amid what he described as a loss of editorial independence. “He said flat out, ‘Your show needs to go to a visionary, and CBS is not a visionary. You’ve got to take this somewhere else,’” Woodson recalls. “And we’re very glad we did now.” (Over the weekend, it was reported that Owens will join independent video journalism start-up Newpress, which publishes work from YouTubers and podcasters, as its executive producer when his noncompete with CBS ends next March. VF has reached out to Owens and Newpress for comment.)

The two former 60 Minutes employees find it “heartbreaking” to see the structural overhauls at CBS News. “It’s hard to find news institutions you can trust these days, and it’s sad that that’s all being stripped away,” Kabani says. “It shows you how fragile institutions are—just one year, everything can change. All of these amazing correspondents have been fired or left. I can’t even recognize the place, really.”

Woodson, who worked primarily with veteran 60 Minutes journalist Lesley Stahl and won an Emmy for her very last story on the show—about a hostage who was taken from Israel to Gaza—says she and Kabani “were freaking out” over correspondent Scott Pelley’s headline-making stand for his coworkers during a fiery staff meeting in June 2026. “We didn’t see that as his personality. He, in the most loving way possible, kind of seemed robotic,” Woodson says. “He’s a very kind person, very genuine person, but he could lock in, and he’s such a professional. What you see on TV is who he is off camera. So I didn’t think he’d go up against the leadership, but it’s awesome.”

Kabani, who was interning on Capitol Hill for the Democratic caucus when Trump was elected, says independent news organizations like theirs might not exist without “the splintering of these big institutions—that partially is because of the Trump administration…. People are just so much more skeptical about what they see.”

Their big break at Newsgirls arrived going into 2026, with the release of the Jeffrey Epstein files. The pair had covered the convicted sex offender at CBS. “Mabel worked on the Epstein investigation at 60 Minutes when they got the autopsy photos, so she’s seen all of those,” says Woodson. (“All the naked-dead-Epstein photos are burned into my mind,” Kabani replies with a laugh.) “Exposing corruption was always one of our favorite things to do, coming from 60 Minutes. Those were our favorite stories to work on…. If there’s one type of person that’s the most disliked, that we’ve come across in our reporting, it’s billionaires.”

They channeled their experience covering the story into roughly three videos a day about the Epstein files as they went through them. “There’s millions of documents,” Kabani says. “People wanted it all contextualized in a way that they could just go through really quickly…. That’s when we blew up from 30,000 followers on Instagram to 200,000 in six weeks.” Says Woodson, “I still had the 100K flowers that my parents sent when we hit 200K. It was going so fast.”

The New York City–based creators have since found viral success by going out into the field—such as when they interviewed Staten Islanders supporting Andrew Cuomo before New York City mayor Zohran Mamdani’s victory in the November 2025 election. “We went to the White House UFC fight, and we snuck our way in because this boy had extra tickets,” Kabani says. More recently, they attended the Charlie Kirk statue unveiling in Times Square. “That one was really scary because it wasn’t just Charlie Kirk supporters; it was overtaken by white supremacists,” Kabani says. “It was the first time Wren and I felt like this could be dangerous.”

They’re less intimidated by the comment-section bullies lurking in their audience of roughly 50% men and 50% women. “A bunch of people think Wren and I are the same person and/or twins, even though we are literally different races,” Kabani says.

“We get so many, ‘Is this the same girl?’” Woodson replies. “Especially when you’re speaking about politics. If we’re just telling the truth, if we’re reporting, ‘Trump did this today’—if there’s no way to come back at that, they’ll just attack us.”

When the Newsgirls founders report on members of the Trump family and their various businesses, those in the comment section inevitably turn their attention to Hunter Biden. “We’ve invited all of the Trump kids to come on. We have tried many times,” Woodson says. When they cold-emailed Hunter Biden, he agreed to a video interview. They wanted to know about his “contradictory” political stances. “Hunter still wants to represent the Democratic Party…. But now he’s going on Tucker Carlson; he’s also saying he loves Candace Owens,” Woodson says. “We’re like, ‘Don’t you feel like you’re validating them by going on?’ And he’s like, ‘When has Hunter Biden ever validated anything?’ I thought it was refreshing that he could poke fun at himself.”

The interview also got a boost from Fox News host Jesse Watters, as they learned from a negative comment. “‘Just saw you two hair-flipping losers on Fox News,’” Woodson recalls with a laugh. “[Watters] didn’t say anything about us; he was just hating on Hunter a little bit. But thanks to the hater.”

Woodson and Kabani hope that Newsgirls will one day become, like Call Her Daddy host Alex Cooper’s Unwell empire, a network for other like-minded creators. But first, they’re focused on the upcoming midterms and then the 2028 presidential election, topics that devolve into another survey of the sorry state of things, generally speaking. “I remember the joke during the first Trump presidency was, he’s great for business for journalists. And it really is true, no matter which way you look at it,” Woodson says. “When he won again, we had just launched our show three weeks before, and we were like, Wow, our jobs just got even more important than we realized when we planned this launch.”

A Deep Dive Into How Larry Ellison Built His Influence

#3740. Is Berkshire Hathaway Stock a Buy Near Its All-Time High?

For years, investors wondered what would happen to Berkshire Hathaway (BRKA -1.44%) (BRKB -1.01%) when longtime CEO Warren Buffett stepped down. It was assumed that a Buffett premium was being applied to the stock, given the CEO's impressive track record as an investor. At first, that was true, with the stock falling on the news of his pending departure at the end of 2025. But as 2026 draws to a close, the market's view appears to be changing. And even with the stock now near an all-time high, it could still be worth buying. Here's why.

The big worry was that Buffett was the key to Berkshire Hathaway's success. Obviously, Buffett's investment approach helped to build the company, but it is a very different business now than it was when Buffett first took over as CEO. Today, Berkshire Hathaway is a massively diversified conglomerate, with businesses in insurance, utilities, energy, transportation, housing, retail, and manufacturing, among others.

At a time when investors are worried about the market, Berkshire Hathaway's diversification and focus on fairly simple-to-understand cash-generating businesses provide something of a safe haven. In fact, investors are increasingly worried about the high-tech companies that have driven the market higher, as many are increasingly leaning on their balance sheets to fund growth. To be fair, Berkshire Hathaway has some exposure to technology companies in its stock portfolio, but its operating businesses tend to be much more down-to-earth. And, just as important, new CEO Greg Abel hasn't done anything to compromise the basic nature of Berkshire Hathaway.

That said, the balance sheet is another big safety point. At the end of the second quarter, Berkshire Hathaway had more than $350 billion in cash and short-term investments on its balance sheet. That will provide the company with a material cushion in a bear market. And it will also give Abel the firepower to step in and buy while others are fearful during that bear market. That's something Warren Buffett was famous for, and he likely taught his successor the value of both patience and contrarian thinking.

There has been a huge change at Berkshire Hathaway with Buffett stepping aside. But roughly 10 months into Greg Abel's tenure, investors are clearly getting more comfortable with the change. And with Wall Street increasingly worried about the tech sector, Berkshire Hathaway's more mundane business profile and huge cash hoard make it look like an attractive alternative for those seeking out a safe-haven investment. Indeed, even with the stock near its all-time highs, it may be worth buying for those who think long term, like Abel and, before him, Buffett.

#3739. Google Quietly Linked Your Phone Number Across Your Accounts, but You Can Stop It

Emily Long is a freelance writer based in Salt Lake City. After graduating from Duke University, she spent several years reporting on the federal workforce for Government Executive, a publication of Atlantic Media Company, in Washington, D.C. She has nearly a decade of experience as a freelancer covering tech (including issues related to security, privacy, and streaming) as well as personal finance and travel.

In addition to Lifehacker, her work has been featured on Wirecutter, Tom’s Guide, and ZDNET. Emily has also worked as a travel guide around the U.S. and as a content editor. She has a masters in social work and is a licensed therapist in Utah.

In recent weeks, Google has been automatically connecting Android users' phone numbers to every Google account they are signed into on their devices—without requesting consent to do so. This means that if you are logged into personal, work, and disposable accounts on your Android phone, Google may have silently verified and linked your number with all of them even if you've deliberately kept them separate. If you that's not something you want, there's a way to fix it.

As Android Authority reports, some Android users have recently received confirmation emails from an official Google address stating that their phone number has been verified. Google has required occasional phone number re-verification but appears to have updated its support documentation about a year ago to make this feature opt-out by default. As part of this process, Google also adds your phone number to all Google Accounts that are signed in on the device, including current accounts and phone numbers and any that are added or updated in the future.

If you have an Android device, you can disable automatic phone number verification, which should prevent Google from linking sign-in accounts together. You can find this toggle in your Settings app under Google or Google services and preferences > All Services > Privacy & Security > Phone number verification. (Note that the exact path may differ by device.) You should periodically check to ensure this toggle remains off.

Updating this setting does not disconnect phone numbers and Google accounts that have already been linked. You can find out which are which via your Google Account Personal Info page. Select your profile picture in the upper-right corner to go through each signed-in account and open the Phone section to select and delete any numbers you don't want associated.

Finally, Malwarebytes advises scrolling down to the Usage section on the Phone page to view which services are listed as well as reviewing your Security & sign-in settings. If you added your phone number for two-step verification or to a separate app like Google Pay, deleting it from the Phone page won't remove it entirely from your account, and you'll need to update those settings separately. Automatic phone verification shouldn't be the culprit here, but it's useful to audit what your number is being used for and on which accounts if you want to keep things separate.

Emily Long is a freelance writer based in Salt Lake City.

#3738. A Rod Serling War Story Led To One Of Paul Newman's Most Underrated Movies

Arnold Laven's 1956 military courtroom drama "The Rack" isn't terribly well-remembered these days. If it's remarked upon at all, it may be for its performance from Paul Newman in one of his earliest film roles. It's also notable because its screenplay, by Stewart Stern, was adapted from a 1955 episode of the theatrical anthology series "The United States Steel Hour," which was penned by Rod Serling. Said episode, also called "The Rack," originally starred Marshall Thompson in the central role of Edward H. Hall Jr., a U.S. military Captain who has been accused of collaborating with the enemy during the Korean War.

In the movie version of "The Rack," Newman plays Captain Hall, while Walter Pidgeon plays Hall's father, a retired colonel. Hall returns from the war, having survived in a POW camp for a spell, and is mourning the loss of his brother, Peter, in combat. It's soon revealed that Captain Hall is about to be court-martialed for collaborating with the enemy. When Hall's father confronts him on the matter, he instantly admits that yes, he did collaborate with the enemy.

During the subsequent trial, it's revealed that Hall followed enemy instructions, making speeches and signing documents for them. Other American soldiers, meanwhile, withstood torture rather than giving aid to the opposing side. Hall is accused of being a coward. He wishes to plead guilty. Naturally, there is more to this story than initially implicated. The climax of the movie then reveals the horrors that Hall suffered at the hands of the enemy. However bad you think it is, dear readers, it's probably worse. Newman's performance is frank, tormented, and very good, and "The Rack" itself is one of the many underrated Paul Newman movies you should check out.

The "The Rack" was clearly designed to explore the horrors of war. When one goes back to the original Rod Serling teleplay, one can clearly see that "The Rack" is meant to be a condemnation of war violence, detailing just how horrible combat and wartime torture are. One can further read an essay on the Rod Serling website detailing Serling's PTSD and how he feared becoming a monster after fighting in World War II. Men his age had a unique set of phobias and concerns about combat. "The Rack" was clearly a means to explore Serling's fears.

The hour-long 1955 TV version of "The Rack" has been handily archived by the UCLA Film & Television Archive and can be accessed on YouTube. One will see immediately that the TV version is far more melodramatic than the movie iteration and features bigger, showier speeches. "I sold my soul for a filthy blanket that stunk of fish and urine!" Marshall Thompson's Captain Hall yells. "And three hours of uninterrupted sleep! And you know what else, Colonel? At the time, I felt it was one hell of a bargain!"

Thompson is a more demonstrative actor than Paul Newman, so one cannot even really compare their performances. It's like night and day. Thompson plays every emotion enormously, allowing his Captain Hall to emerge as wounded and pathetic. Newman, however, underplays everything, which makes the character richer and more intriguing. He's someone who knows how deeply his sins run.

As for the film version of "The Rack," one can find a vintage New York Times review, written by the inimitable Bosley Crowther, that praised Newman's performance. At the same time, though, Crowther disliked the movie's screenplay, describing it as "thin."

Having watched some of both versions of "The Rack," I can say that the TV version is way preachier. Rod Serling, as we know from his most famous creation, "The Twilight Zone," wasn't always a subtle author, preferring to get to the point.

Both iterations of "The Rack" were notable career turning points for Serling and Paul Newman, in turn. It was only after writing the TV version of "The Rack" that Sterling began contributing more heartily to the TV series "Playhouse 90," and, later on, writing screenplays for movies like "Patterns," "Saddle the Wind," and 1968's "Planet of the Apes." Then, "The Twilight Zone" kicked off in 1959, putting Serling on the American cultural map in perpetuity.

Newman, meanwhile, was a rising star when the big screen version of "The Rack" was released. He had previously been in the films "Somebody Up There Likes Me" and "The Silver Chalice," a disastrous flop that Newman once called his worst movie ever. (Seriously, it's bad.) Similarly, he had only made his TV debut a few years before on the underrated sci-fi anthology show "Tales of Tomorrow" (namely, in the 1952 episode "Ice from Space").

From there, of course, Newman continued to act for decades. No matter when you grew up in the time that followed, you were most likely introduced to him as a child, whether it was through his performance in "Cool Hand Luke" in the 1960s, "The Sting" in the 1970s, "The Color of Money" in the 1980s, "Nobody's Fool" in the 1990s, or even "Cars" in the 2000s. The man lived quite the life.

#3737. I Thought Big Brother Didn't Let Houseguests Lie About Powers Because It's Unfair, But That's Not It

Join The Club for quick access. Enter your email below and we'll send confirmation, and sign you up to our newsletter.

Keep exploring and earning more as a member.

Read reviews, interviews and deep dives on new releases.

Tyson And Parvati Went Off About How Jeff Probst And Survivor Producers Treat Former Players

After Big Brother Unfriending, I Keep Flashing To Devens Telling Dee She Won’t Be Pals With Guests

Jeff Probst Used The Survivor 51 Premiere To Call Out His Haters

Unlock instant access to exclusive member features.

Get full access to premium articles, exclusive features and a growing list of member rewards.

I never would have guessed this was the reason.

When you purchase through links on our site, we may earn an affiliate commission. Here’s how it works.

Big Brother does a lot of things that can't be done on some of the best reality shows, but there are rules. For example, lying about powers and advantages, which is common on Survivor, is a big no-no. I always assumed this was a rule because it was unfair to the Houseguests. However, series EP Rich Meehan recently said it's more about how it would impact the episodes.

Those who watch the BB live feeds with a Paramount+ subscription likely know that producers can struggle to edit a week's worth of content down into three hours of television. On that note, Meehan spoke to EW and presented the idea of how much more challenging it would be if these Houseguests were then able to make up lies about powers they didn't have:

Imagine if five or six people are making up powers, and then you’ve gotta tell all the story that isn’t real. So then you have all the actual story, all the alliance-building, everything else, and then everyone will [make up powers] if everyone knows they’re allowed to do it. And then it becomes a game of who can make up the best power.

At first, I was annoyed to hear it was mainly to make storytelling easier for episodes, but I get what Meehan is saying. If there aren't hard-and-fast rules about the details Houseguests can or can't lie about, then it would just devolve into everyone lying in a game where deception already plays such a huge role.

Rick Devens' Wife Clapped Back At A Big Brother Fan Trolling After Her Cancer Diagnosis

Lying about powers was a hot-button issue in Season 28 of Big Brother, as there was a big fight involving Yash Patel and the Icons when it came to Dee Valladares' BB Bribe. Houseguests were confused because Dee and Yash had conflicting stories about what her power actually did, and both claimed they couldn't lie about it.

Big Brother's producers didn't step in and clarify who was right in that case, and that had me wondering what the difference was in that instance. Perhaps it was because Dee technically broke her power by turning one action into six, which many fans argued shouldn't have been allowed.

Which circles back to my assumption that the rule was in place because it was unfair, but honestly, it just seems as though it is more about convenience. A cynical person would say producers allowed Yash and Dee to lie about the power because it made for good TV, regardless of the rules. I just think it makes it difficult to win Big Brother when it feels as though there's a shaky consistency in how certain regulations are enforced.

Fortunately, no one was so broken up about Big Brother waffling on rule enforcement that it impacted the ratings. CBS gave the series a two-season renewal, and there are whispers that an all-star season is on the way. I know the vets won't settle for any shakiness when it comes to the rules, so hopefully, they stash the BB Bribe away.

Big Brother is done for the season, but another summer of fun will surely be here before fans know it. For those who can't wait, check out previous seasons on Paramount+ and relive some of the series most dramatic moments.

#3733. Who Is Lula, the Four-Time President of Brazil?

Luiz Inácio Lula da Silva, who turns 81 on Tuesday, was reelected to his fourth term as president of Brazil on October 25. He is the seventh in a family of eight children from the state of Pernambuco, in northeastern Brazil. His political career began in the 1970s, at the Indústrias Villares factory, where he started working as a lathe operator at age 14. He led the metalworkers union in São Paulo in one of the strongest protests against the military regime of the time. He has gone from prison to the halls of power, with a beard reminiscent of that of Che Guevara and Fidel Castro. His missing finger, the pinky on his left hand, is a testament to his journey, having been severed by an industrial press during his time as a metalworker.

He took the victory stage alongside his third wife, Rosângela “Janja” Lula da Silva, whom he has been married to since 2022. He celebrated victory over Flávio Bolsonaro in a runoff election after Bolsonaro failed to win an outright majority in the first round.

Lula, who has become the leader of the left movement in Brazil, surpassed his far-right challenger by about 4 million votes: 51.79% to 48.21%. Bolsonaro has still refused to concede. He contests the Supreme Court’s decision to extend polling hours by two hours—a measure that was previously requested by both candidates. The president of the Senate, Davi Alcolumbre, and the the president of the Chamber of Deputies, Hugo Motta—both from center-right parties—congratulated Lula and declared the election valid.

“This election was a defeat for denialism, a defeat for lies,” Lula said in his victory speech, thanking “the Brazilian people for this generosity they have shown me for so long…the youth who took to the streets to defend democracy, and the elders who went to vote.” Lula looked to the youth during the campaign while denouncing foreign interference—whether from digital platforms or directly from Donald Trump: “This country will not accept being anyone’s backyard.” He assured the nation that he will collaborate with parliament, where the right wing has a strong presence. He has already done so over the past four years, making compromises on his progressive policies.

Last spring, few would have bet on yet another victory for Lula. He began a course of superficial radiation therapy on his scalp following the removal of a cancerous skin tumor, after previously being diagnosed with throat cancer in 2011. Throughout the election campaign, he repeatedly emphasized that he was in good health, assuaging doubts as his age and diagnoses aroused concern.

Lula previously governed Brazil from 2003 to 2010, after being elected in two of three campaigns. His promise at the time was to provide everyone with a roof over their head and three meals a day while honoring agreements with foreign investors. His third term came after he spent nearly a year and a half in prison, which he was sentenced to for corruption. The conviction was overturned due to procedural flaws and apparent bias. He won in 2023, defeating Jair Bolsonaro by less than two percentage points, after which Bolsonaro attempted to maintain his power by force. His policy places government investment at the center of the economy in a country with heavy public debt.

Originally published in Vanity Fair España

#3731. Boeing (BA) Q3 2026 Earnings Call Transcript

Need a quote from a Motley Fool analyst? Email [email protected]

The Boeing Company (BA -10.69%) reported a 4% revenue increase as growth in its defense and global services segments offset a less favorable delivery mix in the commercial aircraft division. Management updated its multiyear financial outlook, targeting $10 billion in free cash flow by 2029, while noting that production ramps remain non-linear and dependent on supply chain stability. The company reached critical milestones in aircraft certification for the 737-7 and 737-10 and secured a major defense win for the Navy's sixth-generation fighter program. Executives emphasized a disciplined approach to production rates, prioritizing safety and quality over aggressive monthly output targets to ensure long-term system durability.

Operator: Thank you for standing by. Good day, everyone, and welcome to The Boeing Company's Third Quarter 26 Earnings Conference Call. At this time, all participants are in a listen-only mode. Please be advised that today's call is being recorded. The management discussion and slide presentation plus the analyst Q&A session are being broadcast live over the Internet. Vice President of Investor Relations for opening remarks and introductions. Mr. Hill? Please go ahead.

Ortberg, Boeing's President and Chief Executive Officer and Jesus Malave, Boeing's Executive Vice President and Chief Financial Officer. This quarter's webcast earnings release and presentation which include relevant disclosures and non-GAAP reconciliations, are available on our website. Today's discussion includes forward looking statements that are subject to risks and uncertainty. Including the ones described in our SEC filings. As always, we will leave time at the end of the call for analyst With that, I will turn the call over to Robert K. Ortberg.

Robert K. Ortberg: Well, thanks, Eric, and good morning, everyone. Thanks for joining in today's call. As we enter the final months of the year, we are continuing to make progress across our businesses. having completed many of the key milestones in our 2026 plan, and building on the trust we have restored with our stakeholders. Through the third quarter, we delivered 161 commercial aircraft. The most since 2018. We received FAA certification for the 737-7 in July, and the 737 dash 10 just last week. We reached a new 4-year agreement with union representing our engineering and technical workforce the Pacific Northwest, avoiding any work interruption.

And we secured major program awards across our defense and space portfolio, including multibillion dollar contracts on key programs like the PAC-3, and the F-15EX. And we scored a generational win with US Navy, they selected Boeing to design, build, and deliver its 6th generation fighter jet making Boeing the exclusive 6th generation fighter company for both the US Air Force and the US Navy. These accomplishments reflect the significant progress we are making and they are made possible by the sustained focus of our team on safety, quality, and performance. We know there is more work to do, and I have said before, our progress is not always going to be perfectly linear.

But we continue to improve and I am confident we are on the right track. I will touch on the highlights I just shared as well as areas that require more attention in the quarters ahead and let's start with our development programs. All year, we have said that certifying our new aircraft models is 1 of our most important goals, The FAA certification of the 737-7 and the dash 10 represents the culmination of nearly 100 test flights more than 4 thousand hours of ground and flight testing. And I want to thank our employees who worked on or supported these programs and never lost sight of our mission.

And to our customers and partners, I extend my gratitude for the steadfast support and confidence in these airplanes.
