With the 737-7 and -10 FAA certifications were complete, our focus now centers squarely on the 777-9. We continue to make progress on flight testing and still need approval to begin the balance of the flight test program including ETOPS testing. We continue to expect first delivery in 2027 and all of our latest assumptions continue to be within the EAC we reset last year. Turning now to our commercial factories.

On 737, we continue to work towards stabilizing production at 47 airplanes per month. Our factory's key performance indicators are performing well, particularly in the final assembly where we have seen a 40% improvement in rework since the start of last year. However, as I mentioned at a conference last month, it is taking us longer than we had expected to demonstrate performance at rate 47. We are making progress, but we still have more work to do And I am expecting it will take us into next year to get fully stable at this rate.

Our output of wings is improving, but we need to demonstrate sustained performance at this level and ensure we have a high confidence plan to step up to rate 52 when the system is ready. To reach 52 aircraft per month, we have to get the North Line certified and producing, our back shops, including wings, stable and ready, and fuselages in Wichita stable at the higher rates. Taken together and integrating our learning from this year, our financial plan will target rate 52 in the back half of 2027. And as I have said many times before, it is more important that we do this ramp right than in what particular month we achieve a rate break.

And we will stay disciplined here. Switching now to 787. We are stable at 8 aircraft per month, while the factory is performing well. While we have stability in our factory, delivery cadence has been uneven month to month due to the seat certification challenges we have discussed previously. We are seeing increased stability in our supply chain and closely watch engine delivery performance as it remains the pacing item for rate 10. We still expect to start the transition to 10 per month yet this year and we are making good progress on our Charleston expansion to support even higher production rates.

Beyond the progress we are making in our factories, also continuing to position Boeing for the long term. That is showing up in the quality of our backlog, the demand we are seeing across the portfolio, and in the investments and program wins that position us well into the future. Last month, we achieved a transformational win that will shape the company for decades to come when the US Navy selected Boeing as the winner of the FA XX competition. To be selected for another 6th generation fighter program, is a testament to our innovation, our team, and the trust we have built with our customers.

It is a true honor to support the national security priorities and a responsibility we take very seriously. In addition to FAXX, we continue to see strong demand for proven franchises across our defense and space portfolio. Boeing received a 7-year contract to accelerate and eventually triple the PAC-3 Seeker production supported by our early investments in facilities, workforce, and supply chain. In the quarter, we also received a contract to manufacture 22 more F-15EX aircraft for the US Air Force. And this award brings the total number of aircraft under contract to 120 with the potential for additional upside as the F-15EX continues to demonstrate the value of its unmatched payload and range capability.

Beyond new franchise wins, we also continue to see tangible benefits from the active management approach we have talked about. As I said last quarter, Starliner was a program where we still had work to do with the customer. We have since worked with NASA on a path forward to support future flights while reducing our program risks, including $359 million of additional NASA funding. Taken together, these are the kinds of actions and outcomes that will support our customers, strengthen Boeing, and position us well for the future. That long term position is also beginning to give us greater visibility into the financial path forward.

As I mentioned, over the last 2 years, we have made exceptional progress restoring trust retiring risk, and transforming our culture and rebuilding the foundation of safety and quality that will help ensure durable success. We have also been deliberate about not getting ahead of ourselves given the range of outcomes tied to the production ramp, certification timing, and other critical program execution. But we appreciate that it is been difficult for shareowners to assess how our progress will translate into financial performance. We are now at a point where the range of outcomes is narrowing.

As we look ahead, our outlook is supported by a record backlog of $745 billion with improved pricing dynamics, a more stable production system, and better underwriting on development programs, and our plans for continued progress on aircraft certification. Taken together, those factors give us confidence to share a multiyear free cash flow target for the business. We have narrowed our range and expect to generate approximately $2 billion of free cash flow in 2026, I am expecting that will improve to about $3 billion in 2027. Inclusive of the additional CapEx to support the FA XX development.

We believe this should improve to about $6 billion in 2028 as we start to burn off some of the headwinds and accelerate from there. And I expect to achieve the $10 billion target in 2029. Jay will provide further color on the elements driving this trajectory in his remarks, but it is important to highlight we continue to see significant growth potential beyond $10 billion in line with our prior comments. To sum it up, we remain well positioned for the long term. I am pleased with the progress we have made so far, We have come a long way but we are entering a new phase, and the bar is higher now. As it should be.

Our job is to keep raising our performance to meet higher expectations while always prioritizing safety, quality, and stability. And so we will get a double benefit from both of those. And that is all supported by the backlog that we have.

As we get to 2029 more specifically, we will be at higher production levels. And so I have said in the past that we expect that the building blocks of $10 billion free cash flow on a normalized basis is around 52 on 737 for deliveries. On a delivery rate around 10 for the 787. On a delivery rate and positive cash generation out of 777 program. In 2029 specifically, we will be operating at higher production levels, which translate into higher delivery levels. And so we will be closer to the to the mid to high fifties on deliveries.

In 2029, we will be closer to around 12 per month on a 787, and we will be generating positive free cash flow or expect to generate positive free cash flow on the 777 x in 2029. The reason we need to be at higher levels is because we will still have some residual drags in 2029 And I expect those to be fully behind us for the most part by 2029, there is runway, as I mentioned in my prepared remarks, in 2030 and beyond above $10 billion. #3730. These Shokz Bone Conduction Headphones Are $70 Off Right Now

Bone conduction headphones are ideal for activities where it is vital to be able to hear your surroundings. That's because they use a technology that sends audio vibrations through to the inner ear while leaving your ear canals open. Shokz is one of, if not the best brand making bone conduction headphones right now. At the high end of Shokz's offerings sit the OpenRun Pro 2s, which impressed me when I tested them out for review, but they are a bit pricey at $180. But right now, the older but still capable OpenRun Pros are discounted to $109.95 (originally $179.99), the second-lowest price they've reached according to price-tracking tools.

If you've never used bone conduction headphones, they're worth a try, and the Shokz OpenPro are a great introduction to the technology. Previously, bone conduction headsets tended to suffer from poor bass compared to in-ear models. The OpenRun Pros are designed to compensate for some of that—you won't get the same thumpy bass you'd get from regular headphones, but it'll be the best you can get from any other bone conduction headphones.

As the name implies, these are meant to be used on outdoor runs. They have an IP55 rating for dust- and water-resistance, are lightweight and comfortable, and can be worn with bicycle helmets and sunglasses. You can use them as everyday headphones too; they can take calls, but you'll need to be mindful of the fact that they don't block any competing sound. Personally, I wear my Shokz all the time around the house and sometimes forget I even have them on (that's how comfortable they are).

In fact, her appearance in the movie might end up costing the movie an Oscar nomination for Best Documentary, but I think it is almost certain to get nominated for an even bigger award: Best Picture. Seyfried is also being talked about as a legit candidate for a Best Supporting Actress nomination, too.

Seyfried has been keen to keep her appearance a secret, just like the rest of us who have seen the movie, and that has led to some awkward interviews lately. However, in an interview last year, she very subtly teased that she had returned to the role of Elizabeth Holmes (with an assist from Emma Stone). It’s a role that won her an Emmy for The Dropout (available with a Hulu subscription). Here’s what happened in that older interview.

About a year ago, in November 2025, Seyfried was promoting her hit The Housemaid in an interview with Ben Mankiewicz on CBS Sunday Morning. Mankiewicz asked her about doing accents, and at first, she admitted that Holmes’ voice is “a little higher now.” That might not send up a red flag right away, until you realize that Holmes had not made any public appearances or given any interviews since her trial for fraud that landed her in federal prison. How would Seyfried know?

A few moments later, Seyfried gave more away. After Mankiewicz complimented her on the voice, the Mean Girls star said to him:

Well, I've had some practice lately, but yeah, it's her own...it's her own way of speaking. She can't change it. She did definitely bring it down a few frequencies for her own reasons, but her voice is actually, like...her voice is her voice.

She’d had some practice lately! Interesting, Mankiewicz didn’t follow that up by asking why she’d been practicing, which isn’t a dig at the reporter. He, like the rest of the world, had no idea that she had been working with Fielder and Oppenheim on You Can See Everything, so it would be easy to overlook that subtle comment. It’s only now, now that the movie is out and it’s a hit, that a comment like that would stand out.

We’ve talked a lot about this movie since its release, but talking about Seyfried’s wonderful performance has been tricky, as no one, myself included, wants to spoil anything for audiences. It’s fun to see Seyfried from a year ago revealing this little nugget of info and providing no further context. It’s an easter egg of sorts, and I’m glad CBS dug it up.

Hugh Scott is the Syndication Editor for CinemaBlend. Before CinemaBlend, he was the managing editor for Suggest.com and Gossipcop.com, covering celebrity news and debunking false gossip. He has been in the publishing industry for almost two decades, covering pop culture – movies and TV shows, especially – with a keen interest and love for Gen X culture, the older influences on it, and what it has since inspired. He graduated from Boston University with a degree in Political Science but cured himself of the desire to be a politician almost immediately after graduation.

#3727. Noel Gallagher Talks Vegans, Macca, Underwear on Chicken Shop Date

“Do you know what’s not a high-flying bird? A chicken.” Noel Gallagher is the latest guest on Amelia Dimoldenberg’s Chicken Shop Date. In the new episode, the elder Gallagher brother opines on vegetarians (“Fuck those guys”), Macca (“He’s one of those rare people that when you meet him, you walk away from it feeling good about yourself”), and the long-standing rumor—started by him, mind you—that he doesn’t wear underwear onstage: “That’s nonsense—I wear two pairs of underpants.”

Throughout the clip, Dimoldenberg peppers Gallagher with her characteristically acerbic questions. Asked “how do you make a comeback feel natural and not desperate,” he replies, “You can’t make sure, you just gotta hope that you can do whatever it is you’re trying to do as well as you once did it. And in the case of Oasis, it became apparent after maybe a week in rehearsals that it was gonna be as good—if not better—than it ever was.”

At another point, Gallagher addresses that inscrutable “Champagne Supernova” lyric: “I mean, I’ve still no idea what ‘slowly walking down the hall/Faster than a cannonball’ means. Do you?” “Well, it means you’re walking quite fast,” Dimoldenberg replies, to which Gallagher counters, “I think it’s a drug reference.” The two also engage in some light football banter (Gallagher famously supports Man City, while Dimoldenberg is a United fan). Watch the full segment below.

After a 12-year run, Chicken Shop Date is winding down with its final season. Role Model and Jorja Smith have also made recent appearances on the show. Meanwhile, the second coming of Oasis will proceed apace well into 2027, with live dates across Europe, the UK, and the U.S. slated through September. Next month the band will be honored at the 2026 Rock & Roll Hall of Fame induction ceremony.

#3726. Chrishell Stause and G Flip Never Made Marriage Legal Before Split

Chrishell Stause and G Flip are going their separate ways after four years together and multiple weddings ... but TMZ has learned there's one thing they won't have to worry about -- getting a divorce.

Sources close to the former couple tell TMZ ... the "Selling Sunset" alum and Australian musician never legally tied the knot, despite celebrating their love with multiple wedding ceremonies over the years.

As TMZ first reported back in May 2023 ... Chrishell and G exchanged vows during a Las Vegas ceremony, even sharing photos from their big day ... and we quickly learned they weren't legally married.

At the time, sources told us the couple planned to make things official somewhere down the line ... but we're now told that never happened.

The pair went on to celebrate their relationship with an additional wedding ceremony and vow renewal ... though none of their public declarations of love made their union legally binding.

As TMZ previously reported ... Chrishell announced their breakup Monday, revealing she and G had agreed to handle their split with love and respect.

And while Chrishell was the one who broke the news ... sources later told TMZ it was actually G who decided to end their four-year romance.

Chrishell admitted she's still in love with G and feels "a little bit like a failure" over the split ... while G said they'll always cherish their memories together.

So, while their love story has come to an end ... at least they won't have to deal with the headache of divorce court.

#3724. Kailyn Lowry Reveals What Her Kids Think of Boyfriend Ike Knighton

As Kailyn Lowry launches her latest project, Crashing Out with Kail, her life is far from a wreck. Both at work and at home with her seven kids, she told E!, boyfriend Ike Knighton "fit right in."

Kailyn Lowry has found a source of calm within the chaos.

Because while the Karma & Chaos host's life with seven kids and three podcasts remains stretched beyond its limits—she dubbed her latest project Crashing Out with Kail, after all—her boyfriend Isaac "Ike" Knighton rather easily found his place amid the pandemonium.

"My kids love him," she raved in an exclusive interview with E! News. "So, he really just fit seamlessly."

A shared love of sports helped Knighton easily bond with Elliott (Lowry's 16-year-old son with ex Jo Rivera), Lincoln (she shares the 12-year-old with former husband Javi Marroquin), Lux, 9, Creed, 6 (her sons with ex Chris Lopez), Rio, 4, and nearly 3-year-old twins Verse and Valley (with Lowry's ex-fiancé Elijah Scott).

"But also," noted Lowry, who first connected with the 34-year-old back in 2009, "one of my sons, Creed, they call each other 'twin' and it's just the cutest thing."

So, yes, acknowledged Lowry, "You would think seven kids is so chaotic that how could anyone take this on? But he fit right in."

Which is why she's trying to make his position permanent.

"I want to be a wife one day," Lowry, also 34, explained. "I let him know pretty early on that I'm not interested in, you know, five-plus years and no movement on our relationship. I think it was like, 'This is my timeline. I would like to invest two years. And if it's not gonna go anywhere beyond this two years and we're still dating, it's fine. Not everyone is on my timeline. But this is what I want."

Having manifested a career that includes multiple businesses, millions of social media followers and her latest creation, docuseries Crashing Out with Kail, the Teen Mom alum has some other dreams to pursue.

"I want to have a husband," she stressed. "I want my kids to have a stepfather. I want to build with someone and actually have this life together."

And with her and Knighton's second anniversary coming next spring...

"I would," she replied when asked if she'd follow through on her two-year deadline. "And he knows that."

It's not that a bare left hand is a firm dealbreaker. "If he came to me and was like, 'Hey, I hear you, but this is not something I'm ready for,' I would hear him out," she explained. "And then I would see how I feel about it and I would leave if ultimately that's the conclusion that we came to."

But if she's being honest, "I just don't know that that's what's going to happen."

Because if he wasn't interested in recreating her dream ring, "I would question why he's doing all of the other husband-type things," Lowry noted. "I would seriously be like, 'Are you OK?'"

In other words, men who aren't interested in making a forever promise don't spend their days attending basketball games and soccer practices or taking on an active role in their girlfriend's burgeoning empire.

"I can see why people would think it's hard," Lowry acknowledged of having Knighton join her professional team. But he brought with him years of experience behind the camera and a level-headedness that has helped her learn to separate business from pleasure.

Having joined Lowry for her 21-date Fatherless Behavior Tour this past summer, "He's usually the one that keeps the balance," she explained of Knighton. "I sometimes get sassy and then he handles it very well. I'm the sassy one and he's the logical thinker that doesn't let things seep into the other."

It's a dynamic viewers can see play out in Lowry's new vertical docuseries.

"I feel like so much of my TV life has been portrayed as me sitting on the couch complaining about my kids' dads for 13, 14 years," she explained of taking on the weekly series, with new clips dropping on ShortVydz.com and the ShortVydz app through January. "This gave me the opportunity to really showcase who is Kail outside of Teen Mom, and when my kids' dads are not involved—this is who I really am."

She's a CEO who's crafted an entire media empire from the reality TV gig she signed on for as a teen. "I have great people in my corner," she noted of what fans will see in the weekly clips. "I work really hard." And now she's ready to build a future with the man she simply knows is her forever.

"I feel like it's cliché to say," Lowry acknowledged, "but when you meet someone, you pretty much know very quickly if this is somebody that you want to spend the rest of your life with or not."

And in Knighton she's found someone who's funny, hard-working and fun to be around. "He's just a good human at his core," Lowry explained. "I've always known that and so when we got together in this way, I was like, 'This is the person.'"

Bottom line, she continued, Knighton "inspires me to be a better person and handle things better, and so I really love that about him."

As Lowry prepared to add another branch to her family tree, check out the brood she's grown thus far.

New episodes of Crashing Out with Kail drop each week on ShortVydz.com and the ShortVydz app through January 2027.

Kailyn Lowry was just 17 when she and high school boyfriend Jo Rivera welcomed their eldest son, Isaac Elliott Rivera, on Jan. 18, 2010.

And though they split a year later, the exes have settled into a comfortable co-parenting arrangement. "We’ve come a long way since we were 18," Kailyn wrote on X after attending Jo and now-wife Vee Rivera's 2018 wedding. "I couldn’t ask for a better stepmom."

As for her oldest son, "We have literally grown up together, but in some ways he’s even more grown than I am," she wrote in an Instagram tribute on his 13th birthday. Describing the devoted Olivia Rodrigo fan, musician and basketball player as "thoughtful, brilliant, funny and at times a smartass," the Delaware State grad praised his caring heart.

"I think what really inspires me about him is the way he challenges social norms," she wrote. "Because he is curious and open-minded, he can form his own thoughts and opinions."

Elliott, who announced his intentions to go by his middle name on his mom's Barely Famous podcast in June 2025, while also coming out as gay, "accepts people for who they are and never how they’re 'supposed' to be," Kailyn wrote. "More people should be like him."

Lincoln Marshall Marroquin joined the squad on Nov. 16, 2013, roughly a year after the then-star of Teen Mom 2 wed his father Javi Marroquin.

"Very much into sports," as Kailyn described the tween in a 2022 episode of her Barely Famous podcast, he spends a significant portion of his time bouncing between basketball and football games. He even had an NBA-themed bash for his 10th birthday, with a nod to his favorite player, Allen Iverson.

No surprise, then, that he provided an assist when it came time to think up a name for his youngest brother. "Verse came from Iverson, which is the name that Lincoln recommended," Kailyn revealed in a February 2024 TikTok. "Obviously, it would be on his list because he's a huge basketball fan."

And an all-star sibling. "To know Lincoln is to love him," she wrote in a 2022 birthday tribute. "Dedicated, smart, handsome and the best brother out there."

Football, wrestling, Rubik's Cubes and Michael Jackson—these are a few of Lux Russell Lowry's favorite things.

Kailyn's first child with ex-boyfriend Chris Lopez, Lux was born on Aug. 5, 2017. Though she and Chris would continue their on-and-off romance for several years, "Right now, we don't communicate at all," she revealed to E! News in 2020. "I'm hoping that it will get better with time but I don't have a big vision. I don't have anything negative to say. I hope for our kids that everything works itself out in whatever direction that may be."

Perhaps everyone could benefit from being as open-minded as her little Luxedo. "I love his willingness to try anything," she raved in an August 2026 tribute for his ninth birthday, "& how much he loves all his siblings."

Ahead of Creed Romello Lowry-Lopez's July 30, 2020 arrival, Kailyn admitted to wrestling with a huge decision. "I hid the pregnancy for so long just because I didn't know what I was going to do—if I was going to keep the baby or not," she explained to E! News shortly after his birth. "That was really heavy for me and I really, really was struggling during that time."

Though her relationship with Creed's dad Chris was notably rocky, one glance at his ultrasound sealed her decision.

Much like his older siblings—"All three of them have asked to hold the baby and they're super loving on him," Kailyn told E!—the podcaster was smitten from day one. '

Dubbing him "the funniest, snuggliest, most handsome, most athletic, best son, little and big bro ever," Kailyn wrote on Instagram for his sixth birthday in July 2026, "I cannot want to take you to a falcons game this year & see all the things you will accomplish."

"I wanted to be able to tell my own story on my own terms," Kailyn explained to People of keeping news of her fifth son Rio private until well after his November 2022 birth.

Though she acknowledged he "had a little bit of a traumatic entrance into this world where he went to the NICU," due to breathing issues and fluid in his lungs, he's been "the best baby ever."

First-time dad Elijah Scott deserved some props as well. "It's been very different from my other partners and my kids' other dads," she said of her then-fiancé. "He pours concrete, he gets down and dirty at work. But he's also just a real family man. He's very, very much connected with his own family, with our family, and so he's been a really good partner to me, and I feel like it's a really good match."

Which is perhaps why her first few months with Rio inspired her to keep the party going. "Honestly, being a mom to four was harder than five, but I think that was because my number four is so spicy,” Kailyn revealed. “Number five is fantastic. He's the world's greatest baby like, if all my babies were like this, I'd have 10 more.”

Kailyn finally entered her girl mom era in late October 2023 with the arrival of Verse and Valley, her second and third children with Elijah.

Valley (named for Kailyn's childhood growing up in Pennsylvania's Lehigh Valley), endured a longer NICU stay than her brother as she worked through some feeding issues.

"I had never seen a baby in my life not wake up to eat or show signs of hunger," Kailyn explained on a January 2024 episode of Barely Famous. "She would just sleep through feedings. She never had any feeding cues and when I tried to feed her, she wouldn't take a bottle."

And though Kailyn continued to worry about the twins' bond after their extended separation ("I'm trying to re-magnetize them to get them back to where they were") she's thrilled that her family is finally whole.

"I feel grateful, thankful, fulfilled, happy, complete," she noted. "We're putting an addition on our house because of this. I feel so blessed. They are good babies."

#3723. Sacha Baron Cohen and Nathan Fielder Reveal Just How Shameless America Can Be

I was sitting at Sunset Tower in West Hollywood, telling a publicist how Sacha Baron Cohen duped Newt Gingrich. In the British comedian’s latest movie, Ali G: Who Iz I?, the former House speaker was tricked into recording a public service announcement warning Americans about an imaginary cartel leader who was trying to naturalize 143,000 of his own children, I recounted to her.

Much to my surprise, when I reached out to Gingrich’s team for comment, they didn’t bother to explain away his appearance. On the contrary, they seemed thrilled by the attention it was getting. “Our hits blew up” after it was reported he would be in the movie, a Gingrich representative told Vanity Fair.

“The shameless can always make a living in America,” my publicist acquaintance responded with an air of amusement.

Her sentiment was so right on that it actually stung a bit. I arrived at a similar eerie conclusion after watching Nathan Fielder’s You Can See Everything, in which potential investors indulge Elizabeth Holmes’s idea to create a new version of her defunct fraudulent company, Theranos. Holmes was willing to put unreliable blood-test results in the hands of patients and, even after that fraud was exposed, Fielder showed us investors still willing to support her efforts to get back into the biotech game.

Taken together, the two films, which fittingly arrived in theaters a week apart, offer a rather bleak diagnosis of the American condition.

From social media to politics, we increasingly incentivize the brand of brazen behavior that these filmmakers exposed. Meanwhile, the institutions that once policed this behavior are crumbling, and the new ones emerging in their place seem like they’re engineered to reward it.

In many ways, Fielder is Baron Cohen’s disciple. Baron Cohen pioneered a genre he calls “undercover character documentaries,” where he inhabits fictional personas and enlists unsuspecting regular people to advance the narrative. It’s a high-wire act—an apt analogy considering the comedian is constantly putting himself in legitimately dangerous situations in order to stumble upon a coherent plotline and find the funny.

The Canadian comic, who worked with Baron Cohen on Who Is America? in 2018 and later contributed to Borat Subsequent Moviefilm, took the concept in a more psychological and ambiguous direction. Fielder has made a career out of constructing elaborate social experiments, like building exact replicas of people’s homes and hiring actors to impersonate their loved ones on The Rehearsal. Or when he came up with a marketing idea to rebrand a struggling coffee shop as a “Dumb Starbucks” on Nathan For You, and it turned into a viral sensation.

While Cohen wants to expose the consequences of the con (how conspiracy theories impact the world), Fielder wants to understand how the con works (how these lies start and spread, and why we’re so susceptible to them). “He’s really reinvented the genre, and he had a rule which was not to do anything that I had done,” Baron Cohen tells me. “He’d say ‘we’re going to do the stuff that Sacha would never do.’”

Now the former collaborators have released films that seem to be in conversation with one another, even if the filmmakers are approaching the same questions from opposite directions. (Baron Cohen told VF he had been talking with Fielder about his movie, though he had not yet seen it. Fielder’s reps could not be reached for comment.)

Baron Cohen’s creative innovation in the 1990s was to make people reveal prejudices they might otherwise be embarrassed of. Appearing as Borat, the fictional Kazakh journalist, he once got a roomful of Americans to sing along to a song called “Throw the Jew Down the Well.” The whole thing was revelatory at the time. The Anti-Defamation League didn’t even know what to make of it, and spoke out against Baron Cohen (who is Jewish) for spreading antisemitism.

“At that point we revealed this underbelly of antisemitism,” Baron Cohen tells me. “Now that would not be that revealing.”

He’s right about that. Social media platforms show their users the ugliest side of humanity. You don’t need a fictional Kazakh journalist to find proud racists or to catch politicians saying ridiculous things.

Baron Cohen also finds himself, once again, fighting allegations that Ali G is racist. “I almost got canceled in 1999,” he recalls, when critics began questioning whether his hip-hop-loving alter ego with a Jamaican accent was essentially a modern-day minstrel act. Cohen insists the joke was always on Ali G, not the Black culture the character was so desperately trying to emulate. The character, he explained, is “a white guy who’s a culture appropriator,” a wannabe Tupac Shakur whose swagger far exceeds his self-awareness.

Cohen knew the character’s return would stir up some outrage. In Britain, comedians including Gina Yashere, Mo Gilligan, Curtis Walker, and Dane Baptiste have been critical of Ali G’s reliance on racial stereotypes. So he decided to beat them to the punch by recruiting progressive influencers and putting their outrage into the movie. But the joke took on a life of its own. NewsCord also launched a coordinated campaign urging people to email movie chains in an effort to prevent the film from hitting screens.

There was a report that Amazon/MGM Studios chose to limit the number of theaters screening the movie because of all the controversy, with most locations showing the film only once a day. The movie will begin streaming on Amazon on Wednesday. Meanwhile, Fielder’s film is on track to be the highest-grossing documentary in 15 years.

“This is my first movie in six years, so it’s financially disastrous. It’s a terrible business model. There’s a huge risk, but I feel I have to do it. I feel I really want my fans to laugh really hard,” Baron Cohen tells me. “And I want them to think, and I want to create a society that is a little more tolerant, as Ali G says, to increase the peace, and one where there’s a bit more respect,” he says, using his Ali G accent to punctuate “respect.”

Much of the film is about an invented conspiracy involving a Mexican cartel leader named Alejandro Gonzalez, who supposedly has fathered over 140,000 children. The premise is preposterous on its face (Baron Cohen tells VF it was “a laughable, absurd idea”). Nevertheless, Baron Cohen persuades real politicians, including Gingrich and Congressman Andy Harris, to condemn the fake cartel leader. Harris could not be reached for comment.

But the movie’s most disturbing sequence takes the deception a lot further.

Disguised as an Israeli intelligence operative, Baron Cohen recruits a group of MAGA supporters, including a local Republican Party leader in El Paso, into what they believe is a mission to prevent the birth of the fictitious cartel leader’s child on American soil. We find out that the participants, unaware that they are inside a meticulously produced fiction, are willing to kill an innocent baby in order to prevent the birth of an “anchor baby.”

Baron Cohen described the sequence as a kind of Milgram experiment, invoking the famous psychological tests conducted after World War II to study the power of authority, and get a better understanding about why so many Germans bought into Naziism.

The film also features former congressman George Santos participating in a game involving dolls representing liberal stereotypes, a mock gas chamber, and a button labeled Zyklon B, the poison used by the Nazis at their death camps. Santos did not respond to the request for comment that VF sent on October 20.

I found the elaborate operation involving the newborn particularly dark. Baron Cohen seemed to demonstrate that the same conditions that made fascism possible in 20th-century Europe are very much in place today. Convince people that an innocent person represents an existential threat, furnish them with a frightening conspiracy theory about an immoral enemy, and some may become willing to kill.

When I excitedly described the sequence to colleagues, however, several seemed unimpressed. Of course conspiracy theorists are capable of violence, they intimated. Hadn’t we already established that?

Their reaction struck me as almost as revealing as the movie itself. Baron Cohen spent years building an elaborate psychological experiment to demonstrate something horrifying about Western society; however, it seems like everyone already knows what he was trying to tell us.

“The reason I made the movie is hopefully people stop for a moment and think about where we are,” Baron Cohen tells me. “These hateful lies and conspiracies that were spread on social media and now are being incorporated into AI could lead to murder on a large scale, because once people believe in conspiracy theories, they can do heinous acts.”

He places considerable responsibility on the technology companies that make so much money spreading lies. He singled out Mark Zuckerberg, whom he called “probably the biggest inciter of Jew hatred in 100 years,” referring to the spread of antisemitism on Meta’s platforms. Meta did not respond to a request for comment after VF inquired last week.

His broader indictment was directed at the incentives governing the technology industry and Section 230, which largely shields social media companies from liability for content posted on their sites. “One must assume that these tech companies are in no way moral companies,” he said. “They are concerned about making money.”

Which brings us to Elizabeth Holmes, who for a short period, schemed her way into becoming a billionaire.

The crazy thing about Holmes is that even after her fraud was exposed, she still had enough confidence in her own rhetorical powers to invite another master manipulator, Fielder, into her life. Does she have no shame about the fraud she perpetrated? Or was she too naive to realize that Fielder was going to expose her as a fraud all over again?

Holmes didn’t need a disguise or a hidden-camera setup to fool her marks. She had a black turtleneck, a deep voice and the vocabulary of Silicon Valley. Government officials, executives, and prominent public figures mistook her confidence and ability to perform credibility for evidence that her technology worked. They wanted to believe that they were given a once-in-a-lifetime investment opportunity that could shower them with wealth, and they didn’t bother to scrutinize her claims.

Both films expose the massive gullibility and brazenness in every rung of American society. They collectively reveal that people will believe almost anything if it confirms their fears or hopes, and how far people will go in service of lies.

Luckily for my sanity, I found some hope in a conversation I had with a communications director for one of the politicians Baron Cohen duped in his new movie.

When I explained that her boss had appeared in the fake PSA about the imaginary cartel leader, it was clear that she was digesting the information in real time.

“Now I’m horrified,” she said, before defending herself: “I’ve only been here a year and a half.”

Erika Kirk Doesn't Really Want to Talk Politics. So How's She Going to Lead Turning Point USA?

A Deep Dive Into How Larry Ellison Built His Influence

Making the Elizabeth Holmes Doc Fried Nathan Fielder’s and Lance Oppenheim’s Brains

“History Will Judge”: Fox News Anchor Bret Baier on Donald Trump and Pete Hegseth’s War With the Press

#3722. The One With Matthew Perry Is A Valuable Lesson For Other Streaming Documentaries

This post contains discussions of addiction.

I was ... apprehensive as I sat down to watch the three-part Netflix documentary "The One About Matthew Perry," which interrogates and fully explains the deeply tragic 2023 death of the "Friends" and "Fools Rush In" star from ketamine use and accidental drowning. A lot of documentaries about celebrities who died in tragic ways feel, for lack of a more elegant term, tawdry. Documentaries like "Whitney" and "I Am Heath Ledger" purport to treat subjects like Whitney Houston and Ledger with respect, but they feel sensationalized, rarely celebrate the joy of the person's life, and end up seeming empty when all is said and done.

That is not true of "The One About Matthew Perry," which I found to be a straightforward yet deeply emotional portrait of Perry — a complicated man who struggled with substance abuse for much of his life but tried, fervently, to maintain his sobriety.

Perry's actual friends and family — people who knew him for many, many decades — are vital parts of this documentary. Perry's sister Mia Perry Bowick, who became a licensed psychotherapist to better understand Perry's lifelong addiction issues, is a major presence in all three episodes, as are his longtime friends Roger Castillo and David Pressman, fellow actors and close friends Kevin Pollak and Nate Torrence, and even people who spent time with Perry in rehabilitation centers. Thanks to Emmy winner Mary Robertson's extremely respectful framing of Perry's rise to fame and subsequent mental health struggles, "The One About Matthew Perry" is blunt about what Perry went through, but is also a genuinely moving story about addiction that could be meaningful to people on similarly difficult sobriety journeys.

Before this documentary, Mia Perry Bowick — Matthew Perry's half-sister, with whom he shares their father, John Bennett Perry — had never spoken publicly about her brother's life, legacy, and difficult life. "It's taken time for me to feel ready to share these memories and what it was like to love Matthew through all the complexity of his life — to see him as a whole person, not just as Chandler, and not just as someone who struggled with addiction," she said to Netflix's publication, Tudum.

It's hard to find anyone in 2026 whose life hasn't been touched by addiction in one way or another. Without getting too deep into anything personal, I know that watching a loved one try to overcome addiction can be difficult and, in many cases, devastating. The subject also has to be approached with the utmost sensitivity; as we see in an interview that Perry endured with Peter Hitchens, the late actor was extraordinarily angry when Hitchens, on a talk show, told Perry that addiction is a "choice" and not a disease. "The One About Matthew Perry" dispels this as it gives room for Bowick to speak not just from her experience as a psychotherapist, but eloquently about Perry in particular. She told Tudum:

"As his sister, he shaped who I am today, the person I strive to be, and the work to which I've devoted my life. Matthew had a beautiful heart and a generous spirit, and even in his darkest moments, he could still make you laugh and feel loved. I hope this series gives everyone who loved him, whether personally or from afar, a deeper appreciation for who he truly was, and the legacy he leaves behind."

Through interviews with Matthew Perry's ex-girlfriends, longtime friends, colleagues, and his sister, "The One About Matthew Perry" doesn't shy away from some of the more difficult moments of his life and sobriety journey, from a car accident he caused while riding with his friend Marjorie Harris Newman to the fact that Perry was in and out of rehab when he filmed the "Friends" episode where his character, Chandler Bing, marries Monica Geller (Courteney Cox). It also lets friends like Roger Castillo and David Pressman tell stories about their early years in Los Angeles goofing around. When all is said and done, it's a sweet, humanizing, and difficult portrait of a man who struggled, but tried his best.

Speaking to Vulture, Mary Roberston talked about toeing this seemingly impossible line, saying that the intention of the film is for it to help people. As she put it:

"It's my intention that the film provide balm and entertainment, which is part of how we help people too. I think Matthew would be really upset if I made a film about his life that wasn't funny. It was very important to me that this film be as funny as it could be, and that we find levity in the darkness and in the sorrowful moments. I don't think that humor diminishes our grief or our understanding of his struggles."

"Friends" fan or not, "The One About Matthew Perry" is one worth watching — and it's on Netflix now.

If you or anyone you know needs help with addiction issues, help is available. Visit the Substance Abuse and Mental Health Services Administration website or contact SAMHSA's National Helpline at 1-800-662-HELP (4357).

#3721. Watsco (WSO) Q3 2026 Earnings Call Transcript

Need a quote from a Motley Fool analyst? Email [email protected]

Watsco, Inc. (WSO -1.41%) reported record third quarter revenue and strong cash flow as residential demand trends normalized and unit volumes returned to growth. Management reported that the company completed two significant acquisitions in 2026, Jackson Supply and The Granite Group, which added approximately $730 million in annualized revenue and expanded the distribution network to 107 new locations. The company stated that investments in its digital ecosystem, including e-commerce and AI platforms, are driving higher growth and customer loyalty among technology-enabled contractors. Leadership indicated that the balance sheet remains debt-free, supporting a 10% increase in the annual dividend and providing liquidity for future strategic investments in the fragmented HVAC/R and plumbing markets.

Operator: Good day, and welcome to the Watsco third quarter of 2026 earnings call. All participants will be in a listen-only mode for the duration of the call. Should you need assistance today, please signal a conference specialist by pressing the star key followed by zero. After today's presentation, there will be an opportunity to ask questions. To ask a question, you may press star then one on your telephone keypad. To withdraw your question, you may press star then two. Also, please be aware that today's call is being recorded. I would now like to turn the call over to Albert Nahmad. Please go ahead.

Albert Nahmad: Morning, everyone. Welcome to our third quarter earnings call. This is Al Nahmad, Chairman and CEO. With me is A.J. Nahmad, President, Paul Johnston, Barry Logan, and Rick Gomez. Before we start, a cautionary statement. This conference call has forward-looking statements as defined by SEC laws and regulations that are made pursuant to the safe harbor provisions of these various laws. Ultimate results may differ materially from the forward-looking statements on the report. I am happy to report that Watsco's third quarter results reflect improving demand and simpler operating conditions in our industry and in our business. The last six years brought a pandemic, supply chain disruption, regulatory transitions, and tariff volatility.

Through it all, we stayed the course and invested in our business. Demand trends are now normalizing, organic growth is improving, and our digital ecosystem is producing measurable results in driving growth. Our largest and most impactful product group, which is residential HVAC equipment, grew 7% during the quarter, with gains on both unit volume and pricing. We expect the impact from the A2L transition will be largely over by the end of this year. Third quarter reflects Jackson Supply, which was acquired on June 1 of this year. Jackson has $230 million in annual sales and operates from 25 Sunbelt locations. Jackson is performing well and grew 16% during the quarter.

We are also pleased to have completed the acquisition of The Granite Group. Granite has annual sales of approximately $500 million and serves over 11,000 customers from 82 locations in the Northeast. Granite marks the fourth business acquired by Watsco that serves both HVAC and plumbing contractors. Today, Watsco has 112 locations that offer both HVAC and plumbing products. Our M&A pipeline has always included companies that have historically distributed both HVAC and plumbing products. We hope to have other similar businesses to join us. As is our culture, Granite will continue to independently operate and grow their business with our full support. They have big ambitions, and we will support their leadership in any way we can.

Watsco is a more efficient company this year. We have made investments in technology, SupplySync, VCR, Hydros, and other initiatives that will benefit growth in the years ahead. Excluding these investments, fixed costs have increased 1% so far in 2026. In terms of our balance sheet, we ended the quarter with $649 million cash and no debt. Even after using a portion of our cash to fund the Granite transaction in October, we remain a positive net cash flow position and are committed to maintaining a pristine balance sheet, enabling investments in growth opportunities as they arise. Turning to the third quarter results, sales increased 7% to a record $2.22 billion and 4% on a same-store basis.

Gross profit was a record $586 million. Gross margins were 26.4%. SG&A increased 5%, excluding Jackson, primarily due to variable expenses. Operating income was $225 million with an operating margin of 10.1%, and earnings per share came in at $3.77. Gross margins over the last 12 months has been in a narrow range and more in line with historical seasonality and current business conditions. Having said that, we remain focused on reaching our long-term goal of 30% and believe we have the right tools in place to get us there. Year-to-date, operating cash flow has improved 79% to $304 million, reflecting our continued vigilance towards managing our assets. Finally, I am going to hand this call over to A.J.

Nahmad, who is our President, to provide an update on Watsco's technology initiatives, and he will be joined by other leaders of Watsco. A.J.?

A.J. Nahmad: Thank you, and good morning to everyone. With the complexity of the last few years largely behind us, we believe our technology investments have made us a stronger company with higher growth prospects and a widening competitive moat. Our goals are ambitious and straightforward. First, leverage our industry-leading repository of data, products, market, customer, competitor, pricing, you name it. This underpins and empowers the industry's most advanced technology platforms. Second, revolutionize our customer experience so that contractors, installers, and technicians love doing business and only want to do business with the Watsco companies. Next, transform our supply chain and distribution operations to better serve customers and gain operating efficiencies along the way.

Finally, develop and launch technologies that help our customers grow their businesses. Importantly, we see contractor behavior evolving in ways that benefit the technology-enabled distributor long term. Our core technology platforms continue to scale and add value. E-commerce sales have grown 12% year-to-date, while outpacing overall growth and broader industry metrics. E-commerce reached 37% of sales over the last 12 months, with certain markets at 60%-70% penetration. Digital engagement with our mobile app is strong at more than 73,000 active monthly users. OnCallAir continues its growth trajectory. Over the last year, approximately 351,000 proposals were presented to homeowners, generating $2 billion of gross merchandise value, a 14% increase over the comparable period.

Simply put, the contractors we serve digitally are growing faster, have greater loyalty, and we believe we can lower our cost to serve at scale over time. Just as exciting, the new initiatives announced late last year are making progress. SupplySync.com, our newest platform to serve the growing segment of large institutional customers, launched earlier this year to great fanfare. We have launched a pilot program and are transacting with customers, with a plan to scale it to more and more customers in the coming months. This is a growing channel with unique customer needs that provides incremental growth opportunity for Watsco while leveraging our existing scale and infrastructure.

VCR, which stands for Vendor Consolidation and Rationalization, continues to expand across many of our non-equipment product categories. Relationships with our strategic vendor partners have strengthened, and in many cases, are at all-time highs. Hydros, which is our investment in shared logistics and distribution, has further matured and will continue to scale in 2027 to more locations and more vendors. The transformational use of AI continues to evolve throughout Watsco. I could spend the next few hours just on that subject. These investments, along with our scale, entrepreneurial culture, and capacity to invest, are unmatched in our industry.

We view these technology investments as critical to growing our leadership position in the HVAC industry and attractive to the entrepreneurs of companies like Jackson and Granite and many others before them to aid our M&A strategy of acquiring great businesses in our space. We invite you to visit us and learn more about what we consider to be our long-term competitive advantage. In closing, a reminder of our fundamentals. Watsco is the market leader and the technology leader in what remains a highly fragmented HVAC distribution market. The products we sell are a necessity, and the installed base continues to expand. We have deep and collaborative relationships with industry-leading manufacturers and industry partners.

We offer the broadest variety of products and operate a large and growing network to serve more and more customers. Our unique ownership culture, shared by more than 8,200 employees, rewards and incentivizes long-term performance. With that, let's turn to Q&A.

Jeffrey Sprague: Okay. Then maybe just one other quick one. Plumbing is becoming a little bit more of a strategic focus imperative. Does that fundamentally have a different margin profile than your kind of core legacy HVAC business?

Rick Gomez: I was going to say not necessarily. I think what's true in the plumbing space is if you sell a lot of equipment there, you're going to have one margin profile. If you sell a lot of non-equipment, you're going to have a different margin profile. The same is true there in HVAC, by the way. We see that within our portfolio of businesses too. I wouldn't call it structurally different or radically different. What is, I think, different is that their customer curve, if you will, is probably at a different state of maturity than the HVAC contractor, which is to say there's slightly less consolidation going on there.

Those customers are slightly less technology-enabled today than as compared to the HVAC contractor. And so we think that there's the same kind of maturity and sophistication curve that we've seen with our HVAC contractor base over the last five or 10 years and how they've adopted technology in their business, we think that it's in the very early innings as it relates to the plumbing contractor.

A.J. Nahmad: Which is all opportunity in the long term.

Barry Logan: I wanted to say this too, Jeff. We get a lot of questions about entering plumbing, and what we've actually entered is acquiring businesses that have done both HVAC and plumbing well for decades. Granite's been around for over 50 years doing both HVAC and plumbing well for that period of time. Gateway a few years ago, same thing. In fact, if you look at the top 50 plumbing distributors in the country, 20 of the 50 also sell HVAC and are in the top 50 list of HVAC companies in the industry. It's not a new thing or a novel thing, it's an ordinary thing. We've known these families for a long time.

It's taken us this long to do four of them. That's the approach. To answer your question, again, we don't see a big profile difference in terms of margins because, again, they do both well and the better companies have the higher margin and have figured out how to coexist in those markets with those products.

A.J. Nahmad: I also just want to say, I got to say publicly, Bill and Chris and Amir and the whole Granite team are just world-class and as good as it gets. We can't be more excited to have them finally part of this family. Like Barry said, we've known these families for a long time. It took us this long, as we talked about it with them, to get to the starting line. Now they're part of the family and it's all upside from here, and let the fun begin.

Albert Nahmad: I would add to that, on the big picture that Watsco is now taking-

Albert Nahmad: -committed. Can you hear me?

Albert Nahmad: I say that the big picture for Watsco that we should explain is that we are now committed to growing in the plumbing market, which means that we have enormous opportunities in that distribution business of plumbing as well as the HVAC market. We have two industries that we can play and play hard and get size. The plumbing one, we are just beginning, and it is pretty healthy stuff. We had two other business units before this one that showed us plumbing can add profitability to the business as they already have over the last several years.

That is a huge ability now for us to take share in the plumbing market, where we just have a few hundred million dollars worth of sales in a much larger market. Big opportunity is what I am trying to say.

Operator: All right. Our next question will come from Pratap Singh with UBS. Please go ahead.

Pratap Singh: Thank you, and good morning, everyone. Yes. We continue to hear about customers trending down more towards lower efficiency levels, but it feels like the scope for the mix down could be limited. Can you give your views on what you are seeing and some sense of portfolio impact for you?

Paul Johnston: Yes, we are seeing. When you say low efficiency today, it means that the efficiency is high efficiency compared to what it was five or 10 years ago. As they trade down to the baseline efficiency that we have in the marketplace, that is occurring, and that is why we get very excited about the opportunity to be able to sell the side discharge. I think the side discharge will be a breakthrough where customers will start going back to high efficiency once they see the opportunity.

Rick Gomez: Right. Just that one perspective. This is as entry-level as the industry has been in quite a long time. Paul, you've got some great data around that, but if it's 80%-85% entry level today, I think the thrust of your question is right, which is there's more likely a mix-up opportunity over time, particularly now that you have a side discharge product that competes very well from a price perspective and gets you all the efficiency on the high end. It does allow a contractor to deliver more value to that homeowner versus just entry level and traditional top discharge high efficiency. There's now something that can help broaden the industry beyond where it is today.

Paul Johnston: But every time there's been an efficiency change, we've seen a compression to the low end, and then it spreads back up. It's not something new that we haven't experienced before.

Pratap Singh: That's very helpful. Thank you. Just another on the margin side, you are integrating a couple of big recent acquisitions. Should we expect that to be a headwind to margin in the near term, but with a potential for improvement in the medium term as you scale margins within those businesses? Can you also help size those impact?

Barry Logan: Again, I'd just be careful with this. When we acquire a company like Granite or Jackson, we're keeping the people, we're keeping the culture, we're keeping the infrastructure, we're keeping what's made them successful for 50 years. The question is, how do we help them build on that success and build on their networks? Whether it's adding capital or products or technology, whatever it might be. The word synergy does not exist in our mindset. How do we take costs out? How do we tweak margins? That's not a concept for Watsco. We built a very successful company supporting great companies that became part of our family, and the intent is identical to that theory for Jackson and Granite.

In terms of your question, I don't think there's any risk or short-term kind of trigger or anything that needs to be worried about. The question is, will they be successful as part of the Watsco family? And the only scorecard I can show you is Jackson Supply is up 16% in its first quarter as part of Watsco. I want to impute that into the future, of course.

Barry Logan: But just know that there's no sudden move or sudden synergy or sudden anything relative to these kind of opportunities. It's really how do we build on the great success of these good companies that have been around forever?

Albert Nahmad: That's very well said, Barry.

Paul Johnston: How do we provide them the technology, the financial resources so they can grow faster? That's the opportunity, and that's the only synergy we have.

Albert Nahmad: Another important aspect is that we create an equity culture. We provide equity throughout the organizations that we acquire, which means that they have a stake in the whole company, win or lose. I think that's very important in terms of our culture, providing equity to people that sell us their business, their leaders, so that they act as owners and be very conscious of that.

Operator: This concludes our question and answer session. I'd like to-

Albert Nahmad: Hey, did you have something?

Operator: -Albert, for the closing remarks.

Albert Nahmad: Okay, thanks very much for your interest in Watsco, and we'll talk again in three months or sooner. Bye-bye.
